Edge Statistics for Lozenge Tilings of Polygons, I: Concentration of Height Function on Strip Domains
Jiaoyang Huang

Abstract
. In this paper we study uniformly random lozenge tilings of strip domains. Under the assumption that the limiting arctic boundary has at most one cusp, we prove a nearly optimal concentration estimate for the tiling height functions and arctic boundaries on such domains: with overwhelming probability the tiling height function is within n δ n^{\delta} of its limit shape, and the tiling arctic boundary is within n 1 / 3 + δ n^{1/3+\delta} to its limit shape, for arbitrarily small δ > 0 \delta>0 . This concentration result will be used in [ AH21 ] to prove that the edge statistics of simply-connected polygonal domains, subject to a technical assumption on their limit shape, converge to the Airy line ensemble.



1.
Introduction

A central feature of random lozenge tilings is that they exhibit boundary-induced phase transitions. Depending on the shape of the domain, they can admit
frozen
regions, where the associated height function is flat almost deterministically, and
liquid
regions, where the height function appears more rough and random. We refer to the papers
[
CLP98
]
and
[
CK01
]
for some of the earlier analyses of this phenomenon in lozenge tilings of hexagonal domains, and to the book
[
Gor21
]
for a comprehensive review.

The interface or edge, is the boundary between frozen and liquid regions, and in the limit it converges to a non-random curve, the
arctic curve
. A thorough study of arctic curves on arbitrary polygons was pursued in
[
KO07
,
ADPZ20
]
, where it was shown that their limiting trajectories are algebraic curves. Before the limit, it was predicted that the fluctuations of tiling boundary curves are of order
n^{1/3}
and
n^{2/3}
in the directions transverse and parallel to their limiting trajectories, respectively. And after taking appropriate scaling limit, they converge to the
Airy
2
process
, a universal scaling limit introduced in
[
PS02
]
. We refer to
[
Joh18
]
for a detailed survey.

Following the initial works
[
Joh00
,
Joh02
,
Joh05
]
, this prediction has been established for various tiling models on specific domains. For example, we refer to
[
OR03
,
OR07
,
FS03
]
for certain
q
-weighted random plane partitions;
[
BKMM07
]
for lozenge tilings of hexagons; and
[
Pet14
,
DM18
]
for lozenge tilings of trapezoids (hexagons with cuts along a single side). These results are all based on exact and analyzable formulas, specific to the domain of study, for the correlation kernel for which the tiling forms a determinantal point process. Although for lozenge tilings of arbitrary polygonal domains such explicit formulas are not known, it is believed the edge universality results, namely the convergence of the tiling boundary curves to the Airy
2
process, still hold; see Conjecture 18.7 of the book
[
Gor21
]
.

For the above edge universality results to hold, it is necessary that the tiling arctic boundary concentrates around its limit shape, with fluctuations bounded by
n^{1/3+\delta}
. One challenge to prove the edge universality results is the lack of such concentration estimates for general domains. In this paper we study uniformly random lozenge tilings of strip domains. This is equivalent to a family of
m
non-intersecting random Bernoulli walks conditioned to start and end at specified locations. Under the assumption that the limiting arctic boundary has at most one cusp (see the left and middle of
Figure

1
for examples) and some other technical assumptions, we prove a concentration estimate for the tiling height functions and arctic boundaries on such domains. An informal formulation of this result is provided as follows; we refer to Theorem
2.6
below for a more precise statement.

Theorem 1.1
.

For uniformly random lozenge tilings of strip domains (equivalently, non-intersecting random Bernoulli bridges), with limiting arctic boundary containing at most one cusp, it holds that for any
\delta>0

the tiling height function is within
n^{\delta}
of its limit shape, and the tiling arctic boundary is within
n^{1/3+\delta}
of its limit shape with overwhelming probability.

The above concentration result is optimal up to the
n^{\delta}
factor, and it will be used in Part II of this series
[
AH21
]
to prove the edge universality for random lozenge tilings: the edge statistics of simply-connected polygonal domains, subject to a technical assumption on their limit shape (that we believe to hold generically), converge to the Airy line ensemble at any point (that is not a cusp or tangency location) around the arctic boundary. Recently, universality results for lozenge tilings at other points inside the domain have been established. For example, it was shown that local statistics in the interior of the liquid region converge to the unique translation-invariant, ergodic Gibbs measure of the appropriate slope for hexagons
[
BKMM07
,
Gor08
,
BGR10
]
, domains covered by finitely many trapezoids
[
Pet14
,
Gor17
]
, bounded perturbations of these
[
Las19
]
, and finally for general domains
[
Agg19
]
. It was also recently shown in
[
AG22
]
that, at tangency locations between the arctic boundary and sides of general domains, the limiting statistics converge to the corners process of the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble.

The concentration results imply that particles of non-intersecting random Bernoulli walks conditioned to start and end at specified locations strongly concentrate around their classical locations. Such concentration phenomenon is ubiquitous in random matrix theory, and is known as
eigenvalue rigidity
. In the context of Wigner matrices, it was first proven in
[
EYY12
]
, and later for more general classes of random matrices
[
EKYY13
,
KY13
,
BEK+14
,
HKR18
,
BES17
,
AEK19
,
BES20
]
.

To prove Theorem
1.1
, for
m
non-intersecting random Bernoulli walks conditioned to start and end at specified locations (equivalently, random lozenge tilings of a strip), we follow
[
Hua20b
]
and approximate the random bridge model by a family of non-intersecting Bernoulli random walks with a space and time dependent drift, and derive a discrete stochastic differential equation for the empirical particle density. A dynamical version of loop equations has been developed in
[
Hua20b
]
, to control the martingale term and errors in the stochastic differential equation on a
macroscopic
scale. And a macroscopic central limit theorem has been proven in
[
Hua20b
]
for random lozenge tilings of polygonal domains with exactly one horizontal upper boundary edge.

In the past, loop equations have served as important tools to study fluctuations for interacting particle systems. They were introduced to the mathematical community in
[
Joh98
]
by Johansson to derive macroscopic central limit theorems for continuous
\beta
-ensembles; see also
[
BG13a
,
BG13b
,
KS10
]
. For discrete
\beta
-ensembles,
[
BGG17
]
proved macroscopic central limit theorems through making using of a family of discrete loop equations originating from
[
Nek16
,
NPS13
,
NP11
]
, which could be analyzed similarly to the continuous ones. Loop equations have also played important roles in understanding rigidity phenomena and local statistics for
\beta
-ensembles
[
BEY14a
,
BEY14b
,
GH19
,
BFG15
,
BMP22
]
.

To prove the concentration results on optimal scale, Theorem
1.1
, we need to analyze the discrete stochastic differential equation for the empirical particle density of non-intersecting Bernoulli random walks on any
mesoscopic
scale. We analyze the dynamical loop equations by a multi-scale analysis, which gives nearly optimal estimates of the martingale term and errors in the discrete stochastic differential equation on any mesoscopic scale. With this as an input, we analyze the discrete stochastic differential equation by studying its behavior along the characteristics of the limiting complex Burgers equation, an idea which has previously been used in
[
HL19
,
AH20
,
Bou21
,
Hua20a
]
to study Dyson Brownian motion. However, the fact that non-intersecting Bernoulli random walks behave differently close to cusps and tangency locations poses a new challenge. We need to construct the spectral domains for the characteristics and estimate the error terms of the stochastic differential equation adapted to these singularities. Through this framework, our results also give optimal estimates of extreme Bernoulli walks close to these singular locations for Bernoulli random walks with at most one cusp.

Figure 1.
Shown to the left and middle are arctic boundaries exhibiting a single cusp. Shown to the right is an arctic boundary exhibiting two cusps that point in opposite directions.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In
Section

2
we define the model, the associated variational problem, and state our main results. In Section
3
we collect some properties of complex slopes associated with tilings of double sided trapezoid domains with general boundary height function.
In Section
4
we introduce a weighted version of non-intersecting Bernoulli bridges, and use it to study tilings on the double-sided trapezoid domains. We prove our main result Theorem
2.6
, using concentration estimates for this weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge model. The concentration estimates for weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge model is proven in Section
5
assuming
Proposition

5.5
below.
Finally,
Proposition

5.5
is proven in Sections
6
and
7
by analyzing the dynamical loop equations.
In Appendix
A
, we check that thin slices of polygonal domains provide examples of strips with limiting continuum height function satisfies our assumptions. In Appendices
B
,
C
and
D
, we give the proofs of some propositions from Section
3
. In Appendix
E
, we sketch the derivation of transition probability of the weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge model we used in Section
4
.

Notations

Throughout, we let
\overline{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
,
\mathbb{H}^{+}=\big\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\Imaginary z>0\big\}
, and
\mathbb{H}^{-}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\Imaginary z<0\}
denote the compactified complex plane, upper complex plane, and lower complex plane, respectively. For any real numbers
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we set
a\vee b=\max\{a,b\}
and
a\wedge b=\min\{a,b\}
. For any integer
n\geqslant 1
, we also denote
\mathbb{Z}_{n}=\{\cdots,-2/n,-1/n,0,1/n,2/n,\cdots\}
to be the set of integers, rescaled by
n
. We denote the set
[\![{1,r}]\!]=\{1,2,\cdots,r\}
. We write
X=\OO(Y)
or
X\lesssim Y
if
|X|\leqslant{\mathfrak{C}}Y
;
X\gtrsim Y
if
X\geqslant Y/{\mathfrak{C}}
; and
X\asymp Y
if
Y/{\mathfrak{C}}\leqslant|X|\leqslant{\mathfrak{C}}Y
for some universal constant
{\mathfrak{C}}>0
. We write
X=\oo(Y)
or
X\ll Y
if the ratio
|X|/Y
tends to
0
, and
X=\oo(Y)
or
X\gg Y
if
Y/X
tends to
0
. For two probability measures
\mu
and
\nu
, we let
d(\mu,\nu)
be any metric compatible with the weak topology.
We further denote by
|u-v|
the Euclidean distance between any elements
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For any subset
\mathfrak{R}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we let
\partial\mathfrak{R}
denote its boundary,
\overline{\mathfrak{R}}
denote its closure. For any real number
c\in\mathbb{R}
, we also define the rescaled set
c\mathfrak{R}=\{cr:r\in\mathfrak{R}\}
, and for any
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we define the shifted set
\mathfrak{R}+u=\big\{r+u:r\in\mathfrak{R}\big\}
. We say an event holds with overwhelming probability, if it holds with probability
1-n^{-D}
for any constant
D>1
(assuming
n
is sufficiently large).

2.
Main Results

2.1.
Tilings and Height Functions

We denote by
\mathbb{T}
the
triangular lattice
, namely, the graph whose vertex set is
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and whose edge set consists of edges connecting
(x,t),(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
if
(x^{\prime}-x,t^{\prime}-t)\in\{(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}
. The axes of
\mathbb{T}
are the lines
\{x=0\}
,
\{t=0\}
, and
\{x=t\}
, and the faces of
\mathbb{T}
are triangles with vertices of the form
\big\{(x,t),(x+1,t),(x+1,t+1)\big\}
or
\big\{(x,t),(x,t+1),(x+1,t+1)\big\}
. A
domain

\mathsf{R}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
is a simply-connected induced subgraph of
\mathbb{T}
. With a slight abuse of notation, we also denote by
\mathsf{R}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
the union of triangular faces with vertices in
\mathsf{R}
. When viewing
\mathsf{R}
as a vertex set, the
boundary

\partial\mathsf{R}\subseteq\mathsf{R}
is the set of vertices
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{R}
adjacent to a vertex in
\mathbb{T}\setminus\mathsf{R}
; when viewing
\mathsf{R}
as a union of triangular faces,
\partial\mathsf{R}
is the union of its boundary edges.

A
dimer covering
of a domain
\mathsf{R}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
is defined to be a perfect matching on the dual graph of
\mathsf{R}
. A pair of adjacent triangular faces in any such matching forms a parallelogram, which we will also refer to as a
lozenge
or
tile
. Lozenges can be oriented in one of three ways; see the right side of
Figure

2
for all three orientations. We refer to the topmost lozenge there (that is, one with vertices of the form
\big\{(x,t),(x,t+1),(x+1,t+2),(x+1,t+1)\big\}
) as a
type
1
lozenge. Similarly, we refer to the middle (with vertices of the form
\big\{(x,t),(x+1,t),(x+2,t+1),(x+1,t+1)\big\}
) and bottom (vertices of the form
\big\{(x,t),(x,t+1),(x+1,t+1),(x+1,t)\big\}
) ones there as
type
2
and
type
3
lozenges, respectively. A dimer covering of
\mathsf{R}
can equivalently be interpreted as a tiling of
\mathsf{R}
by lozenges of types
1
,
2
, and
3
. Therefore, we will also refer to a dimer covering of
\mathsf{R}
as a
(lozenge) tiling
. We call
\mathsf{R}

tileable
if it admits a tiling.

Associated with any tiling of
\mathsf{R}
is a
height function

\mathsf{H}:\mathsf{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, namely, a function on the vertices of
\mathsf{R}
that satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{v})-\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{u})\in\{0,1\},\quad\text{whenever $\mathsf{u}=(x,t)$ and $\mathsf{v}\in\big\{(x+1,t),(x,t-1),(x+1,t+1)\big\}$},

for some
(x,t)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

We refer to the restriction
\mathsf{h}=\mathsf{H}|_{\partial\mathsf{R}}
as a
boundary height function
. For any boundary height function
\mathsf{h}:\partial\mathsf{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{h})
denote the set of all height functions
\mathsf{h}:\mathsf{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
with
\mathsf{H}|_{\partial\mathsf{R}}=\mathsf{h}
. In what follows, any height function on a domain
\mathsf{R}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
will always implicitly be extended by linearity to the faces of
\mathsf{R}
, so that it may be viewed as a piecewise linear function on
\mathsf{R}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

\mathsf{H}
\mathsf{H}
\mathsf{H}
\mathsf{H}
\mathsf{H}
\mathsf{H}+1
\mathsf{H}+1
\mathsf{H}
\mathsf{H}

Figure 2.
Depicted to the right are the three types of lozenges. Depicted in the middle is a lozenge tiling of a hexagon. One may view this tiling as a packing of boxes (of the type depicted on the left) into a large corner, which gives rise to a height function (shown in the middle).

For a fixed vertex
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{R}
and integer
h_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
, one can associate with any tiling of
\mathsf{R}
a height function
\mathsf{H}:\mathsf{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
as follows. First, set
\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{v})=h_{0}
, and then define
\mathsf{H}
at the remaining vertices of
\mathsf{R}
in such a way that the height functions along the four vertices of any lozenge in the tiling are of the form depicted on the right side of
Figure

2
. In particular, we require that
\mathsf{H}(x+1,t)=\mathsf{H}(x,t)
if and only if
(x,t)
and
(x+1,t)
are vertices of the same type
1
lozenge, and that
\mathsf{H}(x,t)-\mathsf{H}(x,t+1)=1
if and only if
(x,t)
and
(x,t+1)
are vertices of the same type
2
lozenge. Since
\mathsf{R}
is simply-connected, a height function on
\mathsf{R}
is uniquely determined by these conditions (and the value of
\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{v})=h_{0}
).

We refer to the right side of
Figure

2
for an example; as depicted there, we can also view a lozenge tiling of
\mathsf{R}
as a packing of
\mathsf{R}
by boxes of the type shown on the left side of
Figure

2
. In this case, the value
\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{u})
of the height function associated with this tiling at some vertex
\mathsf{u}\in\mathsf{R}
denotes the height of the stack of boxes at
\mathsf{u}
. Observe in particular that, if there exists a tiling
\mathscr{M}
of
\mathsf{R}
associated with some height function
\mathsf{H}
, then the boundary height function
\mathsf{h}=\mathsf{H}|_{\partial\mathsf{R}}
is independent of
\mathscr{M}
and is uniquely determined by
\mathsf{R}
(except for a global shift, which was above fixed by the value of
\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{v})=h_{0}
).

2.2.
Limit Shapes and Arctic Boundaries

To analyze the limits of height functions of random tilings, it will be useful to introduce continuum analogs of the notions considered in
Section

2.1
. So, set

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}=\big\{(s,t)\in(0,1)\times(-1,0):s+t>0\big\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},

and its closure
\overline{\mathcal{T}}=\big\{(s,t)\in[0,1]\times[-1,0]:s+t\geqslant 0\big\}
. We interpret
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
as the set of possible gradients, also called
slopes
, for a continuum height function;
\mathcal{T}
is then the set of “non-frozen” or “liquid” slopes, whose associated tilings contain tiles of all types. For any simply-connected subset
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we say that a function
H:\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
admissible
if
H
is
1
-Lipschitz and
\nabla H(u)\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}
for almost all
u\in\mathfrak{R}
. We further say a function
h:\partial\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

admits an admissible extension to
\mathfrak{R}
if
\mathrm{Adm}(\mathfrak{R};h)
, the set of admissible functions
H:\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
H|_{\partial\mathfrak{R}}=h
, is not empty.

We say that a sequence of domains
\mathsf{R}_{1},\mathsf{R}_{2},\ldots\subset\mathbb{T}

converges
to a simply-connected subset
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
if
n^{-1}\mathsf{R}_{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}
for each
n\geqslant 1
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\dist(n^{-1}\partial\mathsf{R}_{n},\partial\mathfrak{R})=0
. We further say that a sequence
\mathsf{h}_{1},\mathsf{h}_{2},\ldots
of boundary height functions on
\mathsf{R}_{1},\mathsf{R}_{2},\ldots
, respectively,
converges
to a boundary height function
h:\partial\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
if
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{-1}\mathsf{h}_{n}(nv_{n})=h(v)
if
v_{n}
is any point in
n^{-1}\partial\mathsf{R}_{n}
with limit
v\in\mathfrak{\partial}R
.

To state results on the limiting height function of random tilings, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
and
(s,t)\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}
we denote the
Lobachevsky function

L:\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and the
surface tension

\sigma:\overline{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

(2.2)

\displaystyle L(x)=-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\log|2\sin z|\mathrm{d}z;\qquad\sigma(s,t)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\Big(L(\pi(1-s))+L(-\pi t)+L\big(\pi(s+t)\big)\Big).

For any
H\in\mathrm{Adm}(\mathfrak{R})
, we further denote the
entropy functional

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(H)=\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\sigma\big(\nabla H(z)\big)\mathrm{d}z.

The following variational principle of
[
CKP01
]
states that the height function associated with a uniformly random tiling of a sequence of domains corresponding to
\mathfrak{R}
converges to the maximizer of
\mathcal{E}
with high probability.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
CKP01
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{R}_{1},\mathsf{R}_{2},\ldots\subset\mathbb{T}
denote a sequence of tileable domains, with associated boundary height functions
\mathsf{h}_{1},\mathsf{h}_{2},\ldots
, respectively. Assume that they converge to a simply-connected subset
\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
with piecewise smooth boundary, and a boundary height function
h:\partial\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, respectively. Denoting the height function associated with a uniformly random tiling of
\mathsf{R}_{n}
with boundary height function
\mathsf{h}_{n}
by
\mathsf{H}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigg(\displaystyle\max_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{R}_{n}}\big|n^{-1}\mathsf{H}_{n}(\mathsf{v})-H^{*}(n^{-1}\mathsf{v})\big|>\varepsilon\bigg)=0,

where
H^{*}
is the unique maximzer of
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathfrak{R}
with boundary data
h
,

(2.4)

\displaystyle H^{*}=\displaystyle\argmax_{H\in\mathrm{Adm}(\mathfrak{R};h)}\mathcal{E}(H).

The fact that there is a unique maximizer described as in (
2.4
) follows from Proposition 4.5 of
[
DSS10
]
.

2.3.
Complex Burgers Equation

Figure 3.
Shown above the complex slope
f=f(u)
.

Under a suitable change of coordinates, this maximizer
H^{*}
in (
2.4
) solves a complex variant of the Burgers equation, which makes it amenable to further analysis. To explain this point further, we require some additional notation. For any simply-connected open subset
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with Lipschitz boundary, and boundary height function
h:\partial\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
admitting an admissible extension to
\mathfrak{R}
, define the
liquid region

\mathfrak{L}=\mathfrak{L}(\mathfrak{R};h)\subset\mathfrak{R}

(2.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathfrak{L}=\big\{u=(x,t)\in\mathfrak{R}:\big(\partial_{x}H^{*}(u),\partial_{t}H^{*}(u)\big)\in\mathcal{T}\big\},\end{split}

and the
arctic boundary

\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}(\mathfrak{R};h)\subset\overline{\mathfrak{R}}
as the set of points
u=(x,t)\in\partial{\mathfrak{L}}
, such that for any sequence of points
u_{n}\in{\mathfrak{L}}
converging to
u
,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big(\partial_{x}H^{*}(u_{n}),\partial_{t}H^{*}(u_{n})\big)\rightarrow\partial\mathcal{T},\end{split}

where
H^{*}
is as in (
2.4
). The complement of the liquid region
{\mathfrak{R}}\setminus{\mathfrak{L}}
is called the
frozen region
.
Then we define the
complex slope

f^{*}:\mathfrak{L}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{-}
by, for any
u\in\mathfrak{L}
, setting
f^{*}(u)\in\mathbb{H}^{-}
to be the unique complex number satisfying

(2.7)

\displaystyle\arg^{*}f^{*}(u)=-\pi\partial_{x}H^{*}(u);\qquad\arg^{*}\big(f^{*}(u)+1\big)=\pi\partial_{t}H^{*}(u),

where for any
z\in\overline{\mathbb{H}^{-}}\setminus\{0\}
we have set
\arg^{*}z=\theta\in[-\pi,0]
to be the unique number in
[-\pi,0]
satisfying
e^{-\mathrm{i}\theta}z\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
; see
Figure

3
for a depiction, where there we interpret
1-\partial_{x}H^{*}(u)
and
-\partial_{t}H^{*}(u)
as the approximate proportions of tiles of types
1
and
2
around
nu\in\mathsf{R}_{n}
, respectively (which follows from the definition of the height function from
Section

2.1
).

The following result from
[
KO07
]
indicates that the complex slope
f^{*}
satisfies the complex Burgers equation on the liquid region.

Proposition 2.2

(
[
KO07
, Theorem 1]
)
.

For any
(x,t)\in\mathfrak{L}
, let
f^{*}_{t}(x)=f^{*}(x,t)
we have

(2.8)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f^{*}_{t}(x)+\partial_{x}f^{*}_{t}(x)\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{t}(x)}{f^{*}_{t}(x)+1}=0.

The following result from
[
ADPZ20
]
gives regularity of the arctic curve.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
ADPZ20
, Theorem 1.2, Theorem 1.5, Theorem 1.6]
)
.

The arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}
is piecewise analytic. Take any connected component
\Gamma
of
{\mathfrak{A}}
. There are at most finitely many singularities
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset{\mathfrak{A}}
on
\Gamma
, and they are all
either first order (inward) cusps or tacnodes. The complex slope
f^{*}
extends continuously to
\Gamma
. For any
(x,t)\in\Gamma
,
f_{t}^{*}(x)\in{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}
and the slope of the arctic boundary at
(x,t)
is given by

(2.9)

\displaystyle\frac{f_{t}^{*}(x)+1}{f_{t}^{*}(x)}

For a nonsingular point
(x,t)\in\mathfrak{A}
, we call it a
tangency location
of
\mathfrak{A}
, if the tangent line to
\mathfrak{A}
has slope in
\{0,1,\infty\}
.

2.4.
Lozenge Tiling of Strip Domains

In this section, we give precise definitions of strip domains (also called “double-sided trapezoid domains”).
These domains are different from the ones considered in earlier works, such as
[
Pet14
,
Pet15
,
DM18
]
, since they will accommodate non-frozen boundary conditions along both their north and south edges (instead of only their south ones).

We fix a real number
\mathfrak{t}>0
, and linear functions
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}:[0,\mathfrak{t}]\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
with
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}(s),\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}(s)\in\{0,1\}
, such that
\mathfrak{a}(s)\leqslant\mathfrak{b}(s)
for each
s\in[0,\mathfrak{t}]
. Define the double-sided trapezoid domain

(2.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{t})=\big\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,{\mathfrak{t}}]:\mathfrak{a}(t)\leqslant x\leqslant\mathfrak{b}(t)\big\},

and denote its four boundaries by

(2.11)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{\so}(\mathfrak{D})=\big\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}:t=0\big\};\qquad\quad\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})=\big\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}:t=\mathfrak{t}\big\};\\
&\partial_{\we}(\mathfrak{D})=\big\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}:x=\mathfrak{a}(t)\big\};\qquad\partial_{\ea}(\mathfrak{D})=\big\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}:x=\mathfrak{b}(t)\big\}.\end{aligned}

We refer to
Figure

4
for a depiction.

y=0
y=\mathfrak{t}
\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})
\partial_{\ea}(\mathfrak{D})
\partial_{\we}(\mathfrak{D})
\partial_{\so}(\mathfrak{D})

Figure 4.
Shown above are the four possibilities for
\mathfrak{D}
.

Let
h:\partial\mathfrak{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote a function admitting an admissible extension to
\mathfrak{D}
. We fix a real number
{\mathfrak{m}}>0
, and assume throughout that

(2.12)

\displaystyle h\equiv 0\text{ on }\partial_{\we}(\mathfrak{D}),\quad h\equiv{\mathfrak{m}}\text{ on }\partial_{\ea}(\mathfrak{D}).

Let
H^{*}\in\mathrm{Adm}(\mathfrak{D};h)
denote the maximizer of
\mathcal{E}
as in (
2.4
), and let the liquid region
\mathfrak{L}=\mathfrak{L}(\mathfrak{D};h)
and arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}(\mathfrak{D};h)
be as below (
2.5
). Recall that a point on
\mathfrak{A}
is a
tangency location
if the tangent line to
\mathfrak{A}
through it has slope in
\{0,1,\infty\}
.

We may then define the complex slope
f^{*}:\mathfrak{L}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{-}
as in (
2.7
), which upon denoting
f^{*}_{t}(x)=f^{*}(x,t)
satisfies the complex Burgers equation (
2.8
). Further let
\mathfrak{L}_{\north}=\mathfrak{L}_{\north}(\mathfrak{D};h)
denote the interior of
\overline{\mathfrak{L}}\cap\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})
, and let
\mathfrak{L}_{\so}=\mathfrak{L}_{\so}(\mathfrak{D};h)
denote the interior of
\overline{\mathfrak{L}}\cap\partial_{\so}(\mathfrak{D})
; these are the extensions of the liquid region to the north and south boundaries of
\mathfrak{L}
. For all
t\in(0,\mathfrak{t})
, we define slices of the liquid region (along the horizontal line
y=t
) by

(2.13)

\displaystyle I_{t}^{*}=\big\{x:(x,t)\in\overline{\mathfrak{L}}\big\};\qquad I^{*}_{0}=\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{\so};\qquad I^{*}_{\mathfrak{t}}=\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{\north}.

We need to formulate certain conditions on the limit shape
H^{*}
. We will study the following two cases ( We refer to
Figure

5
for a depiction).
In the first case, we call
\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})

packed
(with respect to
h
) if
\partial_{x}h(v)=1
for each
v\in\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})
. This case has been extensively studied in
[
Pet14
,
Pet15
]
, due to its exact solvability. In this case, the arctic curve is tangent to
\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})
, and
\overline{\mathfrak{L}}\cap\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})
constitutes a single point, and so
\mathfrak{L}_{\north}
is empty. We refer to
Figure

5
for a depiction.

In the second case,
\mathfrak{L}_{\north}=I^{*}_{\mathfrak{t}}
is a single interval. Take
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}>{\mathfrak{t}}
. We denote the extended double-sided trapezoid domain
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
on the time strip
[0,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}]
:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}=\Big\{(x,t):t\in[0,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}],x\in[\mathfrak{a}(t),\mathfrak{b}(t)]\Big\},\quad{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}(t)\in\{0,1\},\quad{\mathfrak{b}}^{\prime}(t)\in\{0,1\}.

We say that
H^{*}
can be
extended to time
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
if there exists an extended height function
\tilde{H}^{*}:\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, which agrees with
H^{*}
on
\mathfrak{D}
and it is the minimizer of the variational principle

(2.14)

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{*}=\arg\sup_{H\in\mathrm{Adm}({{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}};h)}\int_{0}^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sigma(\nabla H){\rm d}x{\rm d}t,

where
\tilde{h}=\tilde{H}^{*}
on
\partial{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
with
\tilde{h}\equiv 0
on
\partial_{\rm we}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
, and
\tilde{h}\equiv{\mathfrak{m}}
on
\partial_{\rm ea}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
.
The corresponding liquid region
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
is simply-connected, open subset
\tilde{\mathfrak{L}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
containing
\mathfrak{L}
, such that the set
\big\{x:(x,\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})\in\tilde{\mathfrak{L}}\big\}
is non-empty and connected. The complex slope
\tilde{f}^{*}:\tilde{\mathfrak{L}}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{-}
extends
f^{*}_{t}(x)
, and satisfies the complex Burgers equation (
2.8
). We denote the corresponding arctic boundary by
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}
as in (
2.6
). For all
t\in[0,\mathfrak{t^{\prime}}]
, we define slices of the extended liquid region (along the horizontal line at time
t
) still as
I_{t}^{*}
as in (
2.13
).

Assumption 2.4
.

Assume the following constraints hold.

(1)

The boundary height function
h
is constant along both
\partial_{\ea}(\mathfrak{D})
and
\partial_{\we}(\mathfrak{D})
as in (
2.12
).

(2)

Either
\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})
is packed with respect to
h
or
\mathfrak{L}_{\north}=I^{*}_{\mathfrak{t}}
is a single interval and there exists
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}>\mathfrak{t}
such that
H^{*}
admits an extension to time
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
.

(3)

\mathfrak{L}_{\north}=I^{*}_{\mathfrak{t}}
is a single interval and there exists
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}>\mathfrak{t}
such that
H^{*}
admits an extension to time
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, we further assume the following holds:

(a)

The arctic curve
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}
contains at most one cusp location. Either
I^{*}_{t}=\big[E_{1}(t),E_{2}(t)\big],

for
0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}=\{(E_{1}(t),t),(E_{2}(t),t):0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\}
and there is no cusp. Or there is one cusp point at
(E_{c},t_{c})
, then for
t_{c}\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
, the slice
I^{*}_{t}
is a single interval

I^{*}_{t}=\big[E_{1}(t),E_{2}(t)\big],

for
0\leqslant t\leqslant t_{c}
, the slice
I^{*}_{t}
consists of two intervals

I^{*}_{t}=\big[E_{1}(t),E^{\prime}_{1}(t)\big]\cup\big[E^{\prime}_{2}(t),E_{2}(t)\big]
, and
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}=\{(E_{1}(t),t),(E_{2}(t),t):0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\}\cup\{(E^{\prime}_{1}(t),t),(E^{\prime}_{2}(t),t):0\leqslant t\leqslant t_{c}\}
(See Figure
6
). We assume that the cusp location is not a tangency location.

(b)

Any tangency location along
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}
is of the form
\min I_{t}^{*}
or
\max I_{t}^{*}
, for some
t\in(0,{\mathfrak{t}})
. At most one tangency location is of the form
\min I_{t}^{*}
, and at most one is of the form
\max I_{t}^{*}
. Moreover, these tangent locations are contained in either
\partial_{\we}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
or
\partial_{\ea}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
.

(c)

On the frozen region
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\setminus\tilde{\mathfrak{L}}
,
\nabla\tilde{H}^{*}(x,t)
is piecewise constant, taking values in
\{(0,0),(1,0),(1,-1)\}
.

(d)

The extended complex slope
\tilde{f}^{*}
extends to the closure of
\tilde{{\mathfrak{L}}}
. Fix any
(x_{0},t_{0})
in the closure of
\tilde{\mathfrak{L}}
. There exists a neighborhood
{\mathfrak{U}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
of
(x_{0},t_{0})
, and a real analytic function
Q_{0}

1
1

1

Here, a function
g
defined on a domain of
{\mathbb{C}}
is called real analytic if it is analytic and
\overline{g(z)}=g(\overline{z})
.
in one variable, such that for any
(x,t)
in the intersection of
{\mathfrak{U}}
and the closure of
\tilde{\mathfrak{L}}
,

(2.15)

\displaystyle Q_{0}\big(\tilde{f}^{*}_{t}(x)\big)=x\big(\tilde{f}^{*}_{t}(x)+1\big)-t\tilde{f}^{*}_{t}(x).

Moreover,
(x,t)\in\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}
if and only if
w=\tilde{f}_{t}^{*}(x)
is a double root of (
2.15
):
Q_{0}(w)=x(w+1)-tw
.

(e)

For any
0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}
the map

(2.16)

\displaystyle(x,r)\in\tilde{\mathfrak{L}}\cap\left({\mathbb{R}}\times[t,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}]\right)\mapsto x+(t-r)\frac{\tilde{f}^{*}_{r}(x)}{\tilde{f}^{*}_{r}(x)+1}\in\mathbb{H}^{+}

is a bijection to its image.

Let us briefly comment on these constraints. The first guarantees that the associated tiling is one of a double-sided trapezoid, as depicted in
Figure

4
. The second states the limit shape is one of the two cases in
Figure

5
. Item (a) in the third statement guarantees that the arctic boundary for the tiling has only one cusp, and the cusp location is not a tangency location. Item (b) in the third statement implies that there are at most two tangency locations along the arctic boundary (and are along the leftmost and rightmost components of the arctic curve, if they exist); Item (c), (d) and (e) in the third statement are technical. Item (c) holds if there exists a height function
H^{\circ}
on
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
extending the boundary data
\tilde{h}
, and all the slopes
\nabla H^{\circ}(x,t)\in\mathcal{T}
; see
[
Gor21
, Proposition 7.10]
. In particular Item (c) and (d) hold for any polygonal domain and slices of (an explicit perturbation of) one given by a polygonal domain.
For (
2.16
), it is crucial that the limit shape does not have cusps pointing at opposite directions. In our case, the limit shape has at most one cusp, it could in principle be weakened; we impose it since doing so will substantially simplify our proofs later.

For our main application in Part II of this series
[
AH21
]
, we will take the limit shape from the tiling of a thin slice of (an explicit perturbation of) one given by a polygonal domain
{\mathfrak{P}}
, and Assumption
2.4
holds. We postpone a detailed discussion to Appendix
A
.

2.5.
Main Results

Now let
n\geqslant 1
be an integer; denote
\mathsf{t}=\mathfrak{t}n
, and
m={\mathfrak{m}}n
(recall from (
2.12
)). Suppose
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>}0
,
\mathsf{D}=n\mathfrak{D}\subset\mathbb{T}
, so that
\mathsf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Let
\mathsf{h}:\partial\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
denote a boundary height function. We further fix a real number
\delta>0
and a (large) positive integer
n
.
We define the augmented variant of
\mathfrak{L}
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{+}(\mathfrak{D})=\mathfrak{L}_{+}^{\delta}(\mathfrak{D})=\left(\mathfrak{L}\cup\bigcup_{u\in\mathfrak{A}}\big\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|v-u|\leqslant n^{\delta-2/3}\big\}\right)\cap\mathfrak{D}

Below, we view the quantities
0<\mathfrak{t}<\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, functions
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}
, and domain
\mathfrak{D}
as independent of
n
. The next assumption indicates how the tiling boundary data
\mathsf{h}
approximates
h
(recall from (
2.12
)) along
\partial\mathfrak{D}
.

Assumption 2.5
.

Adopt
Assumption

2.4
, and assume the following on how
\mathsf{h}
converges to
h
.

(1)

For each
v\in\partial\mathfrak{D}
, we have
\big|\mathsf{h}(nv)-nh(v)\big|<n^{\delta/50}
.

(2)

For each
v\in\partial_{\ea}(\mathfrak{D})\cup\partial_{\we}(\mathfrak{D})
, and each
v\in\partial_{\north}\mathfrak{D}\cup\partial_{\so}\mathfrak{D}
such that
v\notin\mathfrak{L}_{+}^{\delta/50}(\mathfrak{D})
, we have
\mathsf{h}(nv)=nh(v)
.

(3)

If
\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})
is packed, then for each
v\in\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})
we have
\mathsf{h}(nv)=nh(v)
.

The first assumption states that
\mathsf{h}
approximates its limit shape; the second and third state that it coincides with its limit shape in the frozen region. Recalling that
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{h})
denotes the set of height functions on
\mathsf{D}
with boundary data
\mathsf{h}
, we can now state our main result on the concentration estimate for a uniformly element of
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{h})
.

y=0
y={\mathfrak{t}}
y=0
y={\mathfrak{t}}
y=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
\mathfrak{L}
\mathfrak{L}

Figure 5.
Shown to the left is an example of limit shape admitting an extension to time
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}>{\mathfrak{t}}
; shown to the right is a liquid region that is packed with respect to
h
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Adopt
Assumption

2.4
and
Assumption

2.5
. Let
\mathsf{H}:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
denote a uniformly random element of
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{h})
. Then, the following two statements hold with overwhelming probability, i.e.,
1-n^{-D}
for any constant
D>1
(assuming
n
is sufficiently large):

(1)

We have
\big|\mathsf{H}(nu)-nH^{*}(u)\big|<n^{\delta}
, for any
u\in\mathfrak{D}
.

(2)

For any
u\in\mathfrak{D}\setminus\mathfrak{L}_{+}^{\delta}(\mathfrak{D})
, we have
\mathsf{H}(nu)=nH^{*}(u)
.

In the following, we give an overview for the proof of
Theorem

2.6
. The complex slopes which characterize the limit shapes of lozenge tilings, and their analytic extensions play fundamental role in our proof. Section
3
is technical, where we collect properties of complex slopes and their analytic extensions.

The proof of
Theorem

2.6
is given in Section
4
. Lozenge tilings of strip domains are equivalent to non-intersecting random Bernoulli walks conditioned to start and end at specified locations. We follow
[
Hua20b
]
and approximate the random bridge model by a family of non-intersecting Bernoulli random walks with a space and time dependent drift in Sections
4.1
and
4.2
. The drifts of these random walks are explicitly given by analytic extensions of complex slopes. In particular, we need to understand complex slopes with general boundary data; their properties are collected in Section
3.1
. A key intermediate result is the optimal rigidity
Proposition

4.4
for these non-intersecting Bernoulli random walks with drifts. Using it as input, together with a monotonicity coupling of lozenge tilings to non-intersecting Bernoulli random walks, we conclude the optimal rigidity for lozenge tilings of strip domains in Section
4.3
.

The proof of
Proposition

4.4
for the optimal rigidity of non-intersecting Bernoulli random walks is given in Sections
5
, with intermediate results proven in Sections
6
and
7
.
We analyze the discrete stochastic differential equation for the empirical particle density of non-intersecting Bernoulli random walks. The results from Sections
6
and
7
are collected in Proposition
5.5
, which gives nearly optimal controls of the martingale term and errors in the discrete stochastic differential equation on any mesoscopic scale. With them as an input, we analyze the discrete stochastic differential equation by studying its behavior along the characteristics of the limiting complex Burgers equation. This leads to the optimal rigidity
Proposition

4.4
.

These intermediate results proven in Sections
6
and
7
are for integrable transition probabilities, which satisfy the
dynamical loop equations
. In Sections
6.1
and
6.2
, we introduce a general family of transition probabilities, which includes those of non-intersection Bernoulli random walks as a special case and enjoy the dynamical loop equations. In Sections
6.3
and
6.4
, utilizing the loop equations and a multi-scale analysis, we obtain estimates of these transition probabilities, which is optimal in the liquid region. Through bootstrapping we obtain optimal estimates in the frozen region as well in Section
7
.

3.
Complex Burgers Equation

We recall the double-sided trapezoid domain
{\mathfrak{D}}
on the strip
[0,\mathfrak{t}]
from (
2.10
). Under
Assumption

2.4
, either the north boundary
\partial_{\rm no}({\mathfrak{D}})
of
{\mathfrak{D}}
is packed, or
\mathfrak{L}_{\north}=I^{*}_{\mathfrak{t}}
is a single interval and
H^{*}
admits an extension to time
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
with
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}>{\mathfrak{t}}
. The first of these cases corresponds to lozenge tilings of trapezoid domains with tightly packed north boundary, whose fluctuations have been studied extensively by this point
[
Pet14
,
Pet15
]
, due to its exact solvability. We will therefore focus on the second case, and remark the minor necessary changes for the first case; see Remark
5.9
below.
In this section, we collect some properties of complex slopes associated with tilings of double sided trapezoid domains with general boundary height function.

In the rest of this paper, we assume that
H^{*}
admits an extension to time
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
. By slight abusing of notations, we denote the extended height function, its liquid region, arctic boundary and complex slope as
H^{*},{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}),{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
and
f^{*}
. Thanks to Item (a) and (c) in the third statement of
Assumption

2.4
,

(3.1)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})=\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}:0<\partial_{x}H^{*}(x,t)<1\},\quad{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})=\overline{\{(x,t)\in\partial{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}):0<t<{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\}}.

We recall the relation between the slope of the arctic boundary and the complex slope from (
2.9
). Thanks to Item (b) in the third statement of
Assumption

2.4
, there is no horizontal tangency location (slope equals
0
) along the arctic boundary
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
. Thus, the slope of the tangent line at every point along this arctic boundary is bounded away from
0
. Thus it follows that
f_{t}^{*}(x)
is bounded away from
-1
, and

(3.2)

\displaystyle\big|f_{t}^{*}(x)+1\big|\gtrsim 1,\quad\left|\frac{f_{t}^{*}(x)}{f_{t}^{*}(x)+1}\right|\lesssim 1.

which will be used repeatedly in this section.

For any
0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}
, we denote the part of the trapezoid domain restricted to the time strip
[t,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}]
as

(3.3)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}={\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\cap\big({\mathbb{R}}\times[t,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}]\big)=\Big\{(x,r):r\in[t,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}],x\in[\mathfrak{a}(r),\mathfrak{b}(r)]\Big\}.

There is a map from the liquid region
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\cap{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
to the pair
(f_{t},z)
,

(3.4)

\displaystyle(x,r)\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\cap{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}\mapsto(f_{t},z)=\left(f_{r}^{*}(x),x+(t-r)\frac{f^{*}_{r}(x)}{f^{*}_{r}(x)+1}\right)\in\mathbb{H}^{-}\times\mathbb{H}^{+}.

By
Theorem

2.3
and
Assumption

2.4
, the above map extends continuously to the boundary of the liquid region.
We remark that in (
3.4
), we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f^{*}_{r}(x)]<0;\qquad\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{r}(x)}{(f^{*}_{r}(x)+1)}\bigg]=\displaystyle\frac{\Imaginary\big[f^{*}_{r}(x)\big]}{|f^{*}_{r}(x)+1|^{2}}<0.

By Assumption
2.16
, if we further project the map (
3.4
) to the second coordinate,

(3.5)

\displaystyle(x,r)\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\cap{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}\mapsto z=x+(t-r)\frac{f^{*}_{r}(x)}{f^{*}_{r}(x)+1}\in\mathbb{H}^{+},

we get an injection. Thus we can view
f_{t}
on the righthand side of (
3.4
) as a function of
z
, i.e.
f_{t}=f_{t}(z)
. By taking
r=t
in (
3.4
), we get
f_{t}(x)=f_{t}^{*}(x)
for
(x,t)\in\overline{\mathfrak{L}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})}
.
f_{t}(z)
can be viewed as an extension of
f_{t}^{*}(x)
to the complex plane. Moreover, The map (
3.4
) and its complex conjugate
(\overline{f},\overline{z})\in\mathbb{H}^{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{-}

glue along the arctic curve to a map from the double of
the liquid region
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\cap{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}\mapsto(f_{t},z)
to a domain
\mathscr{U}_{t}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
.

The next proposition collects some properties of
f_{t}(z)
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Under
Assumption

2.4
, the function
f_{t}(z)
as defined in (
3.4
) is a holomorphic function of
z
and satisfies the complex Burgers equation

(3.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f_{t}(z)+\partial_{z}f_{t}(z)\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}=0.

Proof.

Denote
z=z(x,r,t)=x+(t-r)f_{r}^{*}(x)/(f_{r}^{*}(x)+1)
. First we show that
\partial_{x}z=0
implies that
\partial_{r}z=0
and
(x,r)
is a critical point of the map
(x,r)\mapsto z
. The complex Burgers equation (
2.8
) gives

(3.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\partial_{r}z=\partial_{r}\left(x+(t-r)\frac{f_{r}^{*}(x)}{f_{r}^{*}(x)+1}\right)=-\frac{f_{r}^{*}(x)}{f_{r}^{*}(x)+1}+(t-r)\frac{\partial_{r}f_{r}^{*}(x)}{(f_{r}^{*}(x)+1)^{2}}\\
&=-\frac{f_{r}^{*}(x)}{f_{r}^{*}(x)+1}-(t-r)\frac{f_{r}^{*}(x)}{f_{r}^{*}(x)+1}\frac{\partial_{x}f_{r}^{*}(x)}{(f_{r}^{*}(x)+1)^{2}}=-\frac{f_{r}^{*}(x)}{f_{r}^{*}(x)+1}\partial_{x}\left(x+(t-r)\frac{f_{r}^{*}(x)}{f_{r}^{*}(x)+1}\right)=0.\end{split}

Since
f_{r}^{*}(x)
is analytic in
x,r
, so is
z(x,r,t)
. The collection of critical points are discrete. Next we can view
f_{r}^{*}(x)=f_{t}(z(x,r,t))
as a function of
x,r,t
. By taking derivatives on both sides we get

(3.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{x}f_{r}^{*}(x)=\partial_{z}f_{t}\partial_{x}z+\partial_{\overline{z}}f_{t}\partial_{x}\overline{z},\quad\partial_{r}f_{r}^{*}(x)=\partial_{z}f_{t}\partial_{r}z+\partial_{\overline{z}}f_{t}\partial_{r}\overline{z},\quad\partial_{t}f_{t}+\partial_{z}f_{t}\partial_{t}z+\partial_{\overline{z}}f_{t}\partial_{t}\overline{z}=0.\end{split}

The expression (
3.7
) implies that
\partial_{r}z=-(\partial_{x}z)f_{r}^{*}(x)/(f_{r}^{*}(x)+1)
. Using this as input, we can solve for
\partial_{\overline{z}}f_{t}
using the first two relations in (
3.8
),

\displaystyle 0=\partial_{\overline{z}}f_{t}\partial_{x}\overline{z}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left[\frac{f^{*}_{r}(x)}{f_{r}^{*}(x)+1}\right].

When
\partial_{x}\overline{z}\neq 0
(
(x,r)
is not a critical point), and inside liquid region
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{r}^{*}(x)]<0
, we conclude that
\partial_{\overline{z}}f_{t}=0
, and
f_{t}(z)
is holomorphic at
z
. The set of critical points is discrete, and from our construction
f_{t}(z)
is continuous as a function
z
. By Riemann’s theorem on removable singularities, we conclude that
f_{t}(z)
is a holomorphic function of
z
. Using the last relation in (
3.8
), we get the complex Burgers equation (
3.6
).
∎

The complex Burgers equation (
3.6
) now can be solved using the characteristic flow,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f^{*}_{t}(z_{t}(u))=0,\qquad\text{where $z_{t}(u)$ satisfies}\qquad\partial_{t}z_{t}(u)=\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{t}(z_{t}(u))}{f^{*}_{t}(z_{t}(u))+1},\quad z_{0}(u)=u,

In particular,
z_{t}
is linear in
t
, given explicitly by

(3.10)

\displaystyle z_{t}(u)=u+t\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{0}(u)}{f^{*}_{0}(u)+1}.

If the context is clear, we will simply write
z_{t}(u)
as
z_{t}
, omitting its dependence on the initial value
u
.

From the third statement in
Assumption

2.4
, the arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
has at most one cusp point. In the rest of this section, we study the most general case provided as follows.

Assumption 3.2
.

Under
Assumption

2.4
and that
H^{*}
admits an extension to time
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
with
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}>{\mathfrak{t}}
, we further restrict ourselves to the case:
The arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
has a cusp point at
(E_{c},t_{c})
, and it has two tangent locations
at
(E_{1}(t_{1}),t_{1})
and
(E_{2}(t_{2}),t_{2})
with slopes
1
and
\infty
respectively

Remark 3.3
.

Under
Assumption

2.4
and
Assumption

3.2
, these two tangent locations
(E_{1}(t_{1}),t_{1})
and
(E_{2}(t_{2}),t_{2})
are on the west
\partial_{\rm we}{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
and east
\partial_{\rm ea}{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
boundary of
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
respectively. It follows that
{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}(t)=1
and
{\mathfrak{b}}^{\prime}(t)=0
(otherwise the west and east boundary will not be outside the liquid region). We conclude that the arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
is tangent to the west
\partial_{\rm we}{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
and east
\partial_{\rm ea}{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
boundary of
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}

at
(E_{1}(t_{1}),t_{1})
and
(E_{2}(t_{2}),t_{2})
respectively.

Next we show that on each connected component of frozen region,
\nabla H^{*}
is constant and takes values in
\{(0,0),(1,0),(1,-1)\}
. We will only prove this for the region
\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}:0\leqslant t\leqslant t_{1},{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\leqslant x\leqslant E_{1}(t)\}
. By the third statement in
Assumption

2.4
, the complex slope
f_{t}^{*}(x)
extends continuously to the arctic curve
\{(E_{1}(t),t):0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\}
. Thanks to the relation (
2.9
) between
f_{t}^{*}(x)
and the slope, we have
f_{t}^{*}(E_{1}(t))>0
for
0\leqslant t<t_{1}
. Then by the defining relation (
2.7
) of the complex slope, we have
\nabla H^{*}(E_{1}(t),t)=(0,0)
for
0\leqslant t<t_{1}
. It follows that
H^{*}
is constant on the arctic curve
\{(E_{1}(t),t):0\leqslant t<t_{1}\}
. Moreover on the west boundary of
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
,
H^{*}({\mathfrak{a}}(t),t)\equiv 0
. As a consequence,
H^{*}\equiv 0
on the boundary of the region
\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}:0\leqslant t\leqslant t_{1},{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\leqslant x\leqslant E_{1}(t)\}
. We conclude that
H^{*}\equiv 0
on
\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}:0\leqslant t\leqslant t_{1},{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\leqslant x\leqslant E_{1}(t)\}
and
\nabla H^{*}=(0,0)
.

We recall the time slices of the liquid region from (
2.13
) and (
3.1
)

(3.11)

\displaystyle I^{*}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\big\{x:(x,t)\in\overline{{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})}\big\}=\overline{\{{\mathfrak{a}}(t)<x<{\mathfrak{b}}(t):0<\partial_{x}H(x,t)<1\}},\quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}.

The complement
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]\setminus I^{*}_{t}
consists of several intervals. On each interval we either have
\partial_{x}H_{t}^{*}(x,t)\equiv 0

or
\partial_{x}H_{t}^{*}(x,t)\equiv 1
.
In the former case, we call the interval a
void region
, and in the latter case we call it a
saturated region
.

y=0
y={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
y=0
y={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}

, the arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})

).

3.1.
Complex Burgers Equation With General Boundary

We recall
{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
from (
3.3
). We call
\beta:\{x:(x,t)\in\partial_{\rm so}{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}\}\mapsto[0,{\mathfrak{m}}]
a
boundary profile
on the south boundary
\partial_{\rm so}{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
of
{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
, if it satisfies
\beta({\mathfrak{a}}(t))=0
,
\beta({\mathfrak{b}}(t))={\mathfrak{m}}
(recall from (
2.12
)), and it is non-decreasing and is Lipschitz with Lipschitz constant
1
.

In this section, we consider the following variational problem
on
{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
. Define
\mathrm{Adm}({{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}};\beta)=\mathrm{Adm}(\mathfrak{D}^{t};\beta,h)
to be the set of functions
H\in\mathrm{Adm}(\mathfrak{D}^{t})
such that the restriction of
H
to the south boundary
\partial_{\rm so}{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
is given by
H(x,t)=\beta(x)
, and the restriction of
H
on the remaining boundary of
{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
(i.e.,
\partial{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}\setminus\partial_{\rm so}{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
) is given by
h
as defined below (
2.14
). Then, set

(3.12)

\displaystyle W_{t}(\beta)=\sup_{H\in\mathrm{Adm}({{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}};\beta)}\int_{t}^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sigma(\nabla H){\rm d}x{\rm d}r.

We denote the maximizer of (
3.12
) by
H_{r}(x;\beta,t)
and the corresponding complex slope by
f_{r}(x;\beta,t)
, defined by

(3.13)

\displaystyle(\partial_{x}H_{r}(x;\beta,t),\partial_{r}H_{t}(x;\beta,t))=\frac{1}{\pi}\left(-\arg^{*}(f_{r}(x;\beta,t)),\arg^{*}(f_{r}(x;\beta,t)+1)\right),

then
f_{r}(x;\beta,t)
satisfies the complex Burgers equation

(3.14)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{r}f_{r}(x;\beta,t)}{f_{r}(x;\beta,t)}+\frac{\partial_{x}f_{r}(x;\beta,t)}{f_{r}(x;\beta,t)+1}=0,

for
(x,r)
in the liquid region

(3.15)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{L}}({{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}};\beta)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\big\{(x,r)\in{\mathcal{D}}^{t}:\big(\partial_{x}H^{*}(x;\beta,t),\partial_{r}H_{r}^{*}(x;\beta,t)\big)\in\mathcal{T},t<r<{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\big\}.

Similarly to (
3.4
), we consider the following map from the liquid region
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t};\beta)
to
\mathbb{H}^{-}\times\mathbb{H}^{+}
,

(3.16)

\displaystyle(x,r)\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t};\beta)\mapsto(f,z)=\left(f_{r}(x;\beta,t),x-(r-t)\frac{f_{r}(x;\beta,t)}{f_{r}(x;\beta,t)+1}\right)\in\mathbb{H}^{-}\times\mathbb{H}^{+},

We further project the map (
3.16
) to the
z
coordinate:

(3.17)

\displaystyle(x,r)\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t};\beta)\mapsto z=x-(r-t)\frac{f_{r}(x;\beta,t)}{f_{r}(x;\beta,t)+1}\in\mathbb{H}^{+}.

Thanks to Item (c) in the third statement in
Assumption

2.4
, for
\beta(x)=H_{t}^{*}(x)
, the boundary of the liquid region
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t};\beta)
consists of three parts: the north boundary, the south boundary, and the arctic curve boundary

(3.18)

\displaystyle\big\{(x,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})\in\overline{{\mathfrak{L}}({{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}};\beta)}\big\}\cup\big\{(x,t)\in\overline{{\mathfrak{L}}({{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}};\beta)}\big\}\cup\overline{\big\{(x,r)\in\partial{\mathfrak{L}}({{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}};\beta):r\in(t,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})\big\}}.

We will prove in
Proposition

3.4
below that, for
\beta(x)
sufficiently close to the height profile
H_{t}^{*}(x)
,
the decomposition (
3.18
) is also true. In particular the arctic boundary
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t};\beta)=\overline{\big\{(x,r)\in\partial{\mathfrak{L}}({{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}};\beta):r\in(t,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})\big\}}
. Moreover,
f_{r}(x;\beta,t)
extends continuously to the boundary of
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t};\beta)
and the projection map (
3.17
) is a bijection. See Figure
7
for an illustration. We establish Proposition
3.4
for
\beta(x)=H_{t}^{*}(x)
in Appendix
B
and the general case in Appendix
C
below.

Proposition 3.4
.

Fix any
0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}
, and a boundary profile
\beta
on the south boundary
\partial_{\rm so}{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
of
{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
. Let
m(z;\beta,t)
be the Stieltjes transform of the boundary profile
\beta
,

(3.19)

\displaystyle m(z;\beta,t)=\int_{{\mathfrak{a}}(t)}^{{\mathfrak{b}}(t)}\frac{\partial_{x}\beta(x)}{z-x}{\rm d}x.

Assume that the boundary profile
\beta
is sufficiently close to
H^{*}_{t}
, that is,
d(\partial_{x}\beta(x),\partial_{x}H_{t}^{*}(x))\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}
, for some small constant
\mathfrak{c}>0
. Then the following statements hold.

(1)

The projection map (
3.17
) extends to the boundary (
3.18
) of the liquid region
\mathfrak{L}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t};\beta)
, and it maps
the north boundary to a curve in the upper half-plane, and the remaining boundary is mapped bijectively to an interval in
{\mathbb{R}}
. The map (
3.17
) and its complex conjugate together give a bijection from two copies of the liquid region
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t},\beta)
, glued along the arctic curve, with a domain
\mathscr{U}_{t}^{\beta}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
.

(2)

We can interpret
f
in (
3.16
) as a function of
z\in\mathscr{U}_{t}^{\beta}
,
f=f(z;\beta,t)
. For
x\in\mathscr{U}_{t}^{\beta}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
with
x<{\mathfrak{a}}(t)
,
f(x;\beta,t)\in(-\infty,-1)
and for
x\in\mathscr{U}_{t}^{\beta}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
with
x>{\mathfrak{b}}(t)
, we have
f(x;\beta,t)\in(-1,0)
. The following quantity is uniformly bounded away from
0
,

(3.20)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Imaginary z}\cdot\Imaginary\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{f(z;\beta,t)}{f(z;\beta,t)+1}\bigg]\Bigg|\gtrsim 1,\quad\text{for $z\in\mathscr{U}_{t}^{\beta}$}.

(3)

On
\mathscr{U}_{t}^{\beta}
,

f(z;\beta,t)
has a decomposition

(3.21)

\displaystyle f(z;\beta,t)=e^{m(z;\beta,t)}g(z;\beta,t)=e^{\tilde{m}(z;\beta,t)}\tilde{g}(z;\beta,t),

where

(3.22)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{m}(z;\beta,t)&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=m(z;\beta,t)-\log\big(z-{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\big)+\log\big({\mathfrak{b}}(t)-z\big),\\
\log\tilde{g}(z;\beta,t)&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\log g(z;\beta,t)+\log\big(z-{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\big)-\log\big({\mathfrak{b}}(t)-z\big).\end{split}

The function
\tilde{m}(z;\beta,t)
is the Stieltjes transform of the measure
\partial_{x}\beta+\bm{1}_{(-\infty,{\mathfrak{a}}(t)]}+\bm{1}_{[{\mathfrak{b}}(t),\infty)}
. The function
\tilde{g}(z;\beta,t)
is real analytic on
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}^{\beta}:=\mathscr{U}_{t}^{\beta}\cup\{x:(x,t)\in\overline{{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t};\beta)}\}
, i.e.,

\tilde{g}(\bar{z};\beta,t)=\overline{\tilde{g}(z;\beta,t)}
. Moreover, it is positive on
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}^{\beta}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
, and it does not have zeros in
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}^{\beta}
.
The function
g(z;\beta,t)
is meromorphic on
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}^{\beta}
. It is positive on
({\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t))
, has a simple pole at
{\mathfrak{a}}(t)
and a simple zero at
{\mathfrak{b}}(t)
, with no other poles or zeros.

(4)

For any
z\in\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}^{\beta}
we have the following perturbation formula

(3.23)

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{z}^{k}\log g(z;\beta,t)-\partial_{z}^{k}\log g(z;H_{t}^{*},t)\big|\lesssim\oint_{\omega}\big|m(w;\beta,t)-m(w;H^{*}_{t},t)\big||{\rm d}w|,\quad 0\leqslant k\leqslant 2,

where
\omega
is a contour enclosing
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
, and the implicit constant is uniform in
\beta
satisfying
d(\partial_{x}\beta,\partial_{x}H_{t}^{*}(x))<\mathfrak{c}
.

We recall the continuum limit height function
H_{t}^{*}(x)
associated with the trapezoid domain
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
from (
2.14
).
If we take
\beta(x)=H_{t}^{*}(x)
in (
3.12
), the solution of the variational principle (
3.12
) coincides with that of (
2.14
), and we define

(3.24)

\displaystyle\rho_{t}^{*}(x):=\partial_{x}H_{t}^{*}(x),\quad f(z;H_{t}^{*},t)=f_{t}^{*}(z).

We recall the liquid region

{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})=\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}:t\in[0,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}],0<\partial_{x}H_{t}^{*}(x)<1\}
. Via the map (
3.17
), we identify the gluing of two copies of
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\cap{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
along the arctic curve with a domain
\mathscr{U}_{t}:=\mathscr{U}_{t}^{H_{t}^{*}}
. The complex slope
f_{t}^{*}(x)
extends to
f_{t}^{*}(z)
on
\mathscr{U}_{t}
.
By
Proposition

3.4
, and we can decompose
f_{t}^{*}(z)
as

(3.25)

\displaystyle\log f_{t}^{*}(z)=m_{t}^{*}(z)+\log g_{t}^{*}(z),\quad g_{t}^{*}(z)=g(z;H_{t}^{*},t),\quad m_{t}^{*}(z)=\int\frac{\rho_{t}^{*}(x)}{z-x}{\rm d}x.

With this notation, the complex Burgers equation from (
3.6
) can be written as

(3.26)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}m^{*}_{t}(z)+\partial_{t}\log g^{*}_{t}(z)+\partial_{z}\log(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1)=0.

Similarly to (
3.22
), we can also write (
3.25
) as

(3.27)

\displaystyle\log f_{t}^{*}(z)=\tilde{m}_{t}^{*}(z)+\log\tilde{g}_{t}^{*}(z),

where
\tilde{m}_{t}^{*}=m_{t}^{*}-\log(z-{\mathfrak{a}}(t))+\log({\mathfrak{b}}(t)-z)
, and
\log\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}=\log g^{*}_{t}+\log\big(z-{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\big)-\log\big({\mathfrak{b}}(t)-z\big)
is analytic in
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}:=\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}^{H_{t}^{*}}
and does not have zeros. Thus,
|\tilde{g}_{t}^{*}(z)|
is uniformly bounded away from
0
and
\infty
on
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}
, namely,
|\tilde{g}_{t}^{*}(z)|\asymp 1
.

y={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
y=t
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t};\beta)
y=t
\mathscr{U}_{t}^{\beta}
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]>0,\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f(z;\beta,t)]<0
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]<0,\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f(z;\beta,t)]>0
