.

In the following proposition, we collect some quantitative estimates for the extended complex slope
f_{t}^{*}(z)
around the arctic boundary, under
Assumption

3.2
. We postpone its proof to Appendix
D
below.

Proposition 3.5
.

Under
Assumption

3.2
, we have

(3.28)

\displaystyle\big|f_{t}^{*}(z)+1\big|\gtrsim 1,\quad\left|\frac{f^{*}_{t}(z)}{f_{t}^{*}(z)+1}\right|\lesssim 1.

Moreover, the following holds with a sufficiently small constant
{\mathfrak{c}}>0
.

(1)

If
\big(E(t),t\big)\in{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
is bounded away from cusp points, in a small neighborhood of
E(t)
, then the quantity
f_{t}^{*}(z)/(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1)
exhibits the square root behavior

(3.29)

\displaystyle\frac{f_{t}^{*}(z)}{f_{t}^{*}(z)+1}=\frac{f_{t}^{*}(E(t))}{f_{t}^{*}(E(t))+1}+\sqrt{{\mathfrak{C}}\big(z-E(t)\big)}+\OO\big(|z-E(t)|^{3/2}\big),\quad\big|z-E(t)\big|\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}},

where
|{\mathfrak{C}}|\asymp 1
may depend on
t
, and for
\Imaginary z>0
, the square root has negative imaginary part. If we further assume that
\big(E(t),t\big)\in{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
is bounded away from tangency locations, then

\displaystyle-\sin(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{t}^{*}(z)])\asymp-\sin(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\log f_{t}^{*}(z)])\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}},&\kappa\geqslant 0,\\
\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta},&\kappa\leqslant 0,\end{array}\right.

where
\eta=\Imaginary z\geqslant 0
, and
\kappa=\dist(\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z,I_{t}^{*})
if
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z\not\in I_{t}^{*}
; otherwise,
\kappa=-\dist(\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z,{\mathbb{R}}\setminus I_{t}^{*})
.

(2)

Around the tangency location
\big(E_{1}(t_{1}),t_{1}\big)
, we have
\big|{\mathfrak{a}}(t)-E_{1}(t)\big|\asymp|t_{1}-t|^{2}
. If
(z,t)=(E_{1}(t)-\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta,t)
is in a neighborhood of the tangency location
\big(E_{1}(t_{1}),t_{1}\big)
, i.e.,
\big|z-E_{1}(t)\big|,|t-t_{1}|\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}
, then for
t\leqslant t_{1}
the interval
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),E(t)]
is a void region,

\displaystyle-\arg^{*}f_{t}^{*}(z)\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}(\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}+|t-t_{1}|)}+\arg(z-{\mathfrak{a}}(t)),&\kappa\geqslant 0,\\
\frac{\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}}{\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}+|t-t_{1}|},&\kappa\leqslant 0,\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{t}^{*}(z)]\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}(\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}+|t-t_{1}|)},&\kappa\geqslant 0,\\
\frac{\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}}{\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}+|t-t_{1}|},&\kappa\leqslant 0.\end{array}\right.

For
t\geqslant t_{1}
, the interval
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),E(t)]
is a saturated region, and

\displaystyle\pi+\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\tilde{m}_{t}^{*}(z)]\asymp\pi+\arg^{*}f_{t}^{*}(z)\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}(\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}+|t-t_{1}|)},&\kappa\geqslant 0,\\
\frac{\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}}{\eta/\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}+|t-t_{1}|},&\kappa\leqslant 0.\end{array}\right.

The analogous statement holds for
(z,t)=\big(E_{2}(t)+\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta,t\big)
in a neighborhood of the tangency location
(E_{2}(t_{2}),t_{2})
.

(3)

Around the cusp singularity
(E_{c},t_{c})
, let
c(t)=E_{c}+(t-t_{c})f_{t_{c}}^{*}(E_{c})/\big(f_{t_{c}}^{*}(E_{c})+1\big)
. If
0\leqslant t_{c}-t\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}
, then we have
E_{2}^{\prime}(t)-E_{1}^{\prime}(t)\asymp(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}
. For
\big|z-E_{1}^{\prime}(t)\big|\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}
, we have

(3.38)

\displaystyle\frac{f_{t}^{*}(z)}{f_{t}^{*}(z)+1}=\frac{f_{t}^{*}(E_{1}^{\prime}(t))}{f_{t}^{*}(E_{1}^{\prime}(t))+1}+\frac{\sqrt{{\mathfrak{C}}(z-E^{\prime}_{1}(t))}}{(t_{c}-t)^{1/4}}+\OO\left((t_{c}-t)^{1/4}|z-E_{1}^{\prime}(t)|^{1/2}+\frac{|z-E_{1}^{\prime}(t)|}{t_{c}-t}\right),

where
|{\mathfrak{C}}|\asymp 1
, and for
\Imaginary z>0
, the square root has negative imaginary part. It follows that

\displaystyle-\sin(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{t}^{*}(z)])\asymp-\sin(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\log f_{t}^{*}(z)])\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}(t_{c}-t)^{1/4}},&\kappa\geqslant 0,\\
\frac{\sqrt{|\kappa|+\eta}}{(t_{c}-t)^{1/4}},&\kappa\leqslant 0.\end{array}\right.

The analogous statement holds for
|z-E_{2}^{\prime}(t)|\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}
.

If
z
is farther from the interval
[E_{1}^{\prime}(t),E_{2}^{\prime}(t)]
, namely,
{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}\leqslant\dist\big(z,[E_{1}^{\prime}(t),E_{2}^{\prime}(t)]\big)\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}
, then
f_{t}^{*}(z)/\big(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1\big)
exhibits the cube root behavior

(3.41)

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{t}(z)}{f^{*}_{t}(z)+1}\bigg]\asymp\big|z-c(t)\big|^{1/3}.

Remark 3.6
.

Suppose
u=(x,t)\in\mathfrak{L}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
is bounded away from a cusp or tangency location of
\mathfrak{A}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
; let
d_{u}=\inf\big\{|x-x_{0}|:(x_{0},t)\in\mathfrak{A}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\big\}
. Then, the fact that
f^{*}_{t}(x_{0})\in\mathbb{R}
if
(x_{0},t)\in\mathfrak{A}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
from
Theorem

2.3
, the first statement of
Proposition

3.5
, and (
2.7
) together imply that there exists a small constant
{\mathfrak{c}}>0
such that
{\mathfrak{c}}d_{u}^{1/2}<\big|\partial_{x}H^{*}(x,t)-\partial_{x}H^{*}(x_{0},t)\big|<{\mathfrak{c}}^{-1}d_{u}^{1/2}
.

4.
Weighted Non-intersecting Bernoulli Bridges

In this section we first explain the correspondence between lozenge tilings of double-sided trapezoid domains and non-intersecting Bernoulli walk ensembles in Section
4.1
. In that way, we can interpret random lozenge tilings of double-sided trapezoid domains as uniformly random non-intersecting Bernoulli walks (or bridges) with specified starting and ending data.
Then we introduce a weighted version of non-intersecting Bernoulli bridges, and use it to study tilings (equivalently, height functions) on the double-sided trapezoid domain
{\mathfrak{D}}
on the strip
[0,{\mathfrak{t}}]
, under Assumptions
3.2
and
2.5
, in Section
4.2
. Then
Theorem

2.6
will follow from concentration estimates for this weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge model.

4.1.
Non-intersecting Bernoulli walk ensembles

A
Bernoulli walk
is a walk
\mathsf{q}=\big(\mathsf{q}(0),\mathsf{q}(1),\ldots,\mathsf{q}({\mathsf{t}})\big)\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathsf{t}}+1}
such that
\mathsf{q}(r+1)-\mathsf{q}(r)\in\{0,1\}
for each
r\in[\![{0,{\mathsf{t}}-1}]\!]
; viewing
r
as a time index,
\big(\mathsf{q}(r)\big)
denotes the space-time trajectory for a Bernoulli walk, which may either not move or jump to the right at each step. For this reason, the interval
[\![{0,{\mathsf{t}}}]\!]
is called the
time span
of the Bernoulli walk
\mathsf{q}
, and a step
(r,r+1)
of this Bernoulli walk may be interpreted as an “non-jump” or a “right-jump” if
\mathsf{q}(r+1)=\mathsf{q}(r)
or
\mathsf{q}(r+1)=\mathsf{q}(r)+1
, respectively. A family of Bernoulli walks
\mathsf{Q}=\big(\mathsf{q}_{1},\mathsf{q}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{q}_{m}\big)
is called
non-intersecting
if
\mathsf{q}_{i}(r)<\mathsf{q}_{j}(r)
whenever
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m
and
0\leqslant r\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}
.

We recall the double-sided trapezoid domain
{\mathfrak{D}}
from (
2.10
), and the boundary height function
h
satisfying (
2.12
). Now let
n\geqslant 1
be an integer; denote
\mathsf{t}=\mathfrak{t}n
, and
m={\mathfrak{m}}n
(recall from (
2.12
)). Suppose
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>}0
,
\mathsf{D}=n\mathfrak{D}\subset\mathbb{T}
, so that
\mathsf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Let
\mathsf{h}:\partial\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
denote a boundary height function satisfying Assumption
2.5
. It is necessary that
{\mathsf{h}}=0
on
\partial_{\rm we}{\mathsf{D}}
and
{\mathsf{h}}=m
on
\partial_{\rm es}{\mathsf{D}}
.

Now take any height function
\mathsf{H}:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
corresponding to a tiling
\mathscr{M}
of
\mathsf{D}
with boundary height function
{\mathsf{h}}
. We may interpret
\mathscr{M}
as a family of
m
particle non-intersecting Bernoulli walks by first omitting all type
1
lozenges from
\mathscr{M}
, and then viewing any type
2
or type
3
tile as a right-jump or non-jump of a Bernoulli walk, respectively; see
Figure

8
for a depiction.

It will be useful to set more precise notation on this correspondence. Recalling that
\mathsf{H}
is extended by linearity to the faces of
\mathsf{D}
,
\partial_{x}\mathsf{H}(x,t)\in\{0,1\}
for all
(x,t)
. In particular, there exist integers
\mathsf{x}_{1}(t)<\mathsf{x}_{2}(t)<\cdots<\mathsf{x}_{m}(t)
such that

(4.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\mathsf{H}(x,t)=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bm{1}\Big(x\in\big[\mathsf{x}_{i}(t),\mathsf{x}_{i}(t)+1\big]\Big),

which are those such that
\mathsf{H}\big({\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)+1,t\big)=\mathsf{H}\big(\mathsf{x}_{i}(t),t\big)+1
. This defines a Bernoulli walk
\mathsf{x}_{i}=\big(\mathsf{x}_{i}(t)\big)
, and a non-intersecting ensemble of Bernoulli walks
\mathsf{X}=\big(\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{m}\big)
.

\mathsf{q}_{1}(0)
\mathsf{q}_{2}(0)
\mathsf{q}_{3}(0)
\mathsf{q}_{4}(0)
\mathsf{q}_{5}(0)
\mathsf{q}_{6}(0)
\mathsf{q}_{1}(2)
\mathsf{q}_{2}(2)
\mathsf{q}_{3}(2)
\mathsf{q}_{4}(2)
\mathsf{q}_{5}(2)
\mathsf{q}_{6}(2)
\mathsf{q}_{1}
\mathsf{q}_{2}
\mathsf{q}_{3}
\mathsf{q}_{4}
\mathsf{q}_{5}
\mathsf{q}_{6}

Figure 8.
Depicted to the left is an ensemble
\mathsf{Q}=\big(\mathsf{q}_{1},\mathsf{q}_{2},\mathsf{q}_{3},\mathsf{q}_{4},\mathsf{q}_{5},\mathsf{q}_{6}\big)
consisting of six non-intersecting Bernoulli walks. Depicted to the right is an associated lozenge tiling.

To proceed, we require some additional notation on non-intersecting Bernoulli walk ensembles. Let
\mathsf{X}=(\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
denote a family of non-intersecting Bernoulli walks, each with time span
[\![{0,{\mathsf{t}}}]\!]
, so that
\mathsf{x}_{j}=\big(\mathsf{x}_{j}(0),\mathsf{x}_{j}(1),\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})\big)
for each
j\in[\![{1,m}]\!]
. Given functions
\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}:[\![{0,{\mathsf{t}}}]\!]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we say that
\mathsf{X}
has
(\mathsf{f};\mathsf{g})
as a
boundary condition
if
\mathsf{f}(r)\leqslant\mathsf{x}_{j}(r)\leqslant\mathsf{g}(r)
for each
r\in[\![{0,{\mathsf{t}}}]\!]
. We refer to
\mathsf{f}
and
\mathsf{g}
as a
left boundary
and
right boundary
for
\mathsf{X}
, respectively, and allow
\mathsf{f}
and
\mathsf{g}
to be
-\infty
or
\infty
. We further say that
\mathsf{X}
has
entrance data

\mathsf{d}=(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{m})
and
exit data

\mathsf{e}=(\mathsf{e}_{1},\mathsf{e}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{e}_{m})
if
\mathsf{x}_{j}(0)=\mathsf{d}_{j}
and
\mathsf{x}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})=\mathsf{e}_{j}
, for each
j\in[\![{1,m}]\!]
. Then, there is a finite number of non-intersecting Bernoulli walk ensembles with any given entrance and exit data
(\mathsf{d};\mathsf{e})
and (possibly infinite) boundary conditions
(\mathsf{f};\mathsf{g})
.

The following lemma from
[
CEP96
]
provides a monotonicity property for non-intersecting Bernoulli walk ensembles randomly sampled under the uniform measure on the set of such families with prescribed entrance, exit, and boundary conditions. In what follows, for any functions
\mathsf{f},\mathsf{f}^{\prime}:[\![{0,{\mathsf{t}}}]\!]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we write
\mathsf{f}\leqslant\mathsf{f}^{\prime}
if
\mathsf{f}(r)\leqslant\mathsf{f}^{\prime}(r)
for each
r\in[\![{0,{\mathsf{t}}}]\!]
. Similarly, for any sequences
\mathsf{d}=(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{m})\subset\mathbb{R}
and
\mathsf{d}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{d}_{1}^{\prime},\mathsf{d}_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{m}^{\prime})\subset\mathbb{R}
, we write
\mathsf{d}\leqslant\mathsf{d}^{\prime}
if
\mathsf{d}_{j}\leqslant\mathsf{d}_{j}^{\prime}
for each
j\in[\![{1,m}]\!]
.

Lemma 4.1

(
[
CEP96
, Lemma 18]
)
.

Fix integers
{\mathsf{t}},m\geqslant 1
; functions
\mathsf{f},\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g}^{\prime}:[\![{0,{\mathsf{t}}}]\!]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
; and
m
-tuples
\mathsf{d},\mathsf{d}^{\prime},\mathsf{e},\mathsf{e}^{\prime}
with coordinates indexed by
[\![{1,m}]\!]
. Let
\mathsf{X}=(\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
denote a uniformly random non-intersecting Bernoulli walk ensemble with boundary data
(\mathsf{f};\mathsf{g})
; entrance data
\mathsf{d}
; and exit data
\mathsf{e}
. Define
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{x}_{1}^{\prime},\mathsf{x}_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{m}^{\prime})
similarly, but with respect to
(\mathsf{f}^{\prime};\mathsf{g}^{\prime})
and
(\mathsf{d}^{\prime};\mathsf{e}^{\prime})
. If
\mathsf{f}\leqslant\mathsf{f}^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{g}\leqslant\mathsf{g}^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{d}\leqslant\mathsf{d}^{\prime}
, and
\mathsf{e}\leqslant\mathsf{e}^{\prime}
, then there exists coupling between
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{x}_{j}\leqslant\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}
almost surely, for each
j\in[\![{1,m}]\!]
.

Remark 4.2
.

An equivalent way of stating
Lemma

4.1
(as was done in
[
CEP96
]
) is through the height functions associated with the Bernoulli walk ensembles
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
. Specifically, let
\mathsf{D}\subset\mathbb{T}
be a finite domain, and let
\mathsf{h},\mathsf{h}^{\prime}:\partial\mathsf{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
denote two boundary height functions such that
\mathsf{h}(v)\geqslant\mathsf{h}^{\prime}(v)
, for each
v\in\partial\mathsf{D}
. Let
\mathsf{H},\mathsf{H}^{\prime}:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
denote two uniformly random height functions on
\mathsf{D}
with boundary data
\mathsf{H}|_{\partial\mathsf{D}}=\mathsf{h}
and
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}|_{\partial\mathsf{D}}=\mathsf{h}^{\prime}
. Then,
Lemma

4.1
implies (and is equivalent to) the existence of a coupling between
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{u})\geqslant\mathsf{H}^{\prime}(\mathsf{u})
almost surely, for each
\mathsf{u}\in\mathsf{D}
.

4.2.
Weighted Non-intersecting Bernoulli Bridges

In this section, we introduce a weighted measure on non-intersecting Bernoulli bridges, which corresponds to non-intersecting Bernoulli bridges with random boundary data at the final time
{\mathfrak{t}}
.

To implement this, in the rest of this paper, we denote
\mathbb{Z}_{n}=\{\cdots,-2/n,-1/n,0,1/n,2/n,\cdots\}
to be the set of integers rescaled by
n
, and rescale the non-intersecting Bernoulli walk ensemble (
4.1
) (both time and space) by a factor
n
. Then time and particle locations are all indexed by
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.

A
particle configuration
is an increasing sequence
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{m}
. We encode any such particle configuration by the empirical measure

(4.2)

\displaystyle\rho(x;{\bm{x}})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bm{1}(x\in[x_{i},x_{i}+1/n]).

For any
t\in[0,{\mathfrak{t}})\cap\mathbb{Z}_{n}
and particle configuration
{\bm{x}}\subseteq[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)-1/n]
, we construct a boundary profile
\beta(x)=\beta(x;{\bm{x}})
corresponding to
{\bm{x}}
with the empirical measure (
4.2
):

(4.3)

\displaystyle\beta(x;{\bm{x}})=\int_{{\mathfrak{a}}(t)}^{x}\rho(x;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}x,\quad\text{meaning}\quad\partial_{x}\beta(x;{\bm{x}})=\rho(x;{\bm{x}}).

By construction it satisfies
\beta(x_{i}+1/n;{\bm{x}})=i/n
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant m
.

We will choose the weights in a way that the height functions of weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli bridges at time
{\mathfrak{t}}
concentrate around
H_{{\mathfrak{t}}}^{*}(x)
. Then
Theorem

2.6
will follow from concentration estimates for this weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge model.
We define

(4.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}Y_{t}({\bm{x}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=W_{t}(\beta(x;{\bm{x}}))-\frac{1}{2}\int\log|x-y|\rho(x;{\bm{x}})\rho(y;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}x{\rm d}y\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\int\int_{{\mathfrak{a}}(t)}^{x}\log(x-y)\rho(x;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}y{\rm d}x+\int\int_{x}^{{\mathfrak{b}}(t)}\log(y-x)\rho(x;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}y{\rm d}x,\end{split}

For any
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{m})\subseteq\big[{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{t}}),{\mathfrak{b}}({\mathfrak{t}})\big]
, we then define the partition function

(4.5)

\displaystyle N_{{\mathfrak{t}}}({\bm{x}})=\frac{V({\bm{x}})e^{n^{2}Y_{{\mathfrak{t}}}({\bm{x}})}}{\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Gamma_{n}\big(x_{j}-{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{t}})\big)\Gamma_{n}\big({\mathfrak{b}}({\mathfrak{t}})-x_{j}-1/n\big)},

where
V({\bm{x}})=\prod_{i<j}(x_{j}-x_{i})
is the Vandermonde determinant,
\Gamma_{n}(k/n)=(k/n)((k-1)/n)\cdots(1/n)=k!/n^{k}
, and for any
{\bm{x}}\not\subseteq\big[{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{t}}),{\mathfrak{b}}({\mathfrak{t}})\big]
, we simply set
N_{{\mathfrak{t}}}({\bm{x}})=0
.
Then for any
t\in[0,{\mathfrak{t}})\cap\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, and
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{m})\subseteq[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
,
N_{t}({\bm{x}})
is defined recursively as

(4.6)

\displaystyle N_{t}({\bm{x}})=\sum_{{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}N_{t+1/n}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}/n).

We then define a family of weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge
\big\{{\bm{x}}_{t}=(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t),\cdots,x_{m}(t))\big\}_{t\in[0,{\mathfrak{t}}]\cap\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
whose transition probability is given by

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}_{t+1/n}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}/n|{\bm{x}}_{t}={\bm{x}})=\frac{N_{t+1/n}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}/n)}{N_{t}({\bm{x}})},\quad{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{m},

for any
t\in[0,{\mathfrak{t}})\cap\mathbb{Z}_{n}
and
{\bm{x}}\subseteq[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
. This weighted model is one for non-intersecting Bernoulli bridges with random boundary data at time
{\mathfrak{t}}
. Indeed, given any initial particle configuration
{\bm{x}}_{0}\subseteq[{\mathfrak{a}}(0),{\mathfrak{b}}(0)]
, it can be sampled in two steps. First we sample the boundary configuration
{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}}
using (
4.5
):

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}})=\frac{\#\{\text{non-intersecting Bernoulli bridges from ${\bm{x}}_{0}$ to ${\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}}$}\}\cdot N_{\mathfrak{t}}({\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}})}{\sum_{{\bm{x}}}\#\{\text{non-intersecting Bernoulli bridges from ${\bm{x}}_{0}$ to ${\bm{x}}$}\}\cdot N_{\mathfrak{t}}({\bm{x}})}.

Then we sample a non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge with boundary data
{\bm{x}}_{0},{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}}
uniformly from all possible non-intersecting Bernoulli bridges from
{\bm{x}}_{0}
to
{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}}
.

The recursion (
4.6
) can be solved using the Feynman–Kac formula. The transition probability (
4.7
) is approximated through the following proposition. Its proof is essentially the same as in
[
Hua21
, Section 9]
; we outline it in Appendix
E
below.

Proposition 4.3
.

Adopt
Assumption

3.2
.
Given any particle configuration
{\bm{x}}\subseteq[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
with density
\rho(x;\bm{x})
, define the boundary profile
\beta
by
\partial_{x}\beta=\rho(x;{\bm{x}})
. Assume that
\beta
is close to the continuum limiting profile at time
t
, i.e.,
d\big(\rho(x;{\bm{x}}),\partial_{x}H_{t}^{*}(x)\big)\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}
for some small constant
{\mathfrak{c}}
. Then the transition probability (
4.7
) is given by

(4.9)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{t}({\bm{x}})}\frac{V({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}/n)}{V({\bm{x}})}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\phi^{+}(x_{i};\beta,t)^{e_{i}}\phi^{-}(x_{i};\beta,t)^{1-e_{i}}\exp\Bigg(\displaystyle\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m}\frac{e_{i}e_{j}}{n^{2}}\kappa(x_{i},x_{j};\beta,t)+\OO(1/n)\Bigg),

where

(4.10)

\displaystyle\phi^{+}(z;\beta,t)=\frac{{\mathfrak{b}}(t)-1/n-z}{{\mathfrak{b}}(t)-z}\varphi^{+}(z;\beta,t)e^{\frac{1}{n}\psi_{t}^{+}(z;\beta,t)},\quad\phi^{-}(z;\beta,t)=\varphi^{-}(z;\beta,t).

and

(4.11)

\displaystyle\varphi^{-}(z;\beta,t)=z-{\mathfrak{a}}(t),\quad\varphi^{+}(z;\beta,t)=\tilde{g}(z;\beta,t)\big({\mathfrak{b}}(t)-z\big),

where
\tilde{g}(z;\beta,t)
is from (
3.22
);
\psi^{+}(z;\beta,t)
and
\kappa(z,z^{\prime};\beta,t)
are analytic in
z,z^{\prime}
and uniformly bounded in a neighborhood of
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
; and the error term of size
\OO(1/n)
uniform in
\beta
.

4.3.
Optimal Rigidity for Weighted Non-intersecting Bernoulli Bridges

We denote the height function for the weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge model as
H(x,t)=H_{t}(x)
, so that

(4.12)

\displaystyle H(x,t)=H_{t}(x)=\int_{{\mathfrak{a}}(t)}^{x}\rho(y;{\bm{x}}_{t}){\rm d}y,\quad\partial_{x}H(x,t)=\rho(x;{\bm{x}}_{t})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bm{1}([x_{i}(t),x_{i}(t)+1/n]),

where
{\bm{x}}_{t}=\big(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t),\cdots,x_{m}(t)\big)
.

To understand the height fluctuations around the boundary of the liquid region, we need an enlarged version of

I^{*}_{t}
as defined in (
3.11
). We recall that under
Assumption

3.2
, for
t_{c}\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
, the slice
I^{*}_{t}
is a single interval

I^{*}_{t}=\big[E_{1}(t),E_{2}(t)\big],

and for
0\leqslant t\leqslant t_{c}
, the slice
I^{*}_{t}
consists of two intervals

I^{*}_{t}=\big[E_{1}(t),E^{\prime}_{1}(t)\big]\cup\big[E^{\prime}_{2}(t),E_{2}(t)\big].

In the rest of this section, we fix an arbitrarily small constant
{\mathfrak{d}}>0
. For any
\big(E(t),t\big)=\big(E_{i}(t),t\big)
on the arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
with
i\in\{1,2\}
, we define the distance function

(4.13)

\displaystyle\tau\big(E(t),t\big)=n^{-2/3+6{\mathfrak{d}}}|t-t_{i}|^{2/3}\vee n^{-1+10{\mathfrak{d}}}.

Moreover, for any
\big(E(t),t\big)=\big(E^{\prime}_{i}(t),t\big)
on the arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
with
i\in\{1,2\}
and
t\leqslant t_{c}
, we define the distance function

\displaystyle\tau\big(E(t),t\big)=n^{-2/3+6{\mathfrak{d}}}(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}.

We then define the enlarged intervals

(4.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} I_{t}^{+}=\big[E_{1}(t)-\tau(E_{1}(t),t),E_{2}(t)+\tau(E_{2}(t),t)\big],&\qquad\text{for $t_{c}\leqslant t\leqslant\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}$};\\
I_{t}^{+}=\big[E_{1}(t)-\tau(E_{1}(t),t),E^{\prime}_{1}(t)+\tau(E^{\prime}_{1}(t),t)\big]\cup\big[E^{\prime}_{2}(t)-\tau(E^{\prime}_{2}(t),t),E_{2}(t)+\tau(E_{2}(t),t)\big],&\qquad\text{for $0\leqslant t<t_{c}$}.\end{aligned}

We remark that from the third statement in
Proposition

3.5
,
E_{2}^{\prime}(t)-E_{1}^{\prime}(t)\asymp(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}
. Thus for
t\geqslant t_{c}-n^{-1/2+6{\mathfrak{d}}}
, (
4.14
) reduces to a single interval,

(4.15)

\displaystyle I_{t}^{+}=\Big[E_{1}(t)-\tau\big(E_{1}(t),t\big),E_{2}(t)+\tau\big(E_{2}(t),t\big)\Big].

See Figure
6
for an illustration.

In Sections
5
, we will prove the following concentration result on the height function for the weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge model (
4.7
). We remark that our proof applies to any Markov process with transition probability in the form (
4.9
).

Proposition 4.4
.

Fix an arbitrarily small constant
{\mathfrak{d}}>0
, and adopt
Assumption

3.2
and
Assumption

2.5
.
For any initial data
{\bm{x}}_{0}\subseteq\big[{\mathfrak{a}}(0),{\mathfrak{b}}(0)\big]
with the corresponding height function
H_{0}(x)
satisfying

(4.16)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big|H_{0}(x)-H_{0}^{*}(x)\big|\leqslant n^{\mathfrak{d}/3-1},\qquad&\text{if $\dist(x,I_{0}^{*})\leqslant n^{{\mathfrak{d}}/3-2/3}$};\\
H_{0}(x)=H_{0}^{*}(x),\qquad&\text{if $\dist(x,I_{0}^{*})\geqslant n^{{\mathfrak{d}}/3-2/3}$},\end{aligned}

with overwhelming probability the height function
H(x,t)
for the weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge (
4.7
) satisfies, for
0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}
,

(4.17)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big|H(x,t)-H^{*}(x,t)\big|\leqslant n^{3{\mathfrak{d}}-1},\qquad\text{if $x\in I_{t}^{+}$},\\
H(x,t)=H^{*}(x,t),\qquad\text{if $x\not\in I_{t}^{+}$}.\end{aligned}

Given this, we can quickly prove
Theorem

2.6
.

Proof of
Theorem

2.6
.

We recall that the weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli random walks can be sampled in two steps. First we sample the boundary configuration
{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}}
using (
4.8
). Then we sample a non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge with boundary data given by
{\bm{x}}_{0}
and
{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}}
.

We fix the initial data
{\bm{x}}_{0}
corresponding to boundary height function
{\mathsf{h}}
as in
Assumption

2.5
restricted to
{\partial_{\so}{\mathfrak{D}}}
. And we take
\mathfrak{d}=\delta/15
in
Proposition

4.4
, then
Assumption

2.5
implies (
4.16
). By the
t=\mathfrak{t}
case of (
4.17
), a boundary configuration
{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}}
sampled from (
4.8
) satisfies with overwhelming probability the bound

(4.18)

\displaystyle\big|\beta(x;{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}})-H^{*}(x,{\mathfrak{t}})\big|\leqslant n^{3{\mathfrak{d}}-1},\quad\text{if $x\in I_{\mathfrak{t}}^{+}$;}\qquad\qquad\beta(x;{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}})=H^{*}(x,{\mathfrak{t}}),\quad\text{if $x\not\in I_{\mathfrak{t}}^{+}$}.

Moreover, for the (fixed) boundary data
{\bm{x}}_{0},{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}}
, the height functions
H(x,t)
for non-intersecting Bernoulli bridges between
{\bm{x}}_{0}
and
{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}}
satisfy the optimal rigidity estimates (
4.17
) for
0\leqslant t\leqslant\mathfrak{t}
. In the following, we fix a sample of such boundary configuration
{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}}
.

The boundary height function estimates (
4.16
) and (
4.18
) imply that

(4.19)

\displaystyle\beta(x;{\bm{x}}_{t})-(n^{3{\mathfrak{d}}-1}+n^{\delta/2-1})\leqslant n^{-1}{\mathsf{h}}(nx,nt)\leqslant\beta(x;{\bm{x}}_{t})+(n^{3{\mathfrak{d}}-1}+n^{\delta/2-1}),\quad t\in\{0,{\mathfrak{t}}\}.

By
Lemma

4.1
(and
Remark

4.2
), we may couple
H
and
\mathsf{H}
so that with overwhelming probability we have

(4.20)

\displaystyle H(x,t)-(n^{3\mathfrak{d}-1}+n^{\delta/2-1})\leqslant n^{-1}{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)\leqslant H(x,t)+(n^{3\mathfrak{d}-1}+n^{\delta/2-1}),

for each
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}
. Thus, we conclude from this coupling and (
4.17
) that with overwhelming probability

\displaystyle\big|n^{-1}{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)-H^{*}(x,t)\big|\leqslant\big|n^{-1}{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)-H(x,t)\big|+\big|H(x,t)-H^{*}(x,t)\big|\leqslant n^{\delta/2-1}+2n^{3{\mathfrak{d}}-1}\leqslant n^{\delta-1},

for any
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}
, provided that
\delta>4\mathfrak{d}
.
This gives the first statement in
Theorem

2.6
.

For the second statement in
Theorem

2.6
, we need to show that
n^{-1}{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)=H^{*}(x,t)
for any
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\setminus{\mathfrak{L}}_{+}^{\delta}({\mathfrak{D}})
. For any time
0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}
, the time slice
\{x:(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\setminus{\mathfrak{L}}_{+}^{\delta}({\mathfrak{D}})\}
consists of several intervals. On each interval either
\partial_{x}H^{*}(x,t)\equiv 0
, or
\partial_{x}H^{*}(x,t)\equiv 1
, see
Remark

3.3
. We denote by
J_{t}
one such interval, with
\partial_{x}H^{*}(x,t)\equiv 0
corresponding to a void region, and prove
n^{-1}{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)=H^{*}(x,t)
for
x\in J_{t}
. The proof in the other case when
\partial_{x}H^{*}(x,t)\equiv 1
on
J_{t}
is very similar and thus omitted.

In the following we first show that

(4.21)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\beta\left(x-\frac{n^{7{\mathfrak{d}}}+n^{\delta/2}}{n^{2/3}};{\bm{x}}_{t}\right)\leqslant n^{-1}{\mathsf{h}}(nx,nt)\leqslant\beta\left(x+\frac{n^{7{\mathfrak{d}}}+n^{\delta/2}}{n^{2/3}};{\bm{x}}_{t}\right),\quad t\in\{0,{\mathfrak{t}}\}.\end{split}

We prove (
4.21
) for
t={\mathfrak{t}}
; the case
t=0
is essentially the same.
The measure
\partial_{x}H^{*}(x,{\mathfrak{t}})=\rho^{*}_{\mathfrak{t}}(x)
is supported on
I_{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}=[E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}),E_{2}({\mathfrak{t}})]
. Thanks to
Remark

3.6
, it has square root behavior close to the edges
E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}})
and
E_{2}({\mathfrak{t}})
. In particular, for
x\leqslant E_{2}({\mathfrak{t}})-{\mathfrak{C}}(n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}}+n^{\delta/3})/n^{2/3}
, (
4.19
) and this square root behavior together imply

(4.22)

\displaystyle\beta(x;{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}})\leqslant H^{*}(x,{\mathfrak{t}})+n^{3{\mathfrak{d}}-1}+n^{\delta/2-1}\leqslant H^{*}\left(x+\frac{{\mathfrak{C}}(n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}}+n^{\delta/3})}{n^{2/3}},{\mathfrak{t}}\right),

provided the constant
{\mathfrak{C}}
is large enough. For
x\geqslant E_{2}({\mathfrak{t}})-{\mathfrak{C}}(n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}}+n^{\delta/3})/n^{2/3}
, the second statement of (
4.18
) implies that

(4.23)

\displaystyle\beta(x;{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}})\leqslant\beta\left(x+\frac{{\mathfrak{C}}(n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}}+n^{\delta/3}+n^{6{\mathfrak{d}}})+n^{\delta/2}}{n^{2/3}};{\bm{x}}_{\mathfrak{t}}\right)=H^{*}\left(x+\frac{{\mathfrak{C}}(n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}}+n^{\delta/3}+n^{6{\mathfrak{d}}})+n^{\delta/2}}{n^{2/3}},{\mathfrak{t}}\right).

The lower bound in (
4.21
) follows from combining (
4.22
) and (
4.23
), and recalling that
n^{-1}{\mathsf{h}}(nx,n{\mathfrak{t}})=H^{*}(x,{\mathfrak{t}})
. The upper bound in (
4.21
) can be proven in the same way. With (
4.21
) as input, the coupling
Remark

4.2
gives

(4.24)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&H\left(x-\frac{n^{7{\mathfrak{d}}}+n^{\delta/2}}{n^{2/3}},t\right)\leqslant n^{-1}{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)\leqslant H\left(x+\frac{n^{7{\mathfrak{d}}}+n^{\delta/2}}{n^{2/3}},t\right),\end{split}

for any
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}
. For
x\in J_{t}
,
H^{*}(x,t)
is a constant. This, with the second statement of (
4.17
), together imply that with overwhelming probability the left and right sides of (
4.24
) coincide, and both equal
H^{*}(x,t)
, provided we take
\delta>14{\mathfrak{d}}
. Thus, the second statement in
Theorem

2.6
follows.
∎

5.
Optimal Concentration for Weighted Non-intersecting Bernoulli Bridges

In this section we prove the concentration estimate for the weighted non-intersecting Bernoulli bridge model,
Proposition

4.4
.
We recall the weighted Bernoulli bridges from Section
4.2
. The weighted Bernoulli bridges with initial data given by the particle configuration
{\bm{x}}_{0}=\big\{x_{1}(0),x_{2}(0),\cdots,x_{m}(0)\big\}\subseteq\big[{\mathfrak{a}}(0),{\mathfrak{b}}(0)\big]
is a Markov process
\big\{{\bm{x}}_{t}=(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t),\cdots,x_{m}(t))\big\}_{t\in[0,{\mathfrak{t}}]\cap\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
with transition probability given by (
4.9
)

(5.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}_{t+1/n}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}/n|{\bm{x}}_{t}={\bm{x}})=:a_{t}({\bm{e}};{\bm{x}})\\
&=\frac{1}{Z_{t}({\bm{x}})}\frac{V({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}/n)}{V({\bm{x}})}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\phi^{+}(x_{i};\beta,t)^{e_{i}}\phi^{-}(x_{i};\beta,t)^{1-e_{i}}\exp\Bigg(\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m}\frac{e_{i}e_{j}}{n^{2}}\kappa(x_{i},x_{j};\beta,t)+\OO(1/n)\Bigg),\end{split}

where the height profile
\beta
is determined by
\bm{x}_{t}=\bm{x}
through
\partial_{x}\beta=\rho(x;{\bm{x}})
.
We will study the dynamics of the Stieltjes transform of its empirical particle density

(5.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\rho(x;{\bm{x}}_{t})=\partial_{x}H_{t}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bm{1}\Big(x\in\big[x_{i}(t),x_{i}(t)+1/n\big]\Big),\\
&m_{t}(z)=\int\frac{\rho(x;{\bm{x}}_{t})}{z-x}{\rm d}x=\int^{z}_{z-1/n}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{u-x_{i}(t)}{\rm d}u.\end{split}

We recall the continuum limit height function
H^{*}_{t}(x)
, the limiting particle density
\rho_{t}^{*}(x)
and its Stieltjes transform from (
3.24
) and (
3.25
)

(5.3)

\displaystyle\rho_{t}^{*}(x)=\partial_{x}H_{t}^{*}(x),\quad m_{t}^{*}(z)=\int\frac{\rho_{t}^{*}(x)}{z-x}{\rm d}x.

Concentration for the height function will eventually follow from showing that the difference of
m_{t}(z)
and
m_{t}^{*}(z)

(5.4)

\displaystyle\Delta_{t}(z)=m_{t}(z)-m_{t}^{*}(z),

is small on a certain spectral domain. In the Section
5.1
we construct this spectral domain. We collect some preliminary estimates in Section
5.2
. In Section
5.3
we derive dynamical equations for
\Delta_{t}(z)
. Finally in Section
5.4
we analyze the dynamical equations for
\Delta_{t}(z)
and prove
Proposition

4.4
.

5.1.
Spectral Domain

We recall the discussion after (
3.24
). Two copies of
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\cap{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
glued along the arctic curve is mapped bijectively to the domain
\mathscr{U}_{t}
via (
3.17
). For any
0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}
and
0\leqslant r\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t
, we define the domain
{\mathscr{D}}_{t}(r)
as

(5.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Bigg\{x+(t-s)\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{s}(x)}{f^{*}_{s}(x)+1}\in\mathscr{U}_{t}:(x,s)\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}),t+r\leqslant s\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\Bigg\},\end{split}

together with its complex conjugate. Under the identification of the liquid region
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\cap{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
with
\mathscr{U}_{t}
via (
3.17
),
\mathscr{D}_{t}(r)
corresponds to the region
\{(x,s)\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}):t+r\leqslant s\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\}
. See Figure
9
for an illustration.

y={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
y=t+r
\mathscr{D}_{t}(r)
y=t
y=0
\mathfrak{L}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
y=t

Figure 9.

Shown to the left is the gluing of two copies of the liquid region
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
(with
s=0
and
\beta=H_{0}^{*}(x)
), which can be identified with
\mathscr{U}_{t}

). Under this identification,
\mathscr{D}_{t}(r)

) corresponds to the region
\{(x,s)\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}):t+r\leqslant s\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\}
; shown to the right is an illustration of
\mathscr{D}_{t}(r)
inside
\mathscr{U}_{t}
.

In the rest of this section, we fix a small constant
0<{\mathfrak{r}}<{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-{\mathfrak{t}}
.
Since the spectral domain
\mathscr{D}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})
is bounded away from
I_{t}^{*}
, we will see that the behavior of
\Delta_{t}(z)
for
z\in\mathscr{D}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})
is relatively easy to understand.
To understand the Stieltjes transform close to
I_{t}^{*}
, we define the
spectral domains
(observe that we exclude
\mathscr{D}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})
from them):

(5.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{L}=\Big\{z\in\mathscr{U}_{t}:n|\Imaginary[z]\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}^{*}]/f_{t}^{*}|\geqslant n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}}/2\Big\}\setminus{\mathscr{D}}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}}).\end{split}

We recall that under
Assumption

3.2
, for
t_{c}\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
, the slice
I^{*}_{t}
is a single interval

I^{*}_{t}=\big[E_{1}(t),E_{2}(t)\big],

and for
0\leqslant t\leqslant t_{c}
, the slice
I^{*}_{t}
consists of two intervals

I^{*}_{t}=\big[E_{1}(t),E^{\prime}_{1}(t)\big]\cup\big[E^{\prime}_{2}(t),E_{2}(t)\big].

We define
{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm F}
as the union of

(5.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{\bigcup{}}\Big\{z\in\mathscr{U}_{t}:\min\big\{(n\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z)^{2},n\sqrt{\dist(z,I_{t}^{*})|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z|}\big\}\cdot|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}^{*}]/f_{t}^{*}|\geqslant n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}},\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z\leqslant E_{1}(t)-\tau\big(E_{1}(t),t\big)\Big\}\\
&\cup\Big\{z\in\mathscr{U}_{t}:\min\big\{(n\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z)^{2},n\sqrt{\dist(z,I_{t}^{*})|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z|}\big\}\cdot|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}^{*}]/f_{t}^{*}|\geqslant n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}},\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z\geqslant E_{2}(t)+\tau\big(E_{2}(t),t\big)\Big\}\end{split}

(5.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\cup\bm{1}(t\leqslant t_{c}-n^{-1/2+{\mathfrak{d}}})\bigg\{z\in\mathscr{U}_{t}:\min\big\{(n\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z)^{2},n\sqrt{\dist(z,I_{t}^{*})|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z|}\big\}\cdot|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}f_{t}^{*}/f_{t}^{*}|\geqslant n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}},\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\phantom{\bigcup{}}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z\in\Big[E^{\prime}_{1}(t)+\tau\big(E^{\prime}_{1}(t),t\big),E_{2}^{\prime}(t)+\tau\big(E^{\prime}_{2}(t),t\big)\Big]\bigg\}\setminus{\mathscr{D}}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}}).\end{split}

Estimates of Stieltjes transform on
{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm L}
will give the information of particles inside the liquid region, and the estimates on
{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm F}
will be used to control the location of particles in the frozen region. We refer to Figure
10
for a depiction.

Remark 5.1
.

In context of Wigner matrices
[
EY17
]
, the spectral domains are given in the bulk by
\big\{z\in\mathbb{H}^{+}:\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{sc}(z)]\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\gg 1/n\big\}
and at the edge by
\big\{z\in\mathbb{H}^{+}:\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{sc}(z)]\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\dist(z,[-2,2])}\gg 1/n\big\}
, where
m_{sc}
denotes the Stieltjes transform of the semi-circle distribution. In our construction,
\big|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}^{*}]/f_{t}^{*}\big|=-\sin\big(\arg^{*}(f_{t}^{*})\big)
plays the same role as the Stieltjes transform of the semi-circle distribution; this quantity
|\Imaginary f_{t}^{*}/f_{t}^{*}|
will also take into account the singular behaviors of
f_{t}^{*}
close to the tangency locations.

Thanks to
Proposition

3.5
, we have explicit estimates for
\big|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}^{*}(z)]/f_{t}^{*}(z)\big|=-\sin\big(\arg^{*}(f_{t}^{*}(z))\big)
close to the arctic curve. We can rewrite the condition (
5.6
) as
\big|\sin(\arg^{*}(f_{t}^{*}(z)))\big|\geqslant n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}-1}/|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z|
. For
z
in the domain (
5.7
), say
z=E_{1}(t)-\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{H}^{+}
, (
2
) gives

(5.9)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}f_{t}^{*}(z)}{f_{t}^{*}(z)}\right|\asymp\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}\big(\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+|t_{1}-t|\big)}+\sin\arg\big(z-{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\big).

For
\kappa+\eta\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{1}-t)^{2}
, we can rewrite the condition in (
5.7
) as

\displaystyle\min\bigg\{(n\eta)^{2},n\sqrt{(\kappa+\eta)\eta}\bigg\}\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}|t_{1}-t|}\gtrsim n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}},

and it simplifies to

\eta\gtrsim\big(n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}-1}|t_{1}-t|\big)^{2/3}
. For
z
in the domain (
5.8
), say
z=E^{\prime}_{1}(t)+\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{H}^{+}
, with
\kappa+\eta\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}
, (
3
) gives

(5.10)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}f_{t}^{*}(z)}{f_{t}^{*}(z)}\right|\asymp\frac{\eta}{(t_{c}-t)^{1/4}\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}},

and we can rewrite the condition in (
5.8
) as

\eta\gtrsim\big(n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}-1}|t_{c}-t|^{1/4})^{2/3}
.

\mathscr{D}^{\rm L}_{t}
y=t
\mathscr{D}^{\rm L}_{t}\cup\mathscr{D}^{\rm F}_{t}
y=t

Figure 10.
Shown to the left is an illustration of the spectral domain
\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\rm L}
; shown to the right is an illustration of the spectral domain
\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\rm L}\cup\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\rm F}
.

Next, we define the control parameter

(5.11)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{M}}(t):=Me^{Mt}n^{{{\mathfrak{d}}/3}},

and the stopping time

(5.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sigma&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf_{0\leqslant t\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}\left\{\exists z\in{\mathscr{D}}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}}):\left|\Delta_{t}(z)\right|\geqslant\frac{{\mathfrak{M}}(t)}{n}\right\}\wedge\inf_{0\leqslant t\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}\left\{\exists z\in{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm L}:\left|\Delta_{t}(z)\right|\geqslant\frac{Mn^{{\mathfrak{d}}}}{n\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z}\right\}\\
&\qquad\wedge\inf_{0\leqslant t\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}\left\{\exists z\in{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm F}:\left|\Delta_{t}(z)\right|\geqslant\frac{Mn^{{\mathfrak{d}}}}{n\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\dist(z,I_{t}^{*})}}+\frac{Mn^{\mathfrak{d}}}{(n\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z)^{2}}\right\}\wedge{\mathfrak{t}},\end{split}

where the large constant
M>0
will be chosen later, and
a\wedge b=\min\{a,b\}
.

In the rest of this section, we restrict our analysis to complex numbers
z
in these spectral domains satisfying
z\in\mathbb{H}^{+}
; the estimates we show on these quantities will follow for
z\in\mathbb{H}^{-}
by symmetry, so we will not comment on this case in the below. We further recall the characteristic
z_{t}(u)
from (
3.9
) and (
3.10
). Then
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f^{*}_{0}(u)]=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f^{*}_{t}(z_{t}(u))]\leqslant 0
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{t}(u)]=\Imaginary u+t\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{0}^{*}(u)]/|f_{0}^{*}(u)+1|^{2}
is nonincreasing in
t
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Adopt the assumptions of
Proposition

4.4
; fix
u\in\mathbb{H}^{+}
; and abbreviate
z_{s}=z_{s}(u)
for
s\in[0,{\mathfrak{t}}]
. Then the following statements hold.

(1)

If
z_{t}
is in the domain (
5.7
), then for any
0\leqslant s\leqslant t
,

(5.13)

\displaystyle\dist(z_{s},I^{+}_{s})\gtrsim\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}+\sqrt{\dist(z_{s},I_{s}^{*})}(t-s).

(2)

If
z_{t}
is in the domain (
5.8
), then for any
0\leqslant s\leqslant t\leqslant t_{c}
,

(5.14)

\displaystyle\dist(z_{s},I^{+}_{s})\gtrsim\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}+\sqrt{\dist(z_{s},I_{s}^{*})}\frac{t-s}{(t_{c}-s)^{1/4}}.

(3)

If
z_{t}\in{\mathscr{D}}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm L}\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm F}
, then for any
0\leqslant s\leqslant t
, we have
z_{s}\in{\mathscr{D}}_{s}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{s}^{\rm L}\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{s}^{\rm F}
.

(4)

Recall
I_{t}^{+}
from (
4.14
), and the height function
H_{t}(x)
corresponding to the particle configuration
{\bm{x}}_{t}
from (
4.12
). If we further assume that
t\in[0,\sigma)\cap\mathbb{Z}_{n}
then we have

(5.15)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |H_{t}(x)-H_{t}^{*}(x)|\leqslant n^{3{\mathfrak{d}}-1},\qquad&\text{if $x\in I_{t}^{+}$};\\
H_{t}(x)=H_{t}^{*}(x),\qquad&\text{if $x\not\in I_{t}^{+}$}.\end{aligned}

Proof.

We begin by establishing the first statement of the proposition. For
z_{t}
in the domain (
5.7
), we consider the case when
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z_{t}\leqslant E_{1}(t)
, for the case when
\Real z_{t}\geqslant E_{2}(t)
is addressed very similarly. We denote
E(r)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=E_{1}(r)
and
z_{r}=E_{1}(r)-\kappa_{r}+\mathrm{i}\eta_{r}
for
r\in[s,t]
.
From (
3.29
),
f^{*}_{r}(z)/(f^{*}_{r}(z)+1)
has square root behavior in a small neighborhood of
E(r)
,

(5.16)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{r}(z)}{f^{*}_{r}(z)+1}=\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{r}\big(E(r)\big)}{f^{*}_{r}\big(E(r)\big)+1}+{\mathfrak{C}}\sqrt{E(r)-z}+\OO\big(|z-E(r)|\big),\quad|z-E(r)|\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}},

where
{\mathfrak{C}}\gtrsim 1
and the square root is the branch with nonpositive imaginary part.
Moreover, by (
2.9
),
E(r)
satisfies the differential equation

(5.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}E(r)=\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{r}\big(E(r)\big)}{f^{*}_{r}\big(E(r)\big)+1}.

By taking difference between (
5.17
) and the characteristic flow
\partial_{r}z_{r}=f^{*}_{r}(z_{r})/\big(f^{*}_{r}(z_{r})+1\big)
, and then taking the real part we get

(5.18)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\kappa_{r}=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{r}(E(r))}{f^{*}_{r}\big(E(r)\big)+1}-\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{r}(z_{r})}{f^{*}_{r}(z_{r})+1}\bigg].

If there is some time
s\leqslant r\leqslant t
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{r}\geqslant{\mathfrak{c}}/2
, then
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}\geqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{r}\geqslant{\mathfrak{c}}/2
, and so

\displaystyle\dist(z_{s},I^{+}_{s})\geqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}\gtrsim\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}+\sqrt{\dist(z_{s},I_{s}^{*})}(t-s),

holds trivially. In the following, we assume that for all
s\leqslant r\leqslant t
we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{r}\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}/2
.
If
\big|z_{r}-E(r)\big|\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}
and
\kappa_{r}\geqslant 0
, then (
5.16
) and (
5.18
) together give

(5.19)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\kappa_{r}=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{r}(E(r))}{f^{*}_{r}\big(E(r)\big)+1}-\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{r}(z_{r})}{f^{*}_{r}(z_{r})+1}\bigg]\lesssim-\sqrt{\eta_{r}+\kappa_{r}}.

Since
\kappa_{t}\geqslant 0
, we conclude that
\kappa_{r}
will remain positive for
s\leqslant r\leqslant t
. In the following we further assume that for all
s\leqslant r\leqslant t
, we have
0\leqslant\kappa_{r}\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}/2
. Otherwise, we can simply repeat the same argument with
t=r
such that
\kappa_{r}={\mathfrak{c}}/2
(if
r=s
, then
\kappa_{s}\geqslant{\mathfrak{c}}/2\gtrsim 1
, and so (
5.13
) holds again). Under this assumption,
\big|z_{r}-E(r)\big|\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}
and (
5.19
) holds for
s\leqslant r\leqslant t
.
Moreover, the characteristic flow gives (using (
1
))

(5.20)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\eta_{s}=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{s}(z_{s})}{f^{*}_{s}(z_{s})+1}\bigg]\lesssim-\frac{\eta_{s}}{\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}}.

By taking sum of (
5.19
) and (
5.20
), we have

(5.21)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}(\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s})\lesssim-\sqrt{\eta_{s}+\kappa_{s}}.

The differential equation (
5.21
) can be solved directly; it gives

(5.22)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}-\sqrt{\kappa_{t}+\eta_{t}}\gtrsim t-s,\quad(\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s})-(\kappa_{t}+\eta_{t})\gtrsim(t-s)\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}.

We also notice that
\kappa_{t}\geqslant\tau\big(E(t),t\big)
, since
z_{t}
is in the domain (
5.7
). Recall from (
4.13
) that if
|t_{1}-r|\geqslant n^{6{\mathfrak{d}}-1/2}
then we have

\tau\big(E(r),r\big)=(t_{1}-r)^{2/3}/n^{2/3-6{\mathfrak{d}}}
; otherwise we have
\tau\big(E(t),t\big)=n^{10{\mathfrak{d}}-1}
. Thus, for
|t_{1}-t|,|t_{1}-s|\geqslant n^{6{\mathfrak{d}}-1/2}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\tau\big(E(s),s\big)-\tau\big(E(t),t\big)=\frac{(t_{1}-s)^{2/3}-(t_{1}-t)^{2/3}}{n^{2/3-6{\mathfrak{d}}}}\ll(t-s)\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}.

Similarly, for the other cases, one can quickly verify that
\big|\tau(E(s),s)-\tau(E(t),t)\big|\ll(t-s)\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}
. Hence,

(5.24)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\dist(z_{s},I_{s}^{+})\gtrsim\eta_{s}+\max\Big\{\eta_{s}+\kappa_{s}-\tau\big(E(s),s\big),0\Big\}\gtrsim\eta_{s}+\max\Big\{\eta_{t}+\kappa_{t}-\tau\big(E(s),s\big),0\Big\}+(t-s)\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}\\
&\gtrsim\eta_{s}+\max\Big\{\eta_{t}+\kappa_{t}-\tau\big(E(t),t\big),0\Big\}+(t-s)\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}\gtrsim\eta_{s}+(t-s)\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}},\end{aligned}

This gives (
5.13
).

We next establish the second statement of the proposition. For
z_{t}
in the domain (
5.8
), we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z_{t}\in\big[E_{1}^{\prime}(t),E^{\prime}_{2}(t)\big]
. We denote
E(r)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=E_{1}^{\prime}(r)
,
E^{\prime}(r)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=E_{2}^{\prime}(r)
and
z_{r}=E(r)+\kappa_{r}+\mathrm{i}\eta_{r}
for any
s\leqslant r\leqslant t\leqslant t_{c}
.
Take small
{\mathfrak{c}}>0
which will be chosen later. First assume that
r=\inf\{\tau\in[s,t]:\Imaginary z_{\tau}\geqslant\mathfrak{c}(t_{c}-\tau)^{3/2}/2\}
exists. Next we show that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}\gtrsim(t_{c}-s)^{3/2}
. If
r=s
, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise
r>s
, then we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{r}={\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-r)^{3/2}/2
and
\partial_{r}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{r}\geqslant-(3{\mathfrak{c}}/4)(t_{c}-r)^{1/2}
. From (
3.41
),
f^{*}_{r}(z)/\big(f^{*}_{r}(z)+1\big)
has cube root behavior

(5.25)

\displaystyle-\Imaginary\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{r}(z)}{f^{*}_{r}(z)+1}\bigg]\geqslant C\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]^{1/3},

for
\Imaginary z\geqslant{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-r)^{3/2}/2
. Hence, if
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{r}\geqslant{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-r)^{3/2}/2
, then (
5.25
) yields

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\eta_{r}\leqslant-C({\mathfrak{c}}/2)^{1/3}(t_{c}-r)^{1/2}.

This contradicts with
\partial_{r}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{r}\geqslant-(3{\mathfrak{c}}/4)(t_{c}-r)^{1/2}
, provided we take
({\mathfrak{c}}/2)^{2/3}\leqslant(2C/3)
.
It follows that we always have
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}\gtrsim(t_{c}-s)^{3/2}
, and so

\displaystyle\dist(z_{s},I_{s}^{+})\geqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}\gtrsim\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}+\sqrt{\dist(z_{s},I_{s}^{*})}\frac{t-s}{(t_{c}-s)^{1/4}},

where we have used the fact that
\kappa_{s}\leqslant\big|E_{1}^{\prime}(s)-E_{2}^{\prime}(s)\big|\lesssim(t_{c}-s)^{3/2}
, by the third statement of
Proposition

3.5
.

Next, we assume that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{r}]\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-r)^{3/2}
for
r\in[s,t]
. If
\big|z_{r}-E(r)\big|\leqslant\mathfrak{c}(t_{c}-r)^{3/2}
for some
r\in[s,t]
, then, from (
3.38
),
f^{*}_{r}(z)/\big(f^{*}_{r}(z)+1\big)
has square root behavior with a constant depending on
t_{c}-r
, namely

(5.26)

\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{r}(z)}{f^{*}_{r}(z)+1}=\frac{f^{*}_{r}\big(E(r)\big)}{f^{*}_{r}\big(E(r)\big)+1}+\frac{{\mathfrak{C}}\sqrt{z-E(r)}}{(t_{c}-r)^{1/4}}+\OO\left((t_{c}-r)^{1/4}\big|z-E(r)\big|^{1/2}+\frac{\big|z-E(r)\big|}{t_{c}-r}\right),

where the square root is the branch with nonpositive imaginary part. Similarly to (
5.19
), we have

(5.27)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\kappa_{r}=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\left[\frac{f^{*}_{r}\big(E(r)\big)}{f^{*}_{r}\big(E(r)\big)+1}-\frac{f^{*}_{r}(z_{r})}{f^{*}_{r}(z_{r})+1}\right]\lesssim-\frac{\sqrt{\eta_{r}+\kappa_{r}}}{(t_{c}-r)^{1/4}}.

Thanks to (
5.27
),
\kappa_{r}
will remain positive for
s\leqslant r\leqslant t
. In the following we further assume that for all
s\leqslant r\leqslant t
we have
0\leqslant\kappa_{r}\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-r)^{3/2}/2
. Otherwise, we can simply repeat the same argument as used above with
t=r
such that
\kappa_{r}={\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-r)^{3/2}/2
(if
r=s
, then
\kappa_{s}\geqslant(t_{c}-s)^{3/2}
, and so (
5.14
) holds again). Under this assumption, we have
\big|z_{r}-E(r)\big|\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-r)^{3/2}
and (
5.27
) holds for each
r\in[s,t]
. Moreover, the characteristic flow gives (by (
3
))

(5.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\eta_{r}=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left[\frac{f^{*}_{r}(z_{r})}{f^{*}_{r}(z_{r})+1}\right]\lesssim-\frac{\eta_{r}}{(t_{c}-r)^{1/4}\sqrt{\kappa_{r}+\eta_{r}}}.

By taking sum of (
5.27
) and (
5.28
), we have

(5.29)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(\kappa_{r}+\eta_{r})\lesssim-\frac{\sqrt{\kappa_{r}+\eta_{r}}}{(t_{c}-r)^{1/4}}.

The differential equation (
5.29
) can be solved directly by

(5.30)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}-\sqrt{\kappa_{t}+\eta_{t}}\gtrsim(t_{c}-s)^{3/4}-(t_{c}-t)^{3/4},\quad(\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s})-(\kappa_{t}+\eta_{t})\gtrsim\frac{(t-s)}{(t_{c}-s)^{1/4}}\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}.

We recall from (
4.15
), if
t_{c}-t\leqslant n^{6{\mathfrak{d}}-1/2}
,
I_{t}^{+}
is a single interval and the domain (
5.8
) is empty. For
t_{c}-t\geqslant n^{6{\mathfrak{d}}-1/2}\gg n^{9\mathfrak{d}/2-1/2}
we have

(5.31)

\displaystyle\tau\big(E(s),s\big)-\tau\big(E(t),t\big)=\frac{(t_{c}-s)^{1/6}-(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}}{n^{2/3-6{\mathfrak{d}}}}\ll\frac{(t-s)}{(t_{c}-s)^{1/4}}\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}.

We also notice that
\kappa_{t}\geqslant\tau\big(E(t),t\big)
.

Then, similarly to as in (
5.24
), it follows from combining (
5.30
) and (
5.31
) that

\displaystyle\dist(z_{s},I_{s}^{+})\geqslant\eta_{s}+\max\Big\{\kappa_{s}-\tau\big(E(s),s)\big),0\Big\}\gtrsim\eta_{s}+\frac{(t-s)}{(t_{c}-s)^{1/4}}\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}},

which gives (
5.14
).

We now address the third statement of the proposition. If
z_{t}\in\mathscr{D}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})
, from our definition (
5.5
), we have
z_{s}\in\mathscr{D}_{s}({\mathfrak{r}})
. If
z_{t}\in\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\rm L}
, from the definition of the characteristic flow (
3.9
), we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}\geqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{t}
and
f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})=f_{t}^{*}(z_{t})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle n\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})]}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}\bigg|\geqslant n\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{t}]\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}^{*}(z_{t})]}{f_{t}^{*}(z_{t})}\bigg|\geqslant\displaystyle\frac{n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}}}{2},

and thus
z_{s}\in\mathscr{D}_{s}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup\mathscr{D}_{s}^{\rm L}
. Finally, if
z_{t}\in\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\rm F}
, we show that
z_{s}\in\mathscr{D}_{s}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup\mathscr{D}_{s}^{\rm L}\cup\mathscr{D}_{s}^{\rm F}
. We discuss the case that
z_{t}
is in the domain (
5.7
), namely,
z_{t}=z_{t}(u)=E_{1}(t)-\kappa_{t}+\mathrm{i}\eta_{t}
with
\kappa_{t}>0
; the other cases follow from the same argument. If
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}\geqslant\mathfrak{c}
for some constant
\mathfrak{c}
, then we have
z_{s}\in\mathscr{D}_{s}({\mathfrak{r}})
; so, let us suppose that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}\leqslant\mathfrak{c}
; then set
z_{s}=E_{1}(s)-\kappa_{s}+\mathrm{i}\eta_{s}
.
Thanks to (
5.19
), we have that
\kappa_{s}\geqslant\kappa_{t}
, and
\dist(z_{s},I_{s}^{*})\geqslant\dist(z_{t},I_{t}^{*})
.
If
\eta_{s}\geqslant\kappa_{s}
, then

\displaystyle n\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})]}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}\bigg|\geqslant\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}n\sqrt{\dist(z,I_{s}^{*})\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]}\cdot\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})]}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}\bigg|\geqslant\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}n\sqrt{\dist(z_{t},I_{t}^{*})\Imaginary[z_{t}]}\cdot\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{\Imaginary[f_{t}^{*}(z_{t})]}{f_{t}^{*}(z_{t})}\bigg|\geqslant\displaystyle\frac{n^{2\mathfrak{d}}}{2},

which gives
z_{s}\in\mathscr{D}_{s}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup\mathscr{D}_{s}^{\rm L}
. If instead
\eta_{s}\leqslant\kappa_{s}
, then (
5.19
) gives that
\partial_{r}\kappa_{r}\lesssim-\sqrt{\eta_{r}+\kappa_{r}}\leqslant-\sqrt{\kappa_{r}}
for
s\leqslant r\leqslant t
. As in (
5.22
), we can solve this differential equation to obtain the bound

\displaystyle\kappa_{s}-\kappa_{t}\gtrsim(t-s)\sqrt{\kappa_{s}}\gtrsim(t-s)\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}\gg\tau\big(E(s),s\big)-\tau\big(E(t),t\big),

where we used (
5.23
) for the last inequality. Since
\kappa_{t}\geqslant\tau(E(t),t)
, it follows by rearranging that

\kappa_{s}\geqslant\tau\big(E(s),s\big)
. We conclude that
z_{s}\in\mathscr{D}_{s}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup\mathscr{D}_{s}^{\rm F}
.

It remains to establish the fourth statement of the proposition. From our construction of the stopping time (
5.12
) for
t\in[0,\sigma)\cap\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, uniformly for any
z\in{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm L}\cup\mathscr{D}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})
as defined in (
5.6
), we have

(5.32)

\displaystyle|\Delta_{t}(z)|\leqslant\frac{(\log n)n^{\mathfrak{d}}}{n\Imaginary z}.

Thanks to
Proposition

3.5
, we have always
|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f^{*}_{t}(z)]/f^{*}_{t}(z)|=|\sin(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\log f_{t}^{*}(z)])|\gtrsim|\sin(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{t}^{*}(z)])|
. Therefore,
{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm L}\cup\mathscr{D}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})
contains
z
satisfying
\Imaginary z\gtrsim n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}-1}/|\sin(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{t}^{*}(z)])|
; thus, (
5.32
) holds whenever
\Imaginary z\gtrsim n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}-1}/|\sin(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{t}^{*}(z)])|
. By a standard argument
[
HL19
, Corollary 3.2]
, this implies the first half of (
5.15
):
|H_{t}(x)-H_{t}^{*}(x)|\leqslant n^{3\mathfrak{d}}/n
.

For the second half of (
5.15
), we need to show that the intervals
\big\{x:{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\leqslant x\leqslant E_{1}(t)-\tau(E_{1}(t),t)\big\}
,
\big\{x:E_{2}(t)-\tau(E_{2}(t),t)\leqslant x\leqslant{\mathfrak{b}}(t)\big\}
and
\big\{x:E^{\prime}_{1}(t)+\tau(E^{\prime}_{1}(t),t)\leqslant x\leqslant E^{\prime}_{2}(t)+\tau(E^{\prime}_{2}(t),t)\}
are either void or saturated.

We first prove it for
{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\leqslant x\leqslant E_{1}(t)-\tau\big(E_{1}(t),t\big)
; the case
E_{2}(t)+\tau\big(E_{2}(t),t\big)\leqslant x\leqslant{\mathfrak{b}}(t)
follows from the same argument and will therefore be omitted. Let
E(t)=E_{1}(t)
. We recall
\tau\big(E(t),t\big)=n^{-2/3+6{\mathfrak{d}}}|t-t_{1}|^{2/3}\vee n^{-1+10{\mathfrak{d}}}
from (
4.13
) and
\big|E(t)-{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\big|\asymp(t-t_{1})^{2}
from the second statement of
Proposition

3.5
. Thus, if
|t-t_{1}|\leq\mathfrak{c}n^{5{\mathfrak{d}}-1/2}
for sufficiently small
\mathfrak{c}>0
, then
\big\{x:{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\leqslant x\leqslant E_{1}(t)-\tau(E_{1}(t),t)\big\}
is empty, so there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, for
|t-t_{1}|\geqslant\mathfrak{c}n^{5{\mathfrak{d}}-1/2}
, we fix
\mathfrak{b}=6\mathfrak{d}
, and we take
\kappa=n^{{\mathfrak{b}}-2/3}|t-t_{1}|^{2/3}\geqslant\tau\big(E(t),t\big)
and
\eta=n^{3{\mathfrak{b}}/13-2/3}|t-t_{1}|^{2/3}
. Using (
5.9
), we can check
E(t)-\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\rm F}\cup\mathscr{D}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})
. Thus, for
t\leqslant\sigma
, we have

(5.33)

\displaystyle\Big|m_{t}\big(E(t)-\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\big)-m^{*}_{t}\big(E(t)-\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\big)\Big|\lesssim\frac{n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}}}{n\sqrt{\eta(\kappa+\eta)}}.

Thanks to the square root behavior of
m^{*}_{t}(z)
from (
2
), if
t\leqslant t_{1}
, then
\big[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),E(t)\big]
is a void region, and

(5.34)

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}m_{t}^{*}\big(E(t)-\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\big)\asymp\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}\big(\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+(t_{1}-t)\big)}.

By our choice
\kappa=n^{{\mathfrak{b}}-2/3}|t-t_{1}|^{2/3}\geqslant\tau\big(E(t),t\big)
with
{\mathfrak{b}}\geqslant 6{\mathfrak{d}}
, and
\eta=n^{3{\mathfrak{b}}/13-2/3}|t-t_{1}|^{2/3}
, (
5.33
) and (
5.34
) imply that
-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{t}(E_{1}(t)-\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta)]\ll 1/n\eta
.
However, if there existed a particle
x_{i}(t)
such that
\big|x_{i}(t)-E(t)+\kappa\big|\leqslant\eta
, we would have
that

(5.35)

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\Big[m_{t}\big(E(t)-\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\big)\Big]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\eta}{\big(x_{i}(t)-\kappa-E(t)\big)^{2}+\eta^{2}}\geqslant\frac{1}{2n\eta}.

This leads to a contradiction, and we conclude that there is no particle on
\big[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),E(t)-\tau(E(t),t)\big]
.
If
t\geqslant t_{1}
, then
\big[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),E(t)\big]
is a saturated region, and (
2
) gives

(5.36)

\displaystyle\pi+\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\tilde{m}_{t}^{*}\big(E(t)-\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\big)\asymp\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}\big(\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+(t_{1}-t)\big)}.

With our choice
\kappa=n^{{\mathfrak{b}}-2/3}|t-t_{1}|^{2/3}
with
{\mathfrak{b}}\geqslant 6{\mathfrak{d}}
, and
\eta=n^{3{\mathfrak{b}}/13-2/3}|t-t_{1}|^{2/3}
, (
5.33
) and (
5.36
) implies that
\pi+\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\tilde{m}_{t}(E(t)-\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta)]\ll 1/n\eta
.
Similarly to (
5.35
), we conclude that
\big[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),E(t)-\tau(E(t),t)\big]
is fully packed.

For the interval
E^{\prime}_{1}(t)+\tau(E^{\prime}_{1}(t),t)\leqslant x\leqslant E^{\prime}_{2}(t)-\tau(E^{\prime}_{2}(t),t)
, we take
E(t)=E^{\prime}_{1}(t)
,
E^{\prime}(t)=E^{\prime}_{2}(t)
,
\kappa=n^{{\mathfrak{b}}-2/3}(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}\geqslant\tau\big(E(t),t\big)
with
{\mathfrak{b}}\geqslant 6{\mathfrak{d}}
, and
\eta=n^{3{\mathfrak{b}}/13-2/3}(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}
. Then using (
5.10
), we can check that
E(t)+\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\rm F}\cup\mathscr{D}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})
. Thus, for
t\leqslant\sigma
, we have

(5.37)

\displaystyle\Big|m_{t}\big(E(t)+\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\big)-m_{t}^{*}\big(E(t)+\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\big)\Big|\lesssim\frac{n^{2{\mathfrak{d}}}}{n\sqrt{\eta(\kappa+\eta)}}.

If
\big[E(t),E^{\prime}(t)\big]
is a void region, then (
3
) implies that

(5.38)

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\Big[m^{*}_{t}\big(E(t)+\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta\big)\Big]\lesssim\frac{\eta}{(t_{c}-t)^{1/4}\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}.

By our choice that
\kappa=n^{{\mathfrak{b}}-2/3}(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}
with
{\mathfrak{b}}\geqslant 6{\mathfrak{d}}
, and
\eta=n^{3{\mathfrak{b}}/13-2/3}{\mathfrak{b}}(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}
, (
5.37
) and (
5.38
) together imply that
-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{t}(E(t)+\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta)]\ll 1/n\eta
. Through the same reasoning as above, we conclude that there is no particle on
\big[E(t)+\tau(E(t),t),E^{\prime}(t)-\tau(E^{\prime}(t),t)\big]
. Similarly, if
\big[E(t),E^{\prime}(t)\big]
is a saturated region, by considering
\pi+\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{t}(E(t)-\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta)]
, we will have that
[E(t)+\tau(E(t),t),E^{\prime}(t)-\tau(E^{\prime}(t),t)]
is fully packed. This finishes the proof of (
5.15
).
∎

5.2.
Preliminary Results

In this section, we collect some preliminary estimates, which will be used in Sections
5.3
and
5.4
. We can reorganize the complex Burgers equation (
3.26
) as

(5.39)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}m^{*}_{t}(z)+\partial_{t}\log\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(z)+\partial_{z}\log\big({\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{t}(z)\big)=0,\quad{\mathcal{B}}_{t}^{*}(z)=\big(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1\big)\big(z-{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\big).

Since
\partial_{t}m^{*}_{t}(z)=\OO(1/z^{2})
as
z\rightarrow\infty
, we can do a contour integral on both sides of (
5.39
) to get rid of
\partial_{t}m^{*}_{t}(z)
, and express
\partial_{t}\log\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(z)
as

(5.40)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\log\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega+}\frac{\partial_{w}\log{\mathcal{B}}_{t}^{*}(w){\rm d}w}{w-z}=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega+}\frac{\log{\mathcal{B}}_{t}^{*}(w){\rm d}w}{(w-z)^{2}}.

where the contour
\omega+\subseteq\mathscr{D}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})
, which encloses
[\mathfrak{a}(t),\mathfrak{b}(t)]
and
z
. The second statement of
Proposition

3.4
implies that for
x>{\mathfrak{b}}(t)
or
x<{\mathfrak{a}}(t)
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{t}^{*}(x)=\big(f_{t}^{*}(x)+1\big)\big(x-{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\big)>0
. Moreover, we also have
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}^{*}(z)+1\big]<0
for
\Imaginary z>0
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}^{*}(z)+1\big]>0
for
\Imaginary z<0
. Therefore,
{\mathcal{B}}_{t}^{*}(z)\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus(-\infty,0]
on
\mathscr{U}_{t}\setminus[{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}]
, and
\log{\mathcal{B}}(z)
is well defined on
\mathscr{U}_{t}\setminus[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
. In what follows, we take the branch of the logarithm to be so that
\Imaginary\log u\in[-\pi,\pi)
.

For
m_{t}(z)
, we recall the empirical density
\rho(x;{\bm{x}}_{t})
, and corresponding height function
H_{t}(x)
from (
4.12
). By taking
\beta=H_{t}
, the variational problem (
3.12
) can be encoded by the complex slope
f(z;H_{t},t)
. Thanks to
Proposition

3.4
, we have the following decomposition

(5.41)

\displaystyle f(z;H_{t},t)=e^{m(z;H_{t},t)}g(z;H_{t},t)=e^{\tilde{m}_{t}(z)}\tilde{g}(z;H_{t},t),

where by our construction,
m(z;H_{t},t)=m_{t}(z)
;
\log\tilde{g}(z;H_{t},t)=\log g(z;H_{t},t)+\log(z-{\mathfrak{a}}(t))-\log({\mathfrak{b}}(t)-z)
is analytic in a neighborhood of
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
, and
\tilde{m}_{t}(z)=m_{t}(z)-\log\big(z-{\mathfrak{a}}(t)\big)+\log\big({\mathfrak{b}}(t)-z\big)
.
Similarly to (
5.39
), we also define

(5.42)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{t}(z)=(f(z;H_{t},t)+1)(z-{\mathfrak{a}}(t))=e^{m_{t}(z)}\varphi^{+}(z;H_{t},t)+\varphi^{-}(z;H_{t},t),

where
\varphi^{+}(z;H_{t},t),\varphi^{-}(z;H_{t},t)
are defined in (
4.11
).
For simplicity of notations, if the context is clear, we will simply write

(5.43)

\displaystyle f_{t}(z)=f(z;H_{t},t),\quad g_{t}(z)=g(z;H_{t},t),\quad\tilde{g}_{t}(z)=\tilde{g}(z;H_{t},t).

Observe that
f_{t}(z)
and
g_{t}(z)
defined above are random depending on the particle configuration
{\bm{x}}_{t}
through
H_{t}
, and that
f_{t}(z)
does not necessarily satisfy the complex Burgers equation (unlike
f_{t}^{*}(z)
).

In the following proposition, we collect some estimates of
f_{t}(z)
and
g_{t}(z)
, which will be used later to derive a dynamical equation for
m_{t}(z)
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Adopt the assumptions of
Proposition

4.4
, and recall the control parameter
{\mathfrak{M}}(t)
from (
5.11
). For
0\leqslant t\leqslant\sigma
as defined in (
5.12
), we have the following.

(1)

For
z
in the neighborhood
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}
of
[\mathfrak{a}(t),\mathfrak{b}(t)]
, we have

(5.44)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big|\partial^{k}_{z}\log g_{t}(z)-\partial^{k}_{z}\log g^{*}_{t}(z)\big|\lesssim\frac{{\mathfrak{M}}(t)}{n},\quad 0\leqslant k\leqslant 2,\\
&\log f_{t}(z)-\log f^{*}_{t}(z)=\Delta_{t}(z)+\OO\left(\frac{{\mathfrak{M}}(t)}{n}\right),\end{split}

it follows that
\big|\tilde{g}_{t}(z)\big|,\big|\partial_{z}\tilde{g}_{t}(z)\big|,\big|\partial^{2}_{z}\tilde{g}_{t}(z)\big|\asymp 1
in the neighborhood
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}
of
[\mathfrak{a}(t),\mathfrak{b}(t)]
.

(2)

For
z\in{\mathscr{D}}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm L}\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm F}
, the difference of the Stieltjes transform satisfies

(5.45)

\Delta_{t}(z)\lesssim n^{-{\mathfrak{d}}}\left|\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}f^{*}_{t}(z)}{f^{*}_{t}(z)}\right|\leqslant n^{-{\mathfrak{d}}};

the difference of the complex slopes satisfies

(5.46)

\left|\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}-\frac{f^{*}_{t}(z)}{f^{*}_{t}(z)+1}\right|\lesssim-n^{-{\mathfrak{d}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left[\frac{f_{t}^{*}(z)}{f_{t}^{*}(z)+1}\right]\lesssim n^{-{\mathfrak{d}}},

and it follows that

(5.47)

-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left[\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}\right]\lesssim-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left[\frac{f_{t}^{*}(z)}{f_{t}^{*}(z)+1}\right]\lesssim 1.

(3)

The function
f_{t}
defines a negative measure

\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x+0\mathrm{i})/(f_{t}(x+0\mathrm{i})+1)\big]
on
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
, whose support

(5.48)

\displaystyle I_{t}=\supp\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x+0\mathrm{i})\big]}{f_{t}(x+0\mathrm{i})+1}\bigg),

is contained in
I_{t}^{+}
,

(5.49)

\displaystyle I_{t}\subseteq I^{+}_{t}.

(4)

For
k\geqslant 1
, the
k
-th derivative of
f_{t}(z)/\big(f_{t}(z)+1\big)
satisfies

(5.50)

\displaystyle\left|\partial^{k}_{z}\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\dist(z,I_{t})^{k-1}}\cdot\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}\bigg|.

If we further assume that
z\in{\mathscr{D}}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm L}\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm F}
, then

(5.51)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z}\cdot\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}\bigg|\lesssim\frac{1}{\dist(z,I_{t}^{+})}.

Proof.

For
0\leqslant t\leqslant\sigma
, (
5.15
) implies that the two profiles
H_{t}(x)
and
H_{t}^{*}(x)
are close. In particular,
d(\partial_{x}H_{t}(x),\partial_{x}H_{t}^{*}(x))\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}
, and the assumptions in
Proposition

3.4
holds.
For the difference
\log g_{t}-\log g^{*}_{t}
, (
3.23
) gives for
z\in\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{z}^{k}\log g_{t}(z)-\partial_{z}^{k}\log g^{*}_{t}(z)\big|\lesssim\displaystyle\oint_{\omega}\big|m_{t}(w)-m^{*}_{t}(w)\big||{\rm d}w|\lesssim\frac{{\mathfrak{M}}(t)}{n},

where
\omega\subseteq\mathscr{D}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})
is a contour enclosing
\big[{\mathfrak{a}}(s),{\mathfrak{b}}(s)\big]
, and we used the fact that
|m_{t}(w)-m^{*}_{t}(w)|\leqslant{\mathfrak{M}}(t)/n
on
\mathscr{D}_{t}({\mathfrak{r}})
. Since
|\partial_{z}^{k}\tilde{g}_{t}^{*}(z)|\asymp 1
, we conclude that
|\partial_{z}^{k}\tilde{g}_{t}(z)|\asymp 1
in the neighborhood
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}
of
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
for
0\leqslant k\leqslant 2
.
Thanks to the decomposition (
3.21
), the difference
\log f_{t}-\log f^{*}_{t}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\log f_{t}(z)-\log f^{*}_{t}(z)&=\big(m_{t}(z)-m^{*}_{t}(z)\big)+\log g_{t}(z)-\log g^{*}_{t}(z)\\
&=\big(m_{t}(z)-m^{*}_{t}(z)\big)+\OO\left(\frac{{\mathfrak{M}}(t)}{n}\right)=\Delta_{t}(z)+\OO\left(\frac{{\mathfrak{M}}(t)}{n}\right).\end{split}

This finishes the proof of (
5.44
).

The estimate (
5.45
) follows from the definition of the spectral domains
{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm L},{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\rm F}
from (
5.6
), (
5.7
) and (
5.8
), and of the stopping time
\sigma
from (
5.12
).
The estimate (
5.46
) follows from (
5.44
), (
5.45
) and (
3.28
),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left|\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}-\frac{f^{*}_{t}(z)}{f^{*}_{t}(z)+1}\right|&\lesssim\Bigg(\big|\Delta_{t}(z)\big|+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{M}(t)}{n}\bigg)\Bigg)\frac{\big|f_{t}^{*}(z)\big|}{\big|f_{t}^{*}(z)+1\big|^{2}}\\
&\leqslant\frac{n^{-{\mathfrak{d}}}|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}^{*}(z)]|}{|f_{t}^{*}(z)+1|^{2}}=-n^{-{\mathfrak{d}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left[\frac{f_{t}^{*}(z)}{f_{t}^{*}(z)+1}\right]\lesssim n^{-{\mathfrak{d}}}.\end{aligned}

For
\Imaginary z\geqslant 0
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(z)/(f_{t}(z)+1)\big]=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}(z)]/\big|f_{t}(z)+1\big|^{2}\leqslant 0
; therefore,
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x+0\mathrm{i})/(f_{t}(x+0\mathrm{i})+1)\big]
defines a negative measure on
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
. Thanks to (
5.15
) and the decomposition
\log f_{t}(z)=m_{t}(z)+\log g_{t}(z)
, for
x\in[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]\setminus I_{t}^{+}
we have
m_{t}(x)\in{\mathbb{R}}
and thus
f_{t}(x)\in{\mathbb{R}}
. By the third estimate of (
5.44
), we have
\big|f_{t}^{*}(x)\big|^{-1}\big|f_{t}(x)-f_{t}^{*}(x)\big|=\big|m_{t}(x)-m_{t}^{*}(x)\big|+\OO\big({\mathfrak{M}}(t)/n\big)
for
x\in[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]\setminus I_{t}^{+}
.
Moreover, through essentially the same argument as for the fourth statement of
Proposition

5.2
, we have
\big|m_{t}(x)-m_{t}^{*}(x)\big|=\oo(1)
for
x\in[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]\setminus I_{t}^{+}
. We conclude that
\big|f_{t}^{*}(x)\big|^{-1}\big|f_{t}(x)-f_{t}^{*}(x)\big|=\oo(1)
for
x\in[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]\setminus I_{t}^{+}
. By our assumption,
f_{t}^{*}(x)
is bounded away from
-1
; see (
3.28
). We conclude that
f_{t}(x)\neq-1
for
x\in[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]\setminus I_{t}^{+}
. Therefore, on
x\in[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]\setminus I_{t}^{+}
,
f_{t}(x+0\mathrm{i})/\big(f_{t}(x+0\mathrm{i})+1\big)\in{\mathbb{R}}
and does not have any poles. Therefore, its imaginary part vanishes outside
I_{t}^{+}
, i.e.
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x+0\mathrm{i})/(f_{t}(x+0\mathrm{i})+1)\big]=0
, and the claim (
5.49
) follows.

We can recover the singular part of
f_{t}(z)/\big(f_{t}(z)+1\big)
from the measure
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)/(f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)+1)\big]
. In particular, since for almost every
E\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle\Imaginary\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{f_{t}(E)}{f_{t}(E)+1}\bigg]=\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0}\Imaginary\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_{I_{t}}\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)/(f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)+1)\big]\mathrm{d}x}{x-E-\mathrm{i}\eta}\bigg],

the Schwarz reflection principle implies that

\displaystyle\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{I_{t}}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)/(f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)+1)\big]{\rm d}x}{x-z},

is a real analytic function in
z
on a neighborhood of
I_{t}
. Observe that, when
z
is bounded away from
I_{t}
, this analytic function is bounded. Thus, by the maximum principle, it is uniformly bounded in a neighborhood of
I_{t}
, and we get

(5.52)

\displaystyle\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{I_{t}}\frac{\Imaginary\big[f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)/(f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)+1)\big]{\rm d}x}{x-z}+\OO(1),

where the
\OO(1)
error and its derivatives are uniformly bounded in a neighborhood of
I_{t}
.
Using (
5.52
), we have the following bounds on the derivatives of
f_{t}(z)/(f_{t}(z)+1)
, for any
k\geqslant 1

(5.53)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|\partial^{k}_{z}\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}\right|&=\left|\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{I_{t}}\frac{k!\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)/(f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)+1)\big]{\rm d}x}{(x-z)^{k+1}}\right|+\OO(1)\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{\dist(z,I_{t})^{k-1}}\left|\int_{I_{t}}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)/(f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)+1)\big]{\rm d}x}{|x-z|^{2}}\right|+\OO(1)\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{\dist(z,I_{t})^{k-1}}\left(\frac{\big|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}(z)/(f_{t}(z)+1)]\big|}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]}+\OO(1)\right)+\OO(1)\lesssim\frac{\big|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}(z)/(f_{t}(z)+1)]\big|}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\dist(z,I_{t})^{k-1}},\end{split}

where we used (
3.20
) that
|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}(z)/(f_{t}(z)+1)]|/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\gtrsim 1
for the last inequality.

If
\dist(z,I_{t})\leqslant 2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z
, then (
5.51
) follows from (
5.47
). Otherwise if
\dist(z,I_{t})\geqslant 2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z
, we will use (
5.52
). Since the
\OO(1)
error is a real analytic function, its imaginary part is bounded by
\OO(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z)
. By taking imaginary part, and dividing by
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z
, we get

(5.54)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Imaginary z}\cdot\bigg|\Imaginary\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}\bigg|&\lesssim\int_{I_{t}}\frac{-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)/(f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)+1)\big]{\rm d}x}{|x-z|^{2}}+\OO(1).\end{split}

If
\dist(z,I_{t})\geqslant 2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z
, then for any
x\in I_{t}
,
|\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z-x|\gtrsim\dist(z,I_{t})
.
Denoting
z^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z+\mathrm{i}\dist(z,I_{t})
, we have
|x-z|^{2}=|\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z-x|^{2}+\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]^{2}\gtrsim|\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z-x|^{2}+\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z^{\prime}]^{2}=|x-z^{\prime}|^{2}
. Moreover, we also have that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z}\cdot\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}\bigg|&\lesssim\int_{I_{t}}\frac{-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)/(f_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}0)+1)\big]{\rm d}x}{|x-z^{\prime}|^{2}}+\OO(1)\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{\Imaginary z^{\prime}}\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{t}(z^{\prime})}{f_{t}(z^{\prime})+1}\bigg|+\OO(1)\lesssim\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\dist(z,I_{t})}.\end{split}

This finishes the proof of (
5.51
).
∎

We define the following lattice on the domain
{\mathscr{D}}_{0}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{0}^{\rm L}\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{0}^{\rm F}
,

(5.55)

\mathscr{L}=\mathscr{L}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\big\{z_{t}^{-1}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)\in{\mathscr{D}}_{0}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{0}^{\rm L}\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{0}^{\rm F}:E\in\mathbb{Z}/n^{3},\eta\in\mathbb{Z}/n^{3}\big\}.

From the characteristic flow (
3.9
), as a function of
u\in{\mathscr{D}}_{0}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{0}^{\rm L}\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{0}^{\rm F}
, we have that
z_{t}(u)
is Lipschitz with Lipschitz constant at most
\OO(n)
. Indeed, using (
3.10
), (
5.50
) and (
5.51
) we have

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{u}z_{t}(u)\big|\lesssim 1+t\left|\partial_{u}\frac{f_{0}^{*}(u)}{f_{0}^{*}(u)+1}\right|\lesssim 1+\frac{t}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}u}\cdot\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{0}^{*}(u)}{f_{0}^{*}(u)+1}\bigg|\lesssim\frac{1}{\dist(u,I_{t}^{*})}\lesssim n.

Thus the image of the lattice
{\mathscr{L}}
gives a
\OO(1/n^{2})
mesh of
z_{t}\big({\mathscr{D}}_{0}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{0}^{\rm L}\cup{\mathscr{D}}^{\rm F}_{0}\big)
. In particular we have: for any
0\leqslant t\leqslant 1
and
w\in z_{t}\big({\mathscr{D}}_{0}({\mathfrak{r}})\cup{\mathscr{D}}_{0}^{\rm L}\cup{\mathscr{D}}^{\rm F}_{0}\big)
, there exists some lattice point
u=u(w)\in\mathscr{L}
as in (
5.55
), such that

(5.56)

\displaystyle\big|z_{t}(u)-w\big|\lesssim 1/n^{2}.

In the following proposition we collect some estimates on various integrals, which we will use later.

Proposition 5.4
.

Adopt the assumptions of
Proposition

4.4
and fix
0\leqslant t\leqslant\sigma
(recall from (
5.12
)). If
z_{t}=z_{t}(u)\in\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\rm L}
, then

(5.57)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})+1}\bigg|\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]^{k+1}}\lesssim\frac{(\log n)^{\bm{1}(k=0)}}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{t}]^{k}}.

If
z_{t}=z_{t}(u)\in\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\rm F}
, then

(5.58)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})+1}\bigg|\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]\dist(z_{s},I^{*}_{s})}}\lesssim\frac{\log n\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{t}]}}{\sqrt{\dist(z_{t},I^{*}_{t})}};

(5.59)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})+1}\bigg|\cdot\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}s}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]\dist(z_{s},I^{+}_{s})}\lesssim\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{t}]\dist(z_{t},I^{*}_{t})}};

(5.60)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})+1}\bigg|\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]\dist(z_{s},I^{+}_{s})^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{t}]\dist(z_{t},I^{*}_{t})}.

Proof.

We recall from the characteristic flow (
3.9
),
\big|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f^{*}_{s}(z_{s})/(f^{*}_{s}(z_{s})+1)]\big|=-\partial_{s}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}
. We begin by establishing (
5.57
). For
k=0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})+1}\bigg|\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{s}}=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{-\partial_{s}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]}{\rm d}s=\log\frac{\Imaginary z_{0}}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{t}}\lesssim\log n,

where in the last bound we used the fact that
n^{-1}\ll\Imaginary z_{s}\ll n
for each
s\in[0,t]
. For
k\geqslant 1
, we have

(5.61)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})+1}\bigg|\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]^{k+1}}=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{-\partial_{s}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]^{k+1}}{\rm d}s\lesssim\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{t}]^{k}}.

This finishes the proof of (
5.57
).

The proofs of (
5.58
), (
5.59
) and (
5.60
) are all very similar, and therefore we only establish the third. We must consider two cases, whether
z_{t}
is in the domain (
5.7
) or in the domain (
5.8
). For
z_{t}
in the domain (
5.7
), we will use the square root behavior (
3.29
) to bound
\big|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f^{*}_{s}(z_{s})/(f^{*}_{s}(z_{s})+1)]\big|
, and (
5.13
) to lower bound
\dist(z_{s},I_{s}^{+})
. For
z_{t}
in the domain (
5.8
), we use the square root behavior (
3.38
) to bound
\big|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f^{*}_{s}(z_{s})/(f^{*}_{s}(z_{s})+1)]\big|
, and (
5.14
) to lower bound
\dist(z_{s},I_{s}^{+})
.

For
z_{t}
in the domain (
5.7
), we only prove (
5.60
) when
z_{t}
is closer to the left boundary
E_{1}(t)
, since the proof is entirely analogous when it is closer to the right boundary
E_{2}(t)
. Thus, we denote
z_{t}=E(t)-\kappa_{t}+\mathrm{i}\eta_{t}
where
E(t)=E_{1}(t)
and
\kappa_{t}>0
. Let
z_{s}=E(s)-\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}
for any
0\leqslant s\leqslant t
. Fix small
{\mathfrak{c}}>0
and let
r=\sup\{0\leqslant s\leqslant t:\dist(z_{s},E(s))\geqslant{\mathfrak{c}}\}
. We decompose the integral (
5.60
) into two integrals corresponding to
0\leqslant s\leqslant r
and
r\leqslant s\leqslant t
. For
0\leqslant s\leqslant r
, we have
\dist(z_{s},E_{s})\gtrsim 1
. By the same argument as for (
5.57
), we have

(5.62)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})+1}\bigg|\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]\dist(z_{s},I^{+}_{s})^{2}}\lesssim 1\lesssim\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{t}]\dist(z_{t},I^{*}_{t})}.

For
r\leqslant s\leqslant t
, we have
\dist(z_{s},E_{s})\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}
, then the square root behavior (
3.29
) gives

(5.63)

\displaystyle\frac{\eta_{s}}{\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}}\asymp\bigg|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{s}(z_{s})}{f^{*}_{s}(z_{s})+1}\bigg|=\bigg|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}_{t}(z_{t})}{f^{*}_{t}(z_{t})+1}\bigg|\asymp\frac{\eta_{t}}{\sqrt{\kappa_{t}+\eta_{t}}},

We can bound the integral (
5.60
) as

(5.64)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{r}^{t}\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})+1}\bigg|\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]\dist(z_{s},I^{+}_{s})^{2}}&\lesssim\int_{r}^{t}\bigg|\Imaginary\displaystyle\frac{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})}{f_{s}^{*}(z_{s})+1}\bigg|\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\eta_{s}\big((\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s})^{1/2}(t-s)+\eta_{s}\big)^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\int_{r}^{t}\frac{\eta_{s}/{\sqrt{\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}}}}{\eta_{s}\big((\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s})^{1/2}(t-s)+\eta_{s}\big)^{2}}{\rm d}s\\
&\lesssim\int_{r}^{t}\frac{{\rm d}s}{(\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s})^{3/2}\big(t-s+\eta_{s}/(\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s})^{1/2}\big)^{2}}\\
&\leqslant\int_{r}^{t}\frac{{\rm d}s}{(\kappa_{t}+\eta_{t})^{3/2}\big(t-s+\eta_{t}/(\kappa_{t}+\eta_{t})^{1/2}\big)^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\eta_{t}(\kappa_{t}+\eta_{t})},\end{split}

where in the first line we used (
5.13
); in the last two lines we used
(
5.63
)
and
\kappa_{s}+\eta_{s}\geqslant\kappa_{t}+\eta_{t}
from (
5.22
). The claim (
5.60
) follows from combining (
5.62
) and (
5.64
).

For the second case when
z_{t}
is in the domain (
5.8
) we again only analyze the integral when
z_{t}
is closer to
E^{\prime}_{1}(t)
(as the proof is entirely analogous when
z_{t}
is closer to
E_{2}^{\prime}(t)
). We denote
E(t)=E^{\prime}_{1}(t)
. Since
E_{2}^{\prime}(t)-E_{1}^{\prime}(t)\lesssim(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}
, we have
z_{t}=E(t)+\kappa_{t}+\mathrm{i}\eta_{t}
with
0<\kappa_{t}\lesssim(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}
. If
\eta_{t}\gtrsim(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}\gtrsim\kappa_{t}
, then (
5.61
) gives (
5.60
). Thus, we further restrict ourselves to the case
\eta_{t}\lesssim(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}
. Let
z_{s}=E(s)+\kappa_{s}+\mathrm{i}\eta_{s}
for any
0\leqslant s\leqslant t
. Fix small
{\mathfrak{c}}>0
and let
r=\sup\{0\leqslant s\leqslant t:\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{s}]\geqslant{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{c}-s)^{3/2}\}
