around a cusp location, connecting two points on
t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-\delta
, and
(X,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}),(X^{\prime},{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
are inside the liquid region between
\mathscr{C}
and
t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-\delta
.
Shown to the right is the curve
\mathscr{C}
around a edge point
(E({\mathfrak{s}}),{\mathfrak{s}})
, connecting one point on
t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-\delta
and one point on the arctic curve, and
(X,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}),(X^{\prime},{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
are inside the liquid region between
\mathscr{C}
and
t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-\delta
.
.

Let
f_{c}=f_{t_{c}}(E_{c})
, and denote the map

(A.19)

\displaystyle(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{P}})\mapsto(f,w)=\left(f_{t}(x),x+(t_{c}-t)\frac{f_{t}(x)}{f_{t}(x)+1}\right).

Here,
w=w(x,t)
is a function of
x,t
; if the context is clear we will simply write it as
w
.
We can interpret
f=f_{t_{c}}(w)
as a function of
w
. We will soon see that after restriction to a fan-shaped neighborhood of
E_{c}
,
f_{t_{c}}(w)
is a single valued function.
With the above notations, locally around
(f_{c},E_{c})
we can rewrite (
A.12
) as

(A.20)

\displaystyle Q_{1}(f):=\frac{Q_{0}(f)}{f+1}+\frac{t_{c}f}{f+1}=w.

Since
(E_{c},t_{c})
is a cusp of the arctic boundary for
\mathfrak{P}
, it is characterized by the equations

\displaystyle Q_{0}(f_{c})=E_{c}(f_{c}+1)-t_{c}f_{c};\qquad Q_{0}^{\prime}(f_{c})=E_{c}-t_{c};\qquad Q_{0}^{\prime\prime}(f_{c})=0.

We also have that
Q_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(f_{c})\asymp 1
, since
Q_{0}
does not admit any quadruple roots by
Proposition

A.2
. It follows from (
A.20
) (and the fact that no cusp is simultaneously a tangency location) that

\displaystyle Q_{1}(f_{c})=E_{c};\qquad Q_{1}^{\prime}(f_{c})=0;\qquad Q_{1}^{\prime\prime}(f_{c})=0;\qquad|Q_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(f_{c})|\asymp 1.

This, together with (
A.20
), implies that, locally around
E_{c}
,
f=f_{t_{c}}(w)
has cube root behavior, namely,

(A.21)

\displaystyle f_{t_{c}}(w)=f_{c}+\sqrt[3]{C(w)(w-E_{c}}),

for some real analytic function
C(w)
. Let us discuss the choice for the cube root in (
A.21
); recall that in (
A.19
), we must have
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}f=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}(x)]<0
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}w=(t_{c}-t)\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\big[f_{t}(x)/(f_{t}(x)+1)\big]>0
. Therefore, for
t>t_{c}
in (
A.19
) (here we used that the cusp points upward), its image corresponds to the branch of
f_{t_{c}}(w)
in the lower half plane. Also
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t_{c}}(x)]=-\pi\partial_{x}H_{t_{c}}(x)<0
for
x\in(E_{c}-\varepsilon,E_{c})\cup(E_{c},E_{c}+\varepsilon)
for some small
\varepsilon>0
. Thus the branch of
f_{t_{c}}(w)
has to be the one in the lower half plane corresponding to the fan-shaped region with argument
(-2\pi/3,-\pi/3)
; See Figure
18
. Then
f_{t_{c}}(w)
is a single valued function restricted to a neighborhood of
E_{c}
after removing the branch cut.

Next we consider the preimage of (
A.19
) for
\big(f_{t_{c}}(w),w\big)
with
w
on the circle
w=E_{c}+{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
, where
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
is chosen so that
f(w)
is single valued on this circle (and
{\mathfrak{c}}
chosen to be smaller than
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
). Since
\big|f_{t}(x)/(f_{t}(x)+1)\big|\leqslant{\mathfrak{C}}_{0}
by (
A.14
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}=|w-E_{c}|=\left|x-E_{c}+(t_{c}-t)\frac{f_{t}(x)}{f_{t}(x)+1}\right|\leqslant{\mathfrak{C}}_{0}\big(|x-E_{c}|+|t_{c}-t|\big).

Thus
(x,t)
is bounded away from
(E_{c},t_{c})
, with
\dist\big((x,t),(E_{c},t_{c})\big)\geqslant{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}/2{\mathfrak{C}}_{0}
. Therefore, if we take
{\mathfrak{c}}\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}/4{\mathfrak{C}}_{0}
, then by (
A.17
)
(X,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
and
(X^{\prime},{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
will be inside the preimage curve. For
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]\leqslant-{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}/2
, the corresponding
(x,t)
satisfies

\displaystyle t_{c}-t=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f/(f+1)]}=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f]}|f+1|^{2}\geqslant\frac{{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}}{2{\mathfrak{C}}_{0}^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\sqrt[3]{C(w){\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}}]}\geqslant\frac{{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}}{C{\mathfrak{C}}_{0}^{2}},

for some large constant
C
depending on
C(w)
. If we take
\delta\leqslant c({\mathfrak{P}})\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}/C{\mathfrak{C}}_{0}^{2}
, then the preimage will intersect
t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-\delta
. This gives the desired curve
\mathscr{C}
; see Figure
18
.

Next we consider the image of
\mathscr{C}
under the map (
A.18
)

(A.22)

\displaystyle(x,t)\in\mathscr{C}\mapsto x+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-\delta-t)\frac{f_{t}(x)}{f_{t}(x)+1}=w-(t_{c}-{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}+\delta)\frac{f_{t_{c}}(w)}{f_{t_{c}}(w)+1},

where
w=w(x,t)
is from (
A.19
). For
(x,t)\in\mathscr{C}
,
w
is on the circle
w=E_{c}+{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
. Since it is bounded away from
E_{c}
, namely
|w-E_{c}|={\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{w}\frac{f_{t_{c}}(w)}{f_{t_{c}}(w)+1}\right|\leqslant\frac{\partial_{w}f_{t_{c}}(w)}{|f_{t_{c}}(w)+1|^{2}}\leqslant{\mathfrak{C}}_{0}^{2}\big|\partial_{w}\sqrt[3]{C(w)(w-E_{c})}\big|\leqslant C{\mathfrak{C}}_{0}^{2}({\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})^{-2/3},

where the constant
C
depends on
C(w)
.
Therefore, if we take
|t_{c}-{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}+\delta|\leqslant{\mathfrak{c}}+c({\mathfrak{P}})
much smaller than
({\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})^{2/3}/C{\mathfrak{C}}_{0}^{2}
then, by the implicit function theorem, the map

\displaystyle w\mapsto w-(t_{c}-{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}+\delta)\frac{f_{t_{c}}(w)}{f_{t_{c}}(w)+1},

is a bijection from the curve
\big\{w(x,t):(x,t)\in\mathscr{C}\big\}
onto its image. We conclude that the map (
A.18
) is one-to-one after restriction to
\mathscr{C}
. Thus, the map (
A.18
) is a bijection.

t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-\delta
t=t_{c}
\mathscr{C}
(x,t)
\mapsto
w=w(x,t)=E_{c}+{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
E_{c}
\mapsto
f_{t_{c}}(w)=f_{c}+\sqrt[3]{C(w)(w-E_{c})}

Figure 18.
For
t>t_{c}
in (
A.19
) (dark shaded area), its image satisfies
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]>0
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t_{c}}(w)]<0
. It must be the branch of
f_{t_{c}}(w)
in the lower half plane. After restriction to this branch, the preimage of the circle
w=E_{c}+{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
, is bounded away from
(E_{c},t_{c})
and intersects
t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-\delta
. The preimage between two intersections with
t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-\delta
gives the desired curve
\mathscr{C}
.

Next we consider the case when
(X,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
and
(X^{\prime},{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
are close to a point
\big(E({\mathfrak{s}}),{\mathfrak{s}}\big)
(which is bounded away from a cusp location) on the arctic curve, namely,
\dist\big(\big\{(y,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}):X\leqslant y\leqslant X^{\prime}\big\},(E({\mathfrak{s}}),{\mathfrak{s}})\big)\leqslant 2{\mathfrak{c}}
. Then we can construct a curve
\mathscr{C}
connecting a point on the horizontal level
t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-\delta
to a point on the arctic curve, such that
(X,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
and
(X^{\prime},{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
are inside the liquid region
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{P}})
between
\mathscr{C}
and
t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-\delta
. We denote this region by
\mathscr{D}
; see Figure
17
. We can consider the same map (
A.19
), by replacing the cusp location
(E_{c},t_{c})
by
\big(E({\mathfrak{s}}),{\mathfrak{s}}\big)
. Instead of cube root behavior (
A.21
),
f_{{\mathfrak{s}}}(w)
has square root behavior
f_{\mathfrak{s}}(w)=f_{\mathfrak{s}}(w)+\sqrt{C(w)\big(w-E(\mathfrak{s})\big)}
in a neighborhood of
E({\mathfrak{s}})
. Then, by following essentially the same argument as used in the cusp case, we will have that the map (
A.18
) is also a bijection on
\mathscr{D}
.

∎

Appendix B
Initial Estimates

In this section we study the image of the map (
3.5
), and prove Proposition
3.4
for the special case
\beta=H_{t}^{*}
. This will serve as the initial estimates for the general case by a deformation argument.

By Item (e) in the third statement of our
Assumption

2.4
, the projection map (
3.5
) is a bijection. Together with its complex conjugate, it maps two copies of the liquid region
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\cap{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
gluing along the south boundary and arctic curve to a domain
\mathscr{D}_{0}
. We can interpret
f
in (
3.4
) as a function of
z\in\mathscr{D}_{0}
as
f=f_{t}^{*}(z)
.
The north boundary
I_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}^{*}=[E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}),E_{2}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})]
of
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\cap{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
is a single interval, it is mapped to a curve

(B.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}^{*}(x):=x-({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\frac{f_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}^{*}(x)}{f_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}^{*}(x)+1},\quad x\in I_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}^{*}.

We denote the contour
\mathscr{C}_{0}:=\big\{\mathscr{C}^{*}(x)\cup\overline{\mathscr{C}^{*}(x)}:x\in I_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}^{*}\big\}
, which is also the boundary curve of
\mathscr{D}_{0}
.

Proposition B.1
.

The contour
\mathscr{C}_{0}
defined as in (
B.1
) is an analytic Jordan curve. There exists a closed set
\mathscr{A}_{0}
containing an annulus neighborhood of
\mathscr{C}_{0}
(shrinking
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
if necessary) such that the following holds for
z\in\mathscr{A}_{0}

(B.2)

\displaystyle 1/{\mathfrak{C}}\leqslant|f_{t}^{*}(z)|\leqslant{\mathfrak{C}},\quad|f_{t}^{*}(z)+1|\geqslant 1/{\mathfrak{C}},\quad|\partial_{z}f_{t}^{*}(z)|\leqslant{\mathfrak{C}}

and
1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f_{t}^{*}(z)/(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1))=0
has two zeros (counting multiplicity) at
z=\mathscr{C}^{*}(E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})),\mathscr{C}^{*}(E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

By shrinking
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
if necessary, we can extend
f^{*}_{t}(z)
to a neighborhood of the contour
\mathscr{C}_{0}
. Moreover, we can also choose the time slice
t={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
bounded away from tangency locations. Thus by taking
\mathscr{A}_{0}
the closure of a sufficiently small annulus neighborhood of
\mathscr{C}_{0}
, (
B.2
) holds.

Locally around
(f_{r}^{*}(x),x)
for
(x,r)\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\cap{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
there exists an analytic function
Q_{0}
such that

(B.3)

\displaystyle Q_{0}(f_{r}^{*}(x))=x(f_{r}^{*}(x)+1)-rf_{r}^{*}(x).

Using (
3.4
), we can rewrite (
B.3
) in terms of
(f_{t}^{*}(z),z)
,

(B.4)

\displaystyle Q_{0}(f_{t}^{*}(z))=x(f_{r}^{*}(x)+1)-rf_{r}^{*}(x)=z(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1)-tf_{t}^{*}(z).

By Item (d) in the third statement of
Assumption

2.4
,
the two end points
E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}),E_{2}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
of the
I_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}^{*}
are characterized by
\partial_{f}Q_{0}(f_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}^{*}(E_{i}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})))=E_{i}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})-{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. Since they are not cusp locations, we have
\partial^{2}_{f}Q_{0}(f_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}^{*}(E_{i}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})))\neq 0
. Using the relation (
B.4
), we can write the derivatives of
Q_{0}
in terms of the derivatives of
f_{t}^{*}(z)
. By taking derivatives with respect to
z
on both sides of (
B.4
), and rearranging, we get

(B.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{f}Q_{0}(f_{t}^{*}(z))=z-t+\frac{f_{t}^{*}(z)+1}{\partial_{z}f_{t}^{*}(z)},\quad\partial^{2}_{f}Q_{0}(f_{t}^{*}(z))=-\frac{(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1)^{3}}{(\partial_{z}f_{t}^{*}(z))^{3}}\partial_{z}^{2}\left(\frac{f_{t}^{*}(z)}{f_{t}^{*}(z)+1}\right).

For
z=\mathscr{C}^{*}(x)=x+(t-{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})f_{t}^{*}(z)/(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1)
for some
x\in I_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime*}
, we can rewrite the first relation in (
B.5
) as

(B.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{f}Q_{0}(f_{t}^{*}(z))=\partial_{f}Q_{0}(f_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}^{*}(x))=(x-{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{f_{t}^{*}(z)+1}\left({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t+\frac{1}{\partial_{z}(f_{t}^{*}(z)/(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1))}\right).

The second term on the righthand side of (
B.6
) vanishes only if
x
is one of the end points
E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}),E_{2}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
.
Thus we conclude that along the contour
\mathscr{C}_{0}
,
1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f_{t}^{*}(z)/(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1))=0
only at
z=\mathscr{C}^{*}(E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})),\mathscr{C}^{*}(E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}))
. Since
(E_{i}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}),{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
are not cusp locations, the second relation in (
B.5
) implies that
\partial_{z}^{2}(f_{t}^{*}(z)/(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1))\neq 0
at these two points. By taking
\mathscr{A}_{0}
the closure of a sufficiently small annulus neighborhood of
\mathscr{C}_{0}
,
1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f_{t}^{*}(z)/(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1))=0
has two zeros (counting multiplicity) at
z=\mathscr{C}^{*}(E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})),\mathscr{C}^{*}(E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}))
.

Next we show the contour
\mathscr{C}_{0}
is real analytic.
In fact, from the defining relation (
B.1
), the contour
\mathscr{C}_{0}
can also be constructed from
f_{t}^{*}(z)
as

\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}^{*}(w)/(f_{t}^{*}(w)+1)]=0\}
. For
z=\mathscr{C}^{*}(x)
and
x\neq E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}),E_{2}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
, we have
\partial_{z}(z+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)(f_{t}^{*}(z)/(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1)))\neq 0
. Analytic implicit function theorem implies that
\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}^{*}(w)/(f_{t}^{*}(w)+1)]=0\}
is analytic around
w=z
. Next we discuss the case
z=\mathscr{C}^{*}(E_{i}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}))
, with
i\in\{1,2\}
. By Taylor expansion around
z
,
f_{t}^{*}(w)/(f_{t}^{*}(w)+1)=f_{t}^{*}(z)/(f_{t}^{*}(z)+1)-(w-z)/({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)+a_{2}(w-z)^{2}+a_{3}(w-z)^{3}\cdots
, where
a_{2}\neq 0
. Let
w=z+u+\mathrm{i}v
. In a neighborhood of
z
, the contour
\mathscr{C}_{0}
is characterized by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}^{*}(w)/(f_{t}^{*}(w)+1)]}{({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]}=2a_{2}u+a_{3}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[(u+\mathrm{i}v)^{2}]}{v}+\cdots=0,

where by diving
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]
, we remove the trivial solution that
w\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
a_{2}\neq 0
, the derivative with respect to
u
is nonzero. The analytic implicit function theorem gives that
\mathscr{C}_{0}
is real analytic around
x=E_{i}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
.
∎

Proposition B.2
.

Proposition
3.4
holds for
\beta=H_{t}^{*}
.

Proof.

For
\beta=H_{t}^{*}
, the first statement in
Proposition

3.4
follows from the third statement of
Assumption

2.4
.

\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}:=\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}^{H_{t}^{*}}=\mathscr{D}_{0}
from the discussion above (
B.1
), and

\mathscr{U}_{t}:=\mathscr{U}_{t}^{H_{t}^{*}}
is the regime from
\mathscr{D}_{0}
by removing the interval
I_{t}^{*}=\{x:(x,t)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\}
. Moreover,
f_{t}^{*}(z)=f(z;H_{t}^{*},t)
is analytic on
\mathscr{U}_{t}
.

Next, we show that for
x\in\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
,

(B.7)

\displaystyle\arg^{*}f^{*}_{t}(x+0\mathrm{i})=-\pi(\partial_{x}H^{*}_{t}(x)+\bm{1}_{(-\infty,{\mathfrak{a}}(t)]}(x)+\bm{1}_{({\mathfrak{b}}(t),\infty]}(x)).

For south boundary of
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\cap{\mathfrak{D}}^{t}
,
x\in I_{t}^{*}=\{x:(x,t)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\}
, the argument of the complex slope
f^{*}_{t}(x)
is given explicitly by the defining relation (
2.7
),

(B.8)

\displaystyle\arg^{*}f_{t}^{*}(x+0\mathrm{i})=\arg^{*}f^{*}_{t}(x)=-\pi\partial_{x}H_{t}^{*}(x).

For
z\in(\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}\cap{\mathbb{R}})\setminus I_{t}^{*}
, it corresponds to a point on the arctic curve
(x,r)\in\partial\overline{{\mathfrak{L}}({{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}})}
with
r\in(t,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
, given by (
A.10
); See Figure
15
,

(B.9)

\displaystyle f^{*}_{t}(z)=f^{*}_{r}(x),\quad\frac{r-t}{x-z}=\frac{f^{*}_{r}(x)+1}{f^{*}_{r}(x)}.

Since the segment from
(z,t)
to
(x,r)
stays in the frozen region (we interpret the region on the left of
\partial_{\rm we}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t})
a frozen region consisting of lozenges of type
2
, and the region on the right of
\partial_{\rm es}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t})
a frozen region consisting of lozenges of type
3
), there are several cases.
If the frozen region consists of type 1 lozenges, then
\partial_{x}H^{*}_{t}(z)+\bm{1}_{(-\infty,{\mathfrak{a}}(t)]}(z)+\bm{1}_{({\mathfrak{b}}(t),\infty]}(z)=0
and
f^{*}_{t}(z)=f^{*}_{r}(x)\in[0,+\infty]
;
If the frozen region consists of type 2 lozenges, then
\partial_{x}H^{*}_{t}(z)+\bm{1}_{(-\infty,{\mathfrak{a}}(t)]}(z)+\bm{1}_{({\mathfrak{b}}(t),\infty]}(z)=1
and
f^{*}_{t}(z)=f^{*}_{r}(x)\in[-\infty,-1]
;
If the frozen region consists of type 3 lozenges, then
\partial_{x}H^{*}_{t}(z)+\bm{1}_{(-\infty,{\mathfrak{a}}(t)]}(z)+\bm{1}_{({\mathfrak{b}}(t),\infty]}(z)=1
and
f^{*}_{t}(z)=f^{*}_{r}(x)\in[-1,0]
.
In all cases we have

(B.10)

\displaystyle\arg^{*}f^{*}_{t}(z)=\arg^{*}f^{*}_{r}(x)=-\pi(\partial_{x}H^{*}_{t}(z)+\bm{1}_{(-\infty,{\mathfrak{a}}(t)]}(z)+\bm{1}_{({\mathfrak{b}}(t),\infty]}(z)).

This finishes the proof of (
B.7
).

The above discussion gives that for
x\in\mathscr{U}_{t}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
with
x<{\mathfrak{a}}(t)
,
f^{*}_{t}(x)\in(-\infty,-1)
and for
x\in\mathscr{U}_{t}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
with
x>{\mathfrak{b}}(t)
, we have
f^{*}_{t}(x)\in(0,-1)
.
Thanks to (
3.4
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f^{*}_{t}(x)/(f^{*}_{t}(x)+1)]|}{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}x|}=\frac{1}{r-t}\geqslant\frac{1}{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t}\gtrsim 1.

This gives the second statement of
Proposition

3.4
.

We denote the Stieltjes transform of
\partial_{x}H_{t}^{*}
as
m_{t}^{*}(z)=m_{t}(z;H_{t}^{*},t)
, and its modification
\tilde{m}^{*}_{t}(z)
, which is the Stieltjes transform of the measure
\partial_{x}H^{*}_{t}(x)+\bm{1}_{(-\infty,{\mathfrak{a}}(t)]}(x)+\bm{1}_{({\mathfrak{b}}(t),\infty]}(x)
. Then for any
x\in\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
, (
B.10
) implies

(B.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0+}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\tilde{m}^{*}_{t}(x\pm\mathrm{i}\eta)]=\mp\pi(\partial_{x}H^{*}_{t}(x)+\bm{1}_{(-\infty,{\mathfrak{a}}(t)]}(x)+\bm{1}_{({\mathfrak{b}}(t),\infty]}(x))=\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0+}\arg^{*}f^{*}_{t}(x).\end{split}

We take the ratio of
e^{\tilde{m}^{*}_{t}(z)}
and
f_{t}(z;\beta,s)
,

(B.12)

\displaystyle f^{*}_{t}(z)=e^{\tilde{m}^{*}_{t}(z)}\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(z)=e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)}g^{*}_{t}(z),

then
\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(z)
is well defined in the domain
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}
. It is analytic on
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}\setminus[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
, and (
B.11
) implies that it is continuous on
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
. Morera’s theorem implies that
\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(z)
extends to an analytic function on
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}
. From the construction,
\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(z)
is real analytic, i.e.
\overline{\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(z)}=\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(\bar{z})
, and is positive on
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
.
Moreover, in
{\mathscr{U}}_{t}
,
f^{*}_{t}(z)
and
e^{\tilde{m}^{*}_{t}(z)}
does not have zeros or poles.
Thus
\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(z)
does not have zeros or poles in
{\mathscr{U}}_{t}
. Using the defining relation (
3.22
), we conclude that
g^{*}_{t}(z)
is a meromorphic function in the domain
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}
.
It is positive on the interval
({\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t))
, has a simple pole at
{\mathfrak{a}}(t)
, has a simple zero at
{\mathfrak{b}}(t)
, and does not have other poles or zeros. This gives the third statement of
Proposition

3.4
.

For
\beta=H_{t}^{*}
, the fourth statement of
Proposition

3.4
is an empty statement.

∎

Appendix C
Proof of Propositions
3.4

In this Section we prove Proposition
3.4
.
To study general boundary profile
\beta
, we interpolate
\beta
with
H_{t}^{*}
. We collect facts about Shiffer kernels in Section
C.1
. In Section
C.2
, we construct a deformation
f(z;\theta)
parameterized by
0\leqslant\theta\leqslant 1
, starting from
f(z;0)=f_{t}^{*}(z)
and show if the solution of the deformation satisfies certain properties, then
f(z;1)=f(z;\beta,t)
as in Proposition
3.4
, and it satisfies all the properties in Proposition
3.4
. Finally in
Section
C.3
, we analyze these deformation formulas, and conclude the proof of Proposition
3.4
.

C.1.
Schiffer kernel

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a simply connected domain in the complex plane
{\mathbb{C}}
with boundary given by an analytic curve
\mathscr{C}
. We further assume
\mathscr{D}
is symmetric with respect to the real axis.
Let
\phi(z)
be the Riemann map from
\mathscr{D}\cap\mathbb{H}^{+}
to the upper half plane
\mathbb{H}^{+}
.
By the Schwarz reflection principle, it extends to
\mathscr{D}
, satisfying
\phi(\bar{z})=\overline{\phi(z)}
. The Green’s function of
\mathscr{D}
is given by

\displaystyle G(z,z^{\prime})=\log\big|\phi(z)-\phi(z^{\prime})\big|.

The Schiffer kernel
B(z,z^{\prime}){\rm d}z{\rm d}z^{\prime}
from
[
Eyn18
,
Sch46
]
on
\mathscr{D}
is given in terms of the Riemann map
\phi(z)
or the derivatives of the Green’s function by

(C.1)

\displaystyle B(z,z^{\prime})=\partial_{z}\partial_{z^{\prime}}G(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{\phi^{\prime}(z)\phi^{\prime}(z^{\prime})}{\big(\phi(z)-\phi(z^{\prime})\big)^{2}}.

It is a symmetric meromorphic bilinear differential (namely, a meromorphic
1
-form in
z
tensored by one in
z^{\prime}
, satisfying
B(z,z^{\prime})=B(z^{\prime},z)
); it has double poles on the diagonal,

(C.2)

\displaystyle B(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(z-z^{\prime})^{2}}+K(z,z^{\prime}),

where
K(z,z^{\prime})
is holomorphic.

Since the curve
\mathscr{C}
is real analytic, we show that
\phi(z)
and the Schiffer kernel
B(z,z^{\prime})
can be analytically extended to the contour
\mathscr{C}
. For
z\in\mathscr{C}\cap\mathbb{H}^{+}
, namely
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]>0
, this follows from that Riemann mapping function can be extended analytically to the boundary which contains an analytic arc. If
z
is one of points where
\mathscr{C}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
, then in a small neighborhood of
z
, we can parametrize
\mathscr{C}
as
\gamma(t)
for
t\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, with
\gamma(0)=z
and
\gamma(-t)=\overline{\gamma(t)}
.
\gamma(t)
is an analytic function on a small disk
\mathbb{D}
centered around
0
. Then we can consider
\phi(\gamma(z))
, and extend it to
\mathbb{D}
by Schwarz reflection principle; See Figure
19
. Then locally around
z
,

(C.3)

\displaystyle\phi(z+\Delta z)=\phi(z)+C(\Delta z)\Delta z^{2},

for some analytic function
C(\Delta z)
. Using the relation (
C.1
), we can also extend the Schiffer kernel to the contour
\mathscr{C}
, and
B(z,z^{\prime})=0
if
z
is one of points where
\mathscr{C}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
.

\mathbb{D}
\gamma(t)
E_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
\phi(z)

Figure 19.

We can extend
\phi(z)
analytically to a neighborhood of
z
where
\mathscr{C}
intersect
{\mathbb{R}}
, by the Schwarz reflection principle, such that
\phi(z+\Delta z)=\phi(z)+C(\Delta z)\Delta z^{2},
.

C.2.
Deformation Formulas

To analyze
f(z;\beta,t)
for boundary profile
\beta
close to
H_{t}^{*}
, we interpolate
\beta
with
H_{t}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\beta^{\theta}=(1-\theta)\beta^{0}+\theta\beta^{1},\quad 0\leqslant\theta\leqslant 1,\quad\beta^{0}=H_{t}^{*},\quad\beta^{1}=\beta.

We show that for boundary profile
\beta
sufficiently close to
H_{t}^{*}
,
f(z;\beta,t)
can be obtained by solving the following system of equations: Set
f(z;0)=f_{t}^{*}(z)
and
\mathscr{C}(x;0)=\mathscr{C}^{*}(x)
from (
B.1
). We construct their deformations as
f(z;\theta),\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)
satisfying the following differential equations:

(C.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{\theta}\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)=F(z;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f_{s}(z;\theta)|_{z=\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}\\
&F(z;\theta):=\frac{-({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1)^{2})}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))}.\end{split}

and

(C.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\log f(z;\theta)=-\int_{{\mathfrak{a}}(t)}^{{\mathfrak{b}}(t)}B(z,y;\theta)(\beta^{1}(y)-\beta^{0}(y)){\rm d}y,\quad z\in\mathscr{D}_{\theta}\setminus[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)].

where
\mathscr{C}_{\theta}=\{\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)\cup\overline{\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}:x\in I_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}^{*}\}
,
\mathscr{D}_{\theta}
is the region enclosed by
\mathscr{C}_{\theta}
, and
B(z,z^{\prime};\theta)
(recall from (
C.1
)) is the Schiffer kernel of the domain
\mathscr{D}_{\theta}
.
We remark that if
\mathscr{C}_{\theta}
is an analytic Jordan curve, and the righthand side of (
C.4
),
F(z;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f_{s}(z;\theta)
, is analytic in a neighborhood of
\mathscr{C}_{\theta}
, then the differential equations (
C.4
) and (
C.5
) are well defined.

To understand the righthand side of (
C.4
), we define a function

(C.6)

\displaystyle G(z,z^{\prime};\theta):=\frac{B(z,z^{\prime};\theta)}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))},\quad z,z^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{\theta}\setminus[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)].

Then using (
C.5
) and (
C.6
), for
z\in\mathscr{C}_{\theta}
, we have

(C.7)

\displaystyle F(z;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(z;\theta)=\frac{({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)f(z;\theta)}{(f(z;\theta)+1)^{2}}\int_{{\mathfrak{a}}(t)}^{{\mathfrak{b}}(t)}G(z,y;\theta)(\beta^{1}(y)-\beta^{0}(y)){\rm d}y.

Proposition C.1
.

If
\mathscr{C}_{\theta}
is an analytic Jordan curve, and the righthand side of (
C.4
),
F(z;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f_{s}(z;\theta)
, is analytic on a closed set
\mathscr{A}_{\theta}
containing an annulus neighborhood of
\mathscr{C}_{\theta}
, the derivative of the Schiffer kernel
B(z,z^{\prime};\theta)
with respect to
\theta
is given by

(C.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{\theta}B(z,z^{\prime};\theta)&=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\omega}B(z,w;\theta)F(w;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(w;\theta)B(w,z^{\prime};\theta){\rm d}w\\
&-\bm{1}(\text{$z$ outside $\omega$})\partial_{z}(F(z;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(z;\theta)B(z,z^{\prime};\theta))\\
&-\bm{1}(\text{$z^{\prime}$ outside $\omega$})\partial_{z^{\prime}}(F(z^{\prime};\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f_{s}(z^{\prime};\theta)B(z,z^{\prime};\theta)),\quad z,z^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{\theta},\end{split}

where the contour
\omega\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{\theta}
encloses
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
.
The derivative of
G(z,z^{\prime};\theta)
with respect to
\theta
is given by

(C.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\partial_{\theta}G(z,z^{\prime};\theta)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\omega}G(z,w;\theta)F(w;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f_{s}(w;\theta)B(w,z^{\prime};\theta){\rm d}w\\
&-\frac{({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)f(z;\theta)}{(f(z;\theta)+1)^{2}}\partial_{z}G(z,z^{\prime};\theta)\int_{{\mathfrak{a}}(t)}^{{\mathfrak{b}}(t)}G(z,y;\theta)(\beta^{1}(y)-\beta^{0}(y)){\rm d}y\\
&-\bm{1}(\text{$z^{\prime}$ outside $\omega$})\partial_{z^{\prime}}(F(z^{\prime};\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f_{s}(z^{\prime};\theta)G(z,z^{\prime};\theta))\\
\end{split}

where the contour
\omega\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{\theta}
encloses
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
but not
z
.

Proof.

We recall the defining relation (
C.1
) of the Schiffer kernel, given as the derivatives of the Green’s function. The variational formula of Green’s function has been studied in
[
Sch46
]
under more general conditions. By taking derivatives of the variational formula of Green’s function, see
[
Hua20a
, Theorem 3.7]
, we get for
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{\theta}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\partial_{\theta}B(z,z^{\prime};\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{x\in I^{*}_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}}B(z,\mathscr{C}(x;\theta);\theta)\partial_{\theta}\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)B(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta),z^{\prime};\theta){\rm d}\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)\\
&-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{x\in I^{*}_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}}B(z,\overline{\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)};\theta)\partial_{\theta}\overline{\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}B(\overline{\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)},z^{\prime};\theta){\rm d}\overline{\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}\\
&=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{w\in\mathscr{C}_{\theta}}B(z,w;\theta)F(w;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(w;\theta)B(w,z^{\prime};\theta){\rm d}w,\end{split}

where we used
(
C.4
) for the last line. By our assumption
F(w;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(w;\theta)
is analytic for
w\in\mathscr{A}_{\theta}
. We can get the case when
z
or
z^{\prime}
is outside the contour
\omega
, by deforming the contour
\mathscr{C}_{\theta}
. Since the Schiffer kernel
B(z,w;\theta)
has a double pole at
w=z
, see (
C.2
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\omega}B(z,w;\theta)F(w;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(w;\theta)B(w,z^{\prime};\theta){\rm d}w\\
&=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\omega-}B(z,w;\theta)F(w;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(w;\theta)B(w,z^{\prime};\theta){\rm d}w-\partial_{z}(F(z;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(z;\theta)B(z,z^{\prime};\theta)),\end{split}

where the contour
\omega
encloses
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
and
z,z^{\prime}
, and the contour
\omega-
encloses
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
and
z^{\prime}
but not
z
. If we further deform the contour
\omega-
to not enclose
z^{\prime}
, we will have one more term on the righthand side
-\partial_{z^{\prime}}(F(z^{\prime};\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(z^{\prime};\theta)B(z,z^{\prime};\theta))
. This gives (
C.8
).

The derivative of
G(z,z^{\prime};\theta)
as in (
C.6
) consists of two terms. If the derivative hits the numerator,

(C.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{\partial_{\theta}B(z,z^{\prime};\theta)}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))}=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\omega}G(z,w;\theta)F(w;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(w;\theta)B(w,z^{\prime};\theta){\rm d}w\\
&-\bm{1}(\text{$z^{\prime}$ outside $\omega$})\partial_{z^{\prime}}(F(z^{\prime};\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(z^{\prime};\theta)G(z,z^{\prime};\theta))-\frac{\partial_{z}(F(z;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(z;\theta)B(z,z^{\prime};\theta))}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))},\end{split}

where
z
is outside the contour
\omega
. We recall the expression of
F(z;\theta)
from (
C.4
), we can rewrite the last term on the righthand side of (
C.10
) as

(C.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\partial_{z}(({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{\theta}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))G(z,z^{\prime};\theta))}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))}\\
&=\frac{({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}\partial_{\theta}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))G(z,z^{\prime};\theta))}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))}+\frac{({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{\theta}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))\partial_{z}G(z,z^{\prime};\theta)}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))}.\end{split}

If the derivative with respect to
\theta
hits the denominator of
G(z,z^{\prime};\theta)
in (
C.6
) we get

(C.12)

\displaystyle-G(z,z^{\prime};\theta)\frac{({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{\theta}\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))}.

It follows from combining (
C.10
), (
C.11
) and (
C.12
), and noticing the cancellation between (
C.11
) and (
C.12
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\partial_{\theta}G(z,z^{\prime};\theta)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\omega}G(z,w;\theta)F(w;\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f(w;\theta)B(w,z^{\prime};\theta){\rm d}w\\
&-\frac{({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)f(z;\theta)}{(f(z;\theta)+1)^{2}}\partial_{z}G(z,z^{\prime};\theta)\int_{{\mathfrak{a}}(t)}^{{\mathfrak{b}}(t)}G(z,y;\theta)(\beta^{1}(y)-\beta^{0}(y)){\rm d}y\\
&-\bm{1}(\text{$z^{\prime}$ outside $\omega$})\partial_{z^{\prime}}(F(z^{\prime};\theta)\partial_{\theta}\log f_{s}(z^{\prime};\theta)G(z,z^{\prime};\theta))\\
\end{split}

where we used (
C.7
) for the last term in (
C.11
).

∎

Next we show that if the solution of (
C.4
), (
C.5
) exists up to
\theta=1
, the
f(z;1)=f(z;\beta,t)
as in Proposition
3.4
and Proposition
3.4
holds.

Proposition C.2
.

We assume the solution of (
C.4
), (
C.5
) exists up to
\theta=1
, and for

0\leqslant\theta\leqslant 1
,
\mathscr{C}_{\theta}
is analytic Jordan curve enclosing the interval
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
, and it holds
|\partial_{z}^{k}K(z,z^{\prime};\theta)|\lesssim 1
for any
0\leqslant k\leqslant 2
,
z\in\mathscr{D}_{\theta}
,
z^{\prime}
in a small neighborhood of
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
. Then
f(z;1)=f(z;\beta,t)
, and Proposition
3.4
holds.

We have the following lemma which gives general conditions for a function
f(z)
to be the limiting complex slope of lozenge tilings on a strip.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a simply connected domain in the complex plane
{\mathbb{C}}
with boundary given by an analytic curve
\mathscr{C}
. We further assume
\mathscr{D}
is symmetric with respect to the real axis. There exists a function
f(z)
the following holds

(1)

f(z)
is analytic on
\mathscr{D}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
,
f(\bar{z})=\overline{f(z)}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f(z)]<0
for
z\in\mathscr{D}\cap\mathbb{H}^{+}
.

(2)

Let
\chi(z)=f(z)/(f(z)+1)
.
f(z)
and
\chi(z)
extend analytically to the boundary curve
\mathscr{C}
. Moreover, there exists some
t>0
, such that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]+t\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(z)]=0
for
z\in\mathscr{C}
.

Then we have

f(z)
is the limiting complex slope of lozenge tiling on the strip
[0,t]
, with height function at time
0
given by
-\pi\partial_{x}H_{0}(x)=\arg^{*}f(x+\mathrm{i}0)
almost surely for
x\in\mathscr{D}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
, and at time
t
given by
-\pi\partial_{x}H_{t}(x)|_{x=z+t\chi(z)}=\arg^{*}f(z)
with
z\in\mathscr{C}\cap\mathbb{H}^{+}
.

Proof of
Proposition

C.2
.

We check
f(z;1)
satisfies the assumptions in Lemma
C.3
.
If the solution of (
C.4
), (
C.5
) exists up to
\theta=1
, then
f(z,\theta)
is analytic on
\mathscr{D}_{\theta}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
, extends analytically to the boundary curve
\mathscr{C}_{\theta}
, and
f(\overline{z},\theta)=\overline{f(z,\theta)}
.

To check the second assumption in Lemma
C.3
, we compute

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\partial_{\theta}\left(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\frac{f(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta);\theta)}{f(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta);\theta)+1}\right)\\
&=\partial_{\theta}\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)\left(1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\left.\partial_{z}\frac{f(z;\theta)}{f(z;\theta)+1}\right|_{z=\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}\right)+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\left.\partial_{\theta}\frac{f(z;\theta)}{f(z;\theta)+1}\right|_{z=\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}=0,\end{split}

where we used (
C.4
) and (
C.5
).
Thus we have

(C.13)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\frac{f(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta);\theta)}{f(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta);\theta)+1}=\mathscr{C}(x;0)+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\frac{f(\mathscr{C}(x;0);0)}{f(\mathscr{C}(x;0);0)+1}=x.

where in the last inequality, we used the definition of
\mathscr{C}(x;0)=\mathscr{C}^{*}(x)
from (
B.1
). By taking
\theta=1
,
f(z;\theta)
satisfies the second assumption in Lemma (
C.3
).

Next we show that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\log f(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta);\theta)]
does not change.

(C.14)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\partial_{\theta}\log f(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta);\theta)=\partial_{\theta}\log f(z;\theta)|_{z=\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}+\partial_{z}\log f(z;\theta)|_{z=\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}\partial_{\theta}\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)\\
&=\frac{\partial_{\theta}\log f(z;\theta)|_{z=\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))|_{z=\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}}=\frac{-\int B(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta),y;\theta)(\beta^{1}(y)-\beta^{0}(y)){\rm d}y}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))|_{z=\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}}.\end{split}

By taking derivative with respect to
x
on both sides of (
C.13
) and rearranging we have

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)=\frac{1}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))|_{z=\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}}.

We recall the definition of Schifer kernel from (
C.1
),

(C.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{B(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta),y;\theta)}{1+({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}-t)\partial_{z}(f(z;\theta)/(f(z;\theta)+1))|_{z=\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)}}&=\frac{\phi^{\prime}(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta))\phi^{\prime}(y)}{(\phi(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta))-\phi(y;\theta))^{2}}\partial_{x}\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)\\
&=\partial_{x}\frac{\phi^{\prime}(y;\theta)}{(\phi(y;\theta)-\phi(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta);\theta))}\in{\mathbb{R}},\end{split}

where
\phi(z;\theta)
is the Riemann mapping from
\mathscr{D}_{\theta}\cap\mathbb{H}^{+}
to
\mathbb{H}^{+}
, and we used that it maps the boundary curve to real, namely
\phi(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta);\theta),\phi(y;\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
We conclude by plugging (
C.15
) into (
C.14
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\log f(\mathscr{C}(x;\theta);\theta)]=0,

and

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\log f(\mathscr{C}(x;1);1)]=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\log f(\mathscr{C}(x;0);0)]=-\pi H^{*}_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}(x)\in[-\pi,0].

Next we show that

\lim_{z\in\mathbb{H}^{+}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{1}\cap{\mathbb{R}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\log f(z;1)]\in[-\pi,0].

Then it follows that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f(z;1)]\leqslant 0
on the boundary of
\mathscr{D}_{1}\cap\mathbb{H}^{+}
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f(z;1)]<0
for
z\in\mathscr{D}_{1}\cap\mathbb{H}^{+}
by the strong maximum principle of
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f(z;1)]
. Thus
f(z;1)
also satisfies the first assumption in Lemma
C.3
.

We recall the decomposition of the Schiffer kernel from (
C.2
),

(C.16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{\theta}\log f(z;\theta)&=-\int B(z,y;\theta)(\beta^{1}(y)-\beta^{0}(y)){\rm d}y\\
&=-\int\left(\frac{1}{(z-y)^{2}}+K(z,y;\theta)\right)(\beta^{1}(y)-\beta^{0}(y)){\rm d}y\\
&=-\int\left(K(z,y;\theta)\right)(\beta^{1}(y)-\beta^{0}(y)){\rm d}y+\int\frac{\partial_{y}\beta^{1}(y)-\partial_{y}\beta^{0}(y)}{z-y}.\end{split}

We recall the decomposition of
f_{t}^{*}(z)=f(z;0)
from (
B.12
). By integrating (
C.16
) from
0
to
1
, we get

(C.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\log f(z;1)&=m(z;\beta,t)+\log g^{*}_{t}(z)-\int_{0}^{1}\int\left(K(z,y;\theta)\right)(\beta^{1}(y)-\beta^{0}(y)){\rm d}y{\rm d}\theta\\
&=\tilde{m}(z;\beta,t)+\log\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(z)-\int_{0}^{1}\int\left(K(z,y;\theta)\right)(\beta^{1}(y)-\beta^{0}(y)){\rm d}y{\rm d}\theta,\end{split}

where
m(z;\beta,t),\tilde{m}(z;\beta,t)
are Stieltjes transform of
\partial_{y}\beta^{1}=\partial_{y}\beta
, and
\partial_{y}\beta+\bm{1}_{(-\infty,{\mathfrak{a}}(t)]}(y)+\bm{1}_{({\mathfrak{b}}(t),\infty]}(y))
respectively. The last two terms on the righthand side of (
C.17
) are real analytic, and the density
\partial_{y}\beta+\bm{1}_{(-\infty,{\mathfrak{a}}(t)]}(y)+\bm{1}_{({\mathfrak{b}}(t),\infty]}(y))
is bounded by
1
,
we conclude that

\lim_{z\in\mathbb{H}^{+}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{1}\cap{\mathbb{R}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\log f(z;1)]\in[-\pi,0].

So far we have checked that
f(z;1)
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
C.3
, it is the limiting complex slope of lozenge tiling on the strip
[t,{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}]
(by shifting time by
t
), with the height function at time
t
given by
\beta
, and at time
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
given by
H_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}
. In particular
f(z;1)=f(z;\beta,t)
as in Proposition
3.4
. The first statement in Proposition
3.4
follows by taking
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}_{t}^{\beta}=\mathscr{D}_{1}
and
\mathscr{U}_{t}^{\beta}=\mathscr{D}_{1}\setminus\{x:(x,t)\in\overline{{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}}^{t};\beta)}\}
. The second statement in Proposition
3.4
follows from the decomposition (
C.16
), and the maximum principle of
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f(z;1)/(f(z;1)+1)]/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]
. For the third statement in Proposition
3.4
, we take

(C.18)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\log\tilde{g}(z;\beta,t)=\log\tilde{g}^{*}_{t}(z)-\int_{0}^{1}\int\left(K(z,y;\theta)\right)(\beta(y)-H_{t}^{*}(y)){\rm d}y{\rm d}\theta\\
&\log g(z;\beta,t)=\log g^{*}_{t}(z)-\int_{0}^{1}\int\left(K(z,y;\theta)\right)(\beta(y)-H_{t}^{*}(y)){\rm d}y{\rm d}\theta,\end{split}

as in (
C.17
).

Finally for the fourth statement in Proposition
3.4
, we can rewrite (
C.18
) as a contour integral

(C.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\log g(z;\beta,t)-\log g(z;H_{t}^{*},t)=-\int_{0}^{1}\left(\int K(z,y;\theta)(\beta(y)-H_{t}^{*}(y)){\rm d}y\right){\rm d}\theta\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\int\int_{z^{\prime}={\mathfrak{a}}(t)}^{z^{\prime}=y}K(z,z^{\prime};\theta){\rm d}y(\partial_{y}\beta(y)-\partial_{y}H_{t}^{*}(y)){\rm d}y\right){\rm d}\theta\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\int_{z^{\prime}={\mathfrak{a}}(t)}^{z^{\prime}=w}K(z,z^{\prime};\theta){\rm d}y(m(w;\beta,t)-m_{t}^{*}(w)){\rm d}w\right){\rm d}\theta,\end{split}

where
\omega
is a contour enclosing
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
.
The claim (
3.23
) follows from taking derivative with respect to
z
, then taking absolute value on both sides of (
C.19
), and using that
|\partial_{z}^{k}K(z,z^{\prime};\theta)|\lesssim 1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
C.3
.

One can check that
\chi(z)
is analytic on
\mathscr{D}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
,
\chi(\bar{z})=\overline{\chi(z)}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(z)]<0
for
z\in\mathscr{D}\cap\mathbb{H}^{+}
. By Nevanlinna representation,
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(x+\mathrm{i}0)]
defines a negative measure
\mu
on
I\subset\mathscr{D}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
,

(C.20)

\displaystyle\chi(z)=\int\frac{{\rm d}\mu(x)}{z-x}+\xi(z),

where
\xi(z)
is real analytic. In particular,
\chi(z)
extends analytically to
\mathscr{D}\setminus I
.

For any
z\in\mathscr{D}\setminus I
, we define

(C.21)

\displaystyle s(z)=-\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(z)]},\quad x(z)=z+s(z)\chi(z)\in{\mathbb{R}},\quad f_{s(z)}(x(z))=f(z),

we remark that for
z\in(\mathscr{D}\cap{\mathbb{R}})\setminus I
, we should interpret the first statement in (
C.21
) as
s(z)=-1/\chi^{\prime}(z)
.
Next we show that the map (
C.21
) restricted to
\mathbb{H}^{+}\cup{\mathbb{R}}
,
z\mapsto(x(z),s(z))
is an injection, thus it gives the desired limiting complex slope of lozenge tiling on the strip
[0,t]
.

We first notice that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(z)]/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]
satisfies a strong maximum principle inside
\mathscr{D}\setminus I
. Let
z=x+\mathrm{i}y
and
v(x,y)=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(z)]
, then
v(x,y)
is harmonic and for
|y|\neq 0
,
v(x,y)/y
satisfies the elliptic equation

\displaystyle\Delta\frac{v(x,y)}{y}=\frac{\Delta v(x,y)}{y}-2\left(\frac{\partial_{y}v(x,y)}{y^{2}}-\frac{v(x,y)}{y^{3}}\right)=\frac{2}{y}\partial_{y}\frac{v(x,y)}{y}.

Thus the strong maximum principle holds for
|y|\neq 0
. Next we show that
z=x\in(\mathscr{D}\setminus I)\cap{\mathbb{R}}
is not a local extrema. We can directly Taylor expand
\chi(z)
around
x
,
\chi(x+\Delta x+\Delta y\mathrm{i})=a_{0}+a_{1}(\Delta x+\Delta y\mathrm{i})+a_{2}(\Delta x+\Delta y\mathrm{i})^{2}+\cdots
with
a_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Say
a_{k}
is the first nonvanishing coefficient with
k\geqslant 2
, then taking
\Delta x=\lambda\Delta y

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(x+\Delta x+\Delta y\mathrm{i})]}{\Delta y}&=a_{1}+\frac{a_{k}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[(\lambda\Delta y+\Delta y\mathrm{i})^{k}]}{\Delta y}+\OO(|\Delta y|^{k})\\
&=a_{1}+\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[(\lambda+\mathrm{i})^{k}]a_{k}\Delta y^{k-1}+\OO(|\Delta y|^{k}).\end{split}

For
k\geqslant 2
, there are choices of
\lambda
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[(\lambda+\mathrm{i})^{k}]
is positive or negative. We conclude that
z=x\in(\mathscr{D}\cap{\mathbb{R}})\setminus I
is not a local extrema.

By our assumption for
z\in\mathscr{C}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(z)]/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]=-1/t
and for
z\rightarrow I
(the support of
\mu
), the decomposition (
C.20
) gives that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(z)]/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\rightarrow-\infty
. Thus by maximum principle we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(z)]/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\in(-\infty,-1/t)
for
z\in\mathscr{D}\setminus I
. In particular
s(z)\in(0,t)
as in (
C.21
) for any
z\in\mathscr{D}\setminus I
.

For any
0<s<t
, let
\mathscr{C}_{s}:=\{z\in\mathscr{D}\setminus I:s=s(z)\}
be the boundary curve of the domains

(C.22)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{s}^{-}:=\{z\in\mathscr{D}\setminus I:-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(z)]<s\},\quad\mathscr{D}_{s}^{+}:=\{z\in\mathscr{D}\setminus I:-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(z)]>s\}.

Thanks to the maximum principle,
\mathscr{D}_{s}^{+}
is connected, in fact each point in
\mathscr{D}_{s}^{+}
is connected to the boundary curve
\mathscr{C}
.

\mathscr{D}_{s}^{-}
may consist of many disconnected components, but each component of
\mathscr{D}_{s}^{-}
contains part of
I
.

The set
\mathscr{C}_{s}
is characterized by
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z+s\chi(z)]=0
. The critical points are given by
1+s\partial_{z}\chi(z)=0
, which consists of isolated points. So
\mathscr{C}_{s}
consists of piecewise analytic curves. Next we show the critical points are on the real axis. Otherwise, assume we have a critical point at
z_{c}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{c}]>0
. By symmetric,
\bar{z}_{c}
is also a critical point, such
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z_{c}+s\chi(z_{c})]=0
and
1+s\partial_{z}\chi(z_{c})=0
. Then locally around
z_{c}
, we have
z+s\chi(z)=z_{c}+s\chi(z_{c})+a_{2}(z-z_{c})^{2}+a_{3}(z-z_{c})^{3}+\cdots
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z+s\chi(z)]=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[a_{2}(z-z_{c})^{2}+a_{2}(z-z_{c})^{2}+\cdots]
. We take a small disk
{\mathbb{D}}
centered around
z_{c}
, the set
\mathscr{C}_{s}
divides it into at least four pieces, on each piece, the sign of
s+\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi(z)]
alternates. The same statement holds locally around
\bar{z}_{c}
. See Figure
20
. We recall
\mathscr{D}_{s}^{-},\mathscr{D}_{s}^{+}
from (
C.22
). Since each connected component of
\mathscr{D}_{s}^{-}
contains part of
I
, the two regions marked by “
*
” in Figure
20
belong to the same connected component of
\mathscr{D}_{s}^{-}
, so do the two regions marked by “
-
”. They divide
\mathscr{D}_{s}^{+}
into at least two disconnected components, which leads to a contradiction.

-

z_{c}
*
-
\bar{z}_{c}

\mathscr{D}

Figure 20.
The first plot illustrates that the critical points
z_{c}
must be on the real axis. Shown to the second and third plots,
\mathscr{C}_{s}:=\{z\in\mathscr{D}\setminus I:s=s(z)\}
consists of contours,
\mathscr{C}_{s}^{1},\mathscr{C}_{s}^{2},\cdots,\mathscr{C}_{s}^{r}
, each surrounds a connected component of
\mathscr{D}_{s}^{-}
.
\mathscr{C}_{s}^{k}\cap\mathbb{H}^{+}
are mapped bijectively to disjoint intervals, corresponding the slice of liquid region at time
s
.

We conclude that all the critical points are on the real axis, and
\mathscr{C}_{s}:=\{z\in\mathscr{D}\setminus I:s=s(z)\}
consists of contours, each surrounds a connected component of
\mathscr{D}_{s}^{-}
. These contours can only touch each other on the real axis. We order them from left to right as
\mathscr{C}_{s}^{1},\mathscr{C}_{s}^{2},\cdots,\mathscr{C}_{s}^{r}
.
For any
z\in\mathscr{C}_{s}^{k}\cap\mathbb{H}^{+}
, we parametrize
\mathscr{C}_{s}^{k}
around
z
as
\gamma:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\mapsto\mathscr{C}_{s}^{k}
with
\gamma(0)=z
in clockwise direction. Then the direction of
\gamma^{\prime}(0)
is given by
1/(1+s\partial_{z}\chi(z))
, and
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=c/(1+s\partial_{z}\chi(z))
with some
c>0
. We recall
x(z)=z+s\chi(z)
from (
C.21
),
\partial_{\tau}x(\gamma(\tau))|_{\tau=0}=c>0
. We conclude that
x(z)
maps each arc
\mathscr{C}_{s}^{k}\cap(\mathbb{H}^{+}\cup{\mathbb{R}})
bijectively to an interval
[x(w_{k}(s)),x(w_{k}^{\prime}(s))]=[x_{k}(s),x^{\prime}_{k}(s)]
, where
w_{k}(s),w_{k}^{\prime}(s)
are the left and right end point of the arc
\mathscr{C}_{s}^{k}\cap(\mathbb{H}^{+}\cup{\mathbb{R}})
. Next we show that
x^{\prime}_{k}(s)\leqslant x_{k+1}(s)
.
If
w_{k}^{\prime}(s)=w_{k+1}(s)
, then
x^{\prime}_{k}(s)=x_{k+1}(s)
. Otherwise, for any
z\in(w_{k}^{\prime}(s),w_{k+1}(s))\in\mathscr{D}_{s}^{+}
, the maximum principle gives that
\chi^{\prime}(z)>-1/s
. It follows that the map
z\mapsto z+s\chi(z)
is increasing, since
(z+s\chi(z))^{\prime}=1+s\chi^{\prime}(z)>0
. We conclude that the map
z\mapsto z+s\chi(z)
maps the interval
[w_{k}^{\prime}(s),w_{k+1}(s)]
bijectively to the interval
[x^{\prime}_{k}(s),x_{k+1}(s)]
. In particular
x_{k+1}(s)>x^{\prime}_{k}(s)
.

It follows the map
z\mapsto(s(z),x(z))
in (
C.21
) restricted to
(\mathbb{H}^{+}\cup{\mathbb{R}})
is an injection, which gives a liquid region
{\mathfrak{L}}
, and the slice at time
s
is given by the intervals
[x_{1}(s),x_{1}^{\prime}(s)]\cup\cdots[x_{r}(s),x_{r}^{\prime}(s)]
. See Figure
20
. (
C.21
) also gives a complex slope
f_{s}(x)
for any
(x,s)\in\overline{{\mathfrak{L}}}
, characterized by

(C.23)

\displaystyle f_{s}(x)=f\left(x-s\frac{f_{s}(x)}{f_{s}(x)+1}\right),

which is essentially (
2.15
). Moreover, the complex slope
f_{s}(x)
extends outside the liquid region: for any
x\in(x^{\prime}_{k}(s),x_{k+1}(s))
, there exists some
z\in(w_{k}^{\prime}(s),w_{k+1}(s))
such that
x=z+s\chi(z)
. We can simply take
f_{s}(x)=f(z)
.

The height function
H_{s}(x)
is determined by
-\pi\partial_{x}H_{s}(x)=\arg^{*}(f_{s}(x))
and
\pi\partial_{s}H_{s}(x)=\arg^{*}(f_{s}(x)+1)
for
(x,s)\in{\mathfrak{L}}
. It also extends continuously outside the liquid region
{\mathfrak{L}}
.
It follows from
[
ADPZ20
, Theorem 8.3 and Remark 8.6]
, that
H_{s}(x)
is the limiting height function of lozenge tiling on the strip
[0,t]
with complex slope given by
f_{s}(x)
. Moreover, at time
0
the height function is given by
\partial_{x}H_{0}(x)=-\pi\arg^{*}f(x+\mathrm{i}0)
almost surely for
x\in\mathscr{D}\cap{\mathbb{R}}
, and at time
t
given by
\partial_{x}H_{t}(x)|_{x=z+t\chi(z)}=-\pi\arg^{*}f(z)
with
z\in\mathscr{C}\cap\mathbb{H}^{+}
.

∎

C.3.
Proof of
Proposition

3.4

The system of differential equations for
\mathscr{C}(x;\theta)
and
\log f(z;\theta)
in (
C.4
) and (
C.5
) can be analyzed using the “method of majorant” originated from Cauchy. In the following we recall some notations from
[
Lax53
, Section 2]
. A formal power series in
k
veriables:
A=\sum_{i_{r}\geqslant 0}a_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{k}}y_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots y_{k}^{i_{k}}
is said to majorize another one

B=\sum_{0\leqslant i_{r}<\infty}b_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{k}}(x_{1}-\tilde{x}_{1})^{i_{1}}\cdots(x_{k}-\tilde{x}_{k})^{i_{k}}
at
(\tilde{x}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{x}_{k})
denoted by
B\subset_{(\tilde{x}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{x}_{k})}A
if
|b_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{k}}|\leqslant a_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{k}}
for all
0\leqslant i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{k}
. The following rules will be used repeatedly without mentioning:
(i) If
B_{i}\subset_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}A_{i}
, then
\sum B_{i}\subset_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}\sum A_{i}
, and
\prod B_{i}\subset_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}\prod A_{i}
;
(ii) Let
x_{i}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{\ell})
and
y_{i}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{\ell})
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
, be power series with
x_{i}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{\ell})\subset_{\tilde{\bm{s}}}y_{i}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{\ell})
, and
(x_{1}(\tilde{\bm{s}}),\cdots x_{k}(\tilde{\bm{s}}))=\tilde{\bm{x}}
. Let
A
and
B
be power series in
k
variables satisfying
B\subset_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}A
. Then it holds that

B(x_{1}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{\ell}),\cdots,x_{k}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{\ell}))\subset_{\tilde{s}}A(y_{1}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{\ell}),\cdots,y_{k}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{\ell}))
.

The following power series play important roles in
[
Lax53
, Section 2]
,

(C.24)

\displaystyle f^{0}(u)=\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\frac{u^{j}}{(j+1)^{2}},\quad f^{1}(u)=1+\sum_{j\geqslant 1}\frac{u^{j}}{j^{3}},\quad f^{0}(u)=\partial_{u}f^{1}(u).

Any convergent power series is majorized by
Mf^{0}(ru)
and
Mf^{1}(ru)
, if
M
and
r
are chosen sufficiently large. The power series
f^{0}(u),f^{1}(u)
satisfy

(C.25)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}f^{0}(u)f^{0}(u)\subset_{0}4\partial_{u}f^{0}(u),\quad f^{0}(u)f^{0}(u)\subset_{0}4f^{0}(u),\quad f^{1}(u)f^{1}(u)\subset_{0}4f^{1}(u).

More importantly, composition also preserves them,

(C.26)

\displaystyle f^{0}(auf^{1}(bu))\subset_{0}f^{0}(bu),\quad f^{0}(auf^{0}(bu))\subset_{0}f^{0}(bu),

provided that
4a\leqslant b
. In the rest of this section, we will use
u,u^{\prime},v,v^{\prime}
for formal variables.

Fix a small constant
{\mathfrak{r}}>0
. We recall that
\mathscr{C}_{0}=\{\mathscr{C}^{*}(x)\cup\overline{\mathscr{C}^{*}(x)}:x\in I_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}^{*}\}
from (
B.1
) and its annulus neighborhood of
\mathscr{A}_{0}
from Proposition
B.1
. We denote
\mathscr{C}^{0}=\mathscr{C}_{0}
and take more contours
\mathscr{C}^{1},\mathscr{C}^{2},\mathscr{C}^{3}\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{0}
, such that
\mathscr{C}^{i}
encloses
\mathscr{C}_{i+1}
. They are distance at least
{\mathfrak{r}}
bounded away from each other, and the interval
[{\mathfrak{a}}(t),{\mathfrak{b}}(t)]
. See Figure
21
.

y=s
{\mathfrak{a}}(t)
{\mathfrak{b}}(t)
\mathscr{C}^{0-}

\mathscr{C}^{1}

Figure 21.
We take contours

, from Proposition

. The same as in (

), we can use
f^{0}(u)
to majorize
